1221. ANN HARRINGTON was indicted for stealing 1 jacket, value 10 s., the goods of Henry Richards.
HENRY RICHARDS . At half-past ten o'clock on the 19th of March, I went into the prisoner's house to see a shipmate—I went into an upper room, and bad a glass of gin from the prisoner—I laid down for half-an-hour—when I awoke I missed my jacket—I went down and charged her with stealing it—she said, "Come out, and have a glass of gin or rum out of the money"—I went with her about 100 yards off, and saw an officer, and gave her in charge—she and another female struck me.
THOMAS BARRETT . I produce this jacket, which was pawned by the prisoner on the 19th of March.
WILLIAM PARAMORE ( police-constable K 51.) I heard a disturbance—the prosecutor had the prisoner by her arm—he said, "Take this woman, she has got my jacket"—she said, "He has got the ticket," and I found the ticket in his hat—she struck him on the cheek, and said, "Take that."
Prisoner's Defence. He gave me the jacket to pledge for 8 s.; he soon spent the money in drink; the next morning he came and brought the same jacket which he had redeemed the night before, and asked me to pawn it, which I did, for 10 s.; he then began drinking rum at half-a-pint at a time.
HENRY RICHARDSON re-examined. She put the ticket into my hand just after I came out—I laid in the bed with one of the women the night before,—I did not give her the jacket to pawn, but she pawned it—I took it out the night before, and said nothing about it—I had given her 4 s.
GUILTY . Aged 21.— Confined Nine Months

GEORGE JOHN RESTIEAUX ( police-constable E 49.) I produce the calico. On the 26th of March I saw the prisoner with another girl, in Oxford-street—the prisoner had this calico wrapped up in her apron—I asked where she got it—she said, "What is that to you?"—I said, "You know me?"—she said, "Yes"—she said it was a piece of print she had bought of a woman—before the Magistrate she said she bought it of a woman in Oxford-street.

1224. THOMAS PARKER was indicted for stealing 2 shillings, the monies of John Costello.
JOHN COSTELLO , labourer, Robert-street, Mill-wall, Poplar. I was in bed on the morning of the 18th of March, and heard money jingle on the floor—I awoke, and found the prisoner by the side of my bed, with my jacket in his hand, and the money falling out—2 s. 6 d. fell out on the floor—he gave me 6 d.—he said, "This is my money"—I said, "It is not"—I got up, and the two shillings were gone—more men slept in the room, but they were gone—I had no time to send for an officer then, but gave him in charge at dinner time the same day.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. He was your bed-fellow, was he not? A. Yes; his clothes were on the top of the bed, and mine at the side—I had 4 s. 6 d. in my pocket at first, but after I had 2 s. 6 d. in silver, and some halfpence in my coat pocket—he did not shake my waistcoat pocket and hold it up to me—he asked what money I had in my pocket—I said 4 s. 6 d., and then some money fell, and he said 2 s. of it was his.
CHARLES BUSHNELL ( police-constable K 263.) The prosecutor came and
told me the prisoner had stolen 2 s.—I went and took him, and found two half-crowns and 2 s. 1 d. on him.
GUILTY . Aged 50.— Confined Three Month.

1225. EMMA DRIVER was indicted for stealing 1 pair of stockings, value 6 1/2 d., the goods of Mary Ann Howse; and that she had been before convicted of felony.

On the 16th of March the prisoner and another girl came for tome black flowers, and as they were going out I saw the prisoner take a pair of stock-ings off some cambric at the door—I overtook her—I said, "Where are the stockings?" she said she had none, but as I took her back they dropped from her at the door.
Prisoner. The other girl took them, not me. Witness. I am sure the prisoner took them.
RICHARD THORPE ( police-constable R 155.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—the prisoner is the person.

[trialAccount t18440408-1226]
1226. MATILDA GREEN and ANN CHAPMAN were indicted for stealing 1 shawl, value 20 s., the goods of Lawrence Dillon.
FREDERICK LILLIY . I am in the service of William King and Spencer Lee, of Mary-street, Hampstead-road. This shawl was pledged for 8 s. by Green, between four and fire o'clock, on the 26th of March—there was a female with her, who I could not swear to.
LAWRENCE DILLON . I live in Red-lion-court, Saffron-hill. About ten o'clock, on the 26th of March, I and my wife went out, leaving the prisoners and a little boy there—my box was locked, and this shawl in it—I came home at half-past five o'clock in the evening—the boy was crying on the stairs—the shawl was gone, and the prisoners too—I looked after the prisoners, and met them just against the Regent's-barracks, talking to a young man—Chapman gave me the ticket of the shawl, and told me she was very sorry, she had nothing to do with it, further than Green giving her the ticket.
LAWRENCE DILLON , junior. I was left in the room with the prisoners—Green chucked me down stairs—I was sitting on the stairs—I heard the box break, and heard a hammering—I cried out and said, "The box is broke"—the prisoners ran down—I went into the room, and the box was broken.
Green's Defence. I did not intend to steal it, but I wanted a little money to purchase some food; Chapman knew nothing of it; the prosecutrix said she would not hurt us if we would give up the ticket; I told Chapman to give it to her.
LAWRENCE DILLON re-examined. I did not say so—I believe my wife did.
GREEN — GUILTY . Aged 18.
CHAPMAN — GUILTY . Aged 17.
Confined Two Months.
[trialAccount t18440408-1227]
1227. MARY ANN SMITH and ANN JONES were indicted for stealing 9 veils, value 15 s., the goods of Digory Northy and another.
EDWARD THOMAS . I am in partnership with Digory Northy, a laceman, in Whitechapel. About half-past five o'clock, on the 13th of March, the prisoners came in—Smith asked for a shawl—I showed her one—it did not suit her—some other customers came in—I said if it did not suit she must leave it—I threw it on a box—Smith went to the veils—I saw the drawer was disturbed—they were going out—I went to the step and took Jones—I found on her nine veils—I secured her, and ran after Smith, who had ran about
a hundred yards—I took her—the shawl they looked at was worth 7 s. 6 d.—there was only 1 s. and a few halfpence found on them.
Smith's Defence. I was going to pay a deposit.
SMITH — GUILTY . Aged 17. JONES — GUILTY . Aged 14.

1229. GEORGE BARRATT was indicted for stealing 1 dead fowl, value 2 s. 6 d., the property of William Roberts.
LYDIA ALFXANDRINA ROBERTS . I am the wife of William Roberts. I had a dead fowl—I saw it safe about five minutes before the policeman arrived.

1230. WILLIAM COOK and CHARLES LOTTY were indicted for stealing on the 19th of March, 23 telescopes, value 2 l. 17 s. 6 d., the goods of John Cetta: and THOMAS ROBERT MOORE for feloniously receiving 11 telescopes, part of the same, well knowing them to have been stolen.
JOHN CETTA , telescope-maker, Brook-street, Holborn. I missed these telescopes on the 20th of March—they might have been gone a day or two—these are them.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. What is their value? A. About half-a-crown a piece—I had every reason to suspect one of my servants at first, and that servant told me on the 20th of March, that Cook and Lotty did it, that he had seen them outside the shop.
GEORGE EATON . I am the prosecutor's shop-boy—on the morning of the 19th of March, I had seen Cook and Lotty outside the window for about one minute—I am sure they are the persons—there were three parcels of telescopes there then—they looked all full—I missed them the next morning—there was a pane of glass broken that they were near to.
THOMAS ARCHER ( police-constable G 8.) I apprehended Lotty on the 22nd—I charged him with stealing telescopes from the prosecutor's window—he said he did not steal them, that he saw Cook and another boy standing against the window, he saw Cook put his hand in, take them out, and give them to another boy, they asked him to go to a chandler's shop on Clerkenwell-green,
and they sold them to a shopkeeper—I then took Cook—he said Eaton gave them to him, and told him his master had given them to him for good conduct—I then took him to the station—he said that Moore was the man he had sold them to—I went to Moore's, and asked him if he had bought any telescopes within the last two or three days—he said, "No"—I said, "I am certain you have, and I have a witness outside that can prove it—he said, opening a chest," "Here are four"—I said, "You have more than that"—he said, "I do not know how many I bought, if you will allow me to look in my book, I will tell you"—he said he bought eleven—he then said he bought one for 10 d.; then six for 3 s. 7 1/2 d.; and four for 1 s. 10 d.—I know Mr. Greenwood's handwriting, this is it ( deposition read.)—"The prisoner Cook says, they were brought to me by Lotty, he said he wanted to sell them; on Clerken well-green a boy met Lotty, and told him where he could sell them; we went to Moore's; I sold them to Moore.—Lotty says, it is false; I saw Cook take them out; I did not know what was in them; he asked me to go with him to Turn mill-street.—Moore says, I did not know the value of them, or that they were stolen."
Cross-examined. Q. Does not Moore keep a marine-store shop? A. No, he is a general dealer, he sells cheese, butter, and sweet things for children.
GEORGE EATON re-examined. I did not give any telescopes to Cook—I spoke to him that morning.
(The prisoners received good characters.)
COOK — GUILTY . Aged 13.— Confined Six Months.
LOTTY — NOT GUILTY .
MOORE — GUILTY . Aged 23.— Confined One Year.

1231. JOHN PHILLIPS was indicted for stealing 1 flannel shirt, value 3 s., the goods of Edward Richard Blaker, in a vestel in a port of entry and discharge.
EDWARD RICHARD BLAKER . I am second mate of the steam ship Majestic, in the West India Docks. D." on it, and it is my wife's marking.
CHARLES HASELTINE . I took the prisoner, and told him to deliver up the shirt—he said, "I shall not"—the prosecutor said it was marked "G. D. No. l"—the prisoner said he bought it in Rosemary-lane of a Jew on the Sunday morning—he did not want me to go to the man he bought it of.
Prisoner's Defence. I bought it on Saturday night for 9 s.

[trialAccount t18440408-1238]
1238. JOHN WARD was indicted for embezzlement.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM OLIVE THOMAS . I am store-clerk at Messrs. John Letton's, Elliott, and others' brewery, at Pimlico. and was so in Aug., 1642—our regular men have certain wages, and when they are absent we employ the extra men—an allowance of wages is paid to the extra men, and the rest goes as a gratuity to the invalid—the prisoner was an extra man—I had employed him about fifteen months—I prepared a puncheon of porter and three barrels of ale, to be sent to Mr. Gillingham, of Norwood—while I had employed the prisoner I had sent him with ale and porter to our customers, and he received the money, and accounted to me—I made out the invoice, and banded it to Major, our salesman—this is it, and this is the receipt I made out, as usual—this other paper is a return note, to be signed by the customer, that the goods have been received—the prisoner should account to me for the money, or, in my absence, to Major—on the 6th of August be did not account to me for 9 l. 14 s. 2 d., received from Mr. Gillingham—I did not see him any more; from that day, till I saw him in the London Docks, on the 15th of March this year—he has never accounted to me for this money.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Does not the regular man pay the extra man? A. Not in all cases—I engaged the prisoner shortly after he came—he worked a day or two without my permission, and then I engaged him—I did not make an agreement with him for this particular job—I believe he had constant work at that time, and, to the best of my belief, for a month previous—there is a man named Geary in our employ—it was not Geary's place to employ the extra men.
MR. DOANE. Q. If any number of regular men are absent, you take on the same number of extra, men? A. Yes—it is not the duty of an extra man to take the work of any particular man.
RICHARD THOMAS MAJOR . I am cellarman, in the employ of the prosecutors. On the 6th of Aug., 1842, the prisoner was employed to carry out porter and ale to Mr. Gillingham's—there were two other draymen going out at the same time, to other places—Mr. Thomas produced an invoice—I saw it on the desk—I delivered it to Geary, who was foreman of the three men who were to carry out beer—the prisoner did not account to me for, 9 l. 14 s. 2 d., from Mr. Gillingham—he has never accounted to me for that—I never saw him again till he was taken.
Cross-examined. Q. Were the other two extra or regular men? A. One was a regular man and the other an extra man—Geary drove a dray.
ROBERT GEARY . I am the prosecutors' drayman. On the 6th of Aug., 1842, I was in their service—some beer was sent out by three draymen—I received the invoice from Mr. Major, for some ale and porter to Mr. Gillingham—the prisoner took the beer to Gillingham—I delivered him the proper: invoice—the other man and me went together to other jobs.
HENRY GILLINGHAM . I live at Norwood—I had a quantity of porter and ale, on the 6th of August, 1842, from Elliott's—the prisoner brought in the beer—I paid him 9 l. 14 s. 2 d. for it—he signed the receipt with his initials, and left it with me.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you recollect him at all? A. Yes—I saw him put these initials J. W. on the paper—I believe it was in the evening.
MR. DOANE. Q. What did you do with the invoice and the receipt? A. I put them on the file—they sent for them, and I gave them back.
THOMAS BENJAMIN WALKER ( police-inspector.) In consequence of information I went on the 15th of March to the London Docks, and saw the prisoner—I asked if he had ever worked for Elliott's, the brewers, at Pimlico—he said, "No"—I said, "Well if you did, here is a gentleman here at hand who will know you"—I then took him to Mr. Thomas, and said, "This gentleman charges you with having absconded from the brewery after having received money on account of Messrs. Elliott, is that so?"—he said, "Yes"—I said, "How was it?"—he said, "I got too much to drink, I lost the money, and was ashamed to return."
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 28.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18440408-1239]

[trialAccount t18440408-1240]
1240. HENRY NORMAN was indicted for embezzlement.
MR. PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN WILLIAMS . I am servant to Mr. Woodward, a news agent, in Bull's Head-court. The prisoner was in the "Magnet" newspaper office, in the Strand—on the 22nd of March I paid him 2 l. 7 s. 6 d. for six quires and nine "Magnet" newspapers, on the prosecutor's account.
HENRY HALL . I am in the service of Mr. Brooks, a news agent, in Warwick-square. On the 21st of March I went to the "Magnet" newspaper office, in the Strand, and saw the prisoner—I paid him 3 l. 5 s. for eight quires and eighteen "Magnets"—I got this receipt from him.
JOSEPH HARRISON . I am in the service of Mr. Thomas, news agent, Finch-lane, Cornhill. On the 22nd of March I went to the "Magnet" office—I saw the prisoner, and paid him 2 l. 7 s. 6 d. for six quires and nine copies of the "Magnet."
JOHN BROWN BELL . I am the proprietor of the "Magnet" newspaper, in the Strand. The prisoner was in my employ in March, and had been two or three years, as clerk and publisher of my newspaper—it was part of his duty to receive money when persons came for newspapers—when he received money from the agents it was his duty to enter it in the daily cash-book, which I have here—it was his duty to pay me every day as I might require it—I do not find any entry of 8 l. 5 s. from Mr. Brooks, of Warwick-square, on the 21st of March—if he has received that money he has not paid it to me or accounted for it, except stating in a memorandum that he had received these monies, and had not accounted for them—that memorandum he sent on Saturday, the 22nd—I do not find any entries on the 22nd of March for 2 l. 7 s. 6 d. from Woodward, or 2 l. 7 s. 6 d. from Thomas—the prisoner has paid no such sum or accounted for them, except as I have stated—he left on Friday, the 22nd, and had no opportunity of making any entries afterwards—I desired him to make up his account, and I would have nothing more to do with him as a servant—I took his money away, and had some strong suspicion
that he had not accounted for various sums—he said, "I will let yon know in the morning"—I said, "Depend upon it I shall prosecute you, you may send your account"—he sent his account by his wife in the morning—he absented himself, but be came to my house on the following Wednesday, and I sent for an officer.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Was his duty defined and the mode specified in a written agreement? A. No—we bad no written agreement connected with the payment to be made to him—I had a security for monies—I do not know whether be came to me for the purpose of surrendering himself—he was there when I came home—his brother became security for any defalcation of his—he had a regular salary, no per centage on the papers sold—on the 22nd of March be made up his book, and paid me a balance of 18 l. 15 s.—he had been in the habit of accounting for the money every day—I said, "Is this all the money you have got?"—he hesitated, then put his hand into his pocket, and pulled out 30 s. more—I said, "Is there any more?"—he said, "I will let you know in the morning"—I said I should prosecute him—this is the memorandum he supplied me with—it accounts for these sums, and there are several others—I was not aware of these sums then.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18440408-1241]
1241. WILLIAM RATCLIFFE was indicted for stealing 1 card-case, value 15 s., the goods of Thomas Wilkinson, his master.
WILLIAM JAMES LAUGHTON . I am apprentice to Thomas Wilkinson, ironmonger, Regent-street—the prisoner was his apprentice. This card-case is my master's property—it has never been sold, because it is not in a finished state—it was in a glass case on the table in our warehouse—I do not know when it was lost.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. There was a good deal of valuable property there? A. Yes—the prisoner and I used to sit together, and drink and smoke—this case is not lined inside—I am certain I did not give it to him—I will swear I did not sell it—we have a porter and another young man.
WILLIAM THOMAS CASTLES , pawnbroker, at Knightsbridge. This card-case was pawned on the 13th of Feb., by a woman.
RICHARD MACKENZIE ( police-constable B 44.) I was at Queen-square police-office, before Mr. Burrell, on the 30th of March—I saw two females charged with felony—the prisoner was there—he came to me and claimed two waistcoats and this card-case, which he said he had purchased of a young man named Lowndes, and he had given it to his aunt, Mrs. Tate, to pawn—I had found the duplicate of this card-case on the woman Tate.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he not say he bought it of a young man at Mr. Wilkinson's, in Regent-street? A. Yes—I got the card-case from the pawnbroker—it was produced before the prisoner at the office—he was in the dock at the time—he was taken on the 4th of April—on the 30th of March he said he had given it to his aunt—I had the ticket in my hand that day, and he saw it—I believe his aunt was a washerwoman—the prisoner lives at Knightsbridge—he said nothing when the card-case was produced.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18440408-1242]
1242. GEORGE BROWN and JAMES ANDERSON were indicted for stealing 1 firkin, value 1 s.; and 84lbs. weight of butter, 3 l. 3 s.; the goods of Thomas Searle: and that they had both been before convicted of felony.
GEORGE TURNEY . I am in the service of Thomas Searle, a carrier, between Hounslow and London. On the afternoon of the 28th of March, I took up five firkins of butter at Trinity-lane, to take to William Stacey, at Hounslow—there were two firkins on the outside, and three in the cart—the
outside ones were fastened with a chain and a rope—about eight o'clock that evening I was passing through Knightsbridge—before I got to Kensington turnpike I found a jerk came on the mare's back—I jumped down, and found one of the firkins gone from behind, and the rope cut—before that the shafts did not come down on the mare—I ran back to look for the policeman—I ran back to Knightsbridge-barracks, and saw two gentlemen—from what they said, I looked through the rails, and saw the two prisoners in the Park with the firkin of butter—I am sure it was them—I ran round Barrack-alley, and got a policeman—we met the prisoners together with the firkin on their backs—I caught hold of Brown, who had the butter—he threw it off his shoulder on to my arms, and I was forced to let the butter go—he tried to get away—the sentry at the barracks put up his legs, and both the prisoners fell down together—I caught hold of them, and the policeman directly came running up—I did not lose sight of them at all till they were taken—it was William Stacey's firkin of butter, but it was in my charge.
STEPHEN MASTERS ( police-constable A 178.) About eight o'clock in the evening of the 28th of March, I met the prisoners together in Hyde-park, carrying this firkin of butter—they were coming from Kensington—I turned round after them—I soon after heard an alarm—I went up to Turney, and saw him catch Brown, who was carrying the butter, and he threw it down on to Turney's arms—he tried to run, but they were thrown down by the sentinel, and taken—I am sure they are the persons—they merely said they did not steal it—Brown said to a boy who was following, he wished be would go and tell the old woman what was up, he expected he should go this time—Anderson said he expected to be transported, when he was here before, but he only had six months.
ROBERT BLACKWELL . I produce a certificate of Brown's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—the prisoner is the person—I was present when he left the bar—he pleaded guilty before I came into the Court.
JAMES ANDERSON ( police-constable V 17.) I produce a certificate of Anderson's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—I was present when he received sentence—he is the person.
BROWN — GUILTY . Aged 17.
ANDERSON — GUILTY Aged 18.
Transported for Seven Years.

1244. GEORGE BIRCH was indicted for stealing 3 planes, value 6 s., and 2 saws, 4 s.; the goods of Thomas Downes, his master; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
THOMAS DOWNES , bedstead-maker, Flower and Dean-street, Spital-fields. On the 5th of Feb. I missed three planes and two saws from my workshop—I bad seen them safe the day before—the prisoner was at work at my place that morning—I accused him of taking them—he beckoned one of his shopmates out—I did not see him again for two months—I have not seen the tools again.
JOSEPH READER . I work for the prosecutor. On the morning of the 5th of Feb., he complained of missing his tools, and accused the prisoner of taking them—the prisoner beckoned me out, and confessed to me that he had taken them for drink, and he wanted me to go to his lodging to show me the tickets of his tools—I would not go—he went away, and did not come back.
JOHN SHEARING . I work for the prosecutor. On Monday, 1st of April, I went to the Globe, in the Borough-road, and found the prisoner—he said, "Jack, you are going to take me"—I said, "Yes," and told him to come borne with me to my master—he said, "Yes, I will;" and in coming along, he owned that he took the tools for drink, and he was willing to pay for them, provided his master would take him back to his employ—I gave him in charge on Tower-hill.
GEORGE HYDE ( City police-constable, No. 516.) The prisoner was given to me—he said it was all through drink, or it would not have happened.
GEORGE TEAKLE ( police-sergeant H 8.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY . Aged 26.— Confined Twelve Months.
[trialAccount t18440408-1245]
1245. PETER WINCEY was indicted for stealing three sheets, value 7 s.; the goods of Mark Hammond.
HANNAH HAMMOND . I am the wife of Mark Hammond, and live in Chapman-street, Commercial-road, East. The prisoner lodged at my house for three weeks and three days—he occupied a furnished room, with his wife and two children—he left on the 23rd of March—I missed three sheets and a table-cloth from the room—I afterwards saw the prisoner—I charged him with having taken them—he said he had taken them and pledged them, and said I should have them returned—I said, "It is impossible I can take your word, I have waited nine days, in consequence of a note you sent me"—he did not let me know where he had gone to—these are the sheets.
Prisoner. Q. On the Saturday before I left, did you not tell me if I was in want of money, I might pledge one of these articles? A. Certainly not—you did not say if I would lend you 1 s. till the following day, you would not part with them—I did not send word if you wanted a loaf of bread you might have it—I found this note down by the door, on the evening they sent me the key—they did not send me any address— note read —"Mrs. Hammond, I am sorry to leave you, but as it was your wish for us to do so, we have done so. I am again disappointed in receiving my money, and shall not have it till April. Your sheets and table-cover are at wash; and on Monday evening you shall have them. I will send you the rent with them."
WILLIAM STARR ( policeman.) I went with Mrs. Hammond to No. 13, Baker's-row, and found the prisoner—she gave him in charge—I asked where the sheets were—he said they were in the wash, and then be told me they were in pledge—I received some duplicates from his wife.
HENRY EDWARD STREET , shopman to Messrs, Walker and Brown, pawn brokers.
I have two sheets, pawned by a female—these are the two tickets I gave her.
WILLIAM COLE , shopman to Mr. Savage, pawnbroker, Whitechapel. I have a sheet, pawned by a woman—I gave her this duplicate.
Prisoner's Defence. I was there three weeks; the last twelve days I was in rather distressing circumstances; I was going out on the Saturday morning, and, when in the street, she called me and told me as I was going to receive money I might make use of a blanket or sheet; I told her I would not do so if it was not requisite, but if I was disappointed I should feel obliged if she would allow me to do so.
GUILTY . Aged 31.— Recommended to mercy.— Confined Three Months.
NEW COURT.— Saturday, April 13 th, 1844.
Sixth Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.

1248. WILLIAM BROWN was indicted for feloniously receiving 7 knives, value 3 s.; 13 forks, 6 s.; and 2 printed books, value 15 s.; the goods of Joseph Singleton, clerk; well knowing them to have been stolen.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
REV. JOSEPH SINGLETON . I live at Bay Braham, in Cambridgeshire. On the 14th of Dec. my house was broken into—the persons got in through a larder window—I lost, among other property, a considerable quantity of plate, two dozen knives, two dozen forks, and a number of books—since the 26th of Feb., two books, seven knives, and thirteen forks have been produced by the officer—they formed part of the property stolen from my house that night—the books are a copy of "Milton," and the "Life of Christ."
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. I believe the parties you suspect have been prosecuted? A. No—these are London knives, and have "Alderman, Barbican," on them—they are not of a very recent purchase—here is one of another description, and of another maker—I lost two knives of this sort—there was some writing in this "Life of Christ," but the leaf has been taken out.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. I believe the persons have been prosecuted, but not for this charge? A. I am not certain whether they were in this robbery; it was proved that they had my property in their possession—their names were Cuttriss, Hall, and Bennett—I am firmly persuaded that these knives and forks are mine—I had some of the same description and the same maker.
JAMES BRANNAN ( police-sergeant G 20.) I know the prisoner—I was a witness against Cuttriss and Bennett at the last Chelmsford assizes—I have never seen them with the prisoner—on the 26th of February I went to the prisoner's house, Nelson-street, Hackney-road, with Mr. Clark, the superintendent of the Essex constabulary—the prisoner opened the door—I said, "I want you"—he said, "What for?"—I said, "I will tell you"—I went in—I saw a female take a key off the mantel-piece and go up stairs in a hurried manner—I follower her—she came down, and when I came down after her the prisoner was gone—Clark went in pursuit of him—I waited there some time—when Clark came back we searched, and I found these knives and forks
in a woollen stocking, in a drawer in a chest of drawers in the front parlour—I placed them on the top of the drawers, and whether the female took them away or not I cannot say, but I came away without them—I might have forgotten them, but, to the best of my belief, they were removed—I went back for them on the following Monday, and they were gone—there was more property there, which is not here, I could not find owners for it—I afterwards found the knives and forks in possession of Jane Squires—I believe they are the game I found in the drawer in the parlour—I took away the books, and have had them ever since.
Cross-examined. Q. When you came back you spoke to the prisoner's wife, and she directed you to Squires, who gave you the knives and forks? A. She did after some considerable time—the prisoner's is a private house—he has one lodger, from what I have understood—it is a four-roomed house.
JOHN TIMEWELL CLARK . I am superintendent of the Essex constabulary. I accompanied Brannan to the prisoner's house on the 26th of Feb.—Brannan went up stairs with the woman—I remained down, and was watching another person who was there—I heard the front window go, and the prisoner went out at the front door—these two books were found in the bureau in the front parlour.
JANE SQUIRES . I am single, and live next door to the prisoner, in Nelson-street, Hackney-road. On Friday, the 1st of March, I was going by the prisoner's window, which was shut—his wife tapped at the window, and I went in as far as the parlour door, and she gave me these knives and forks—I kept them till the following Monday, and then gave them to the officer.
EMMA DEAN . I am in the service of the Honourable Algernon Herbert, at Eggleton, in the county of Cambridge. There was a robbery at my master's on the 18th of Jan., and this work-box, bag, and writing-desk, are part of the property lost on that occasion.
Cross-examined. Q. How much property was lost altogether? A. About 20 l. worth.
JOHN PARTRIDGE . I am coachman to Mr. Hill. This box-coat is mine—my master's coach-house was broken open, and I lost this coat.
SALLY GIBLIN . I live with my uncle, Mr. Scott, at Wendon, near Saffron Walden. On the 25th of Jan. my uncle's house was broken open—this carving-knife and candlestick are my uncle's, and were stolen—the fork belonging to this knife was found close by our gate—I was a witness at Chelmsford against Cuttriss and the other.
GEORGE TOWNSEND . I am constable at Fordham, in Cambridgeshire. I know the prisoner—a woman, who went as his sister, lives housekeeper with me—this great-coat was brought to my house—I did not see the prisoner—his sister showed it to me on Thursday, the 8th of Feb., before the prisoner was taken—I took possession of it.
JAMES BRANNAN re-examined. There was another man lodging in the prisoner's house, in the one-pair back room—these articles were not found in the room he lodged in—the books, knives and forks were in the parlour, down stairs, where there was no sleeping at all—Hayward brought the prisoner to the station next morning.
JAMES HAYWARD ( police-constable G 212.) The prisoner was brought to the Police-court at Worship-street by his own attorney, and I took him.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18440408-1249]
1249. WILLIAM BROWN was again indicted > for feloniously receiving 1 carving-knife, value 8 s.; 1 taper-stand, 2 s.; and 1 extinguisher, 1 s.; the goods of Thomas Scott; well knowing the same to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
MR. RYLAND conducted the Prosecution.
SALLY GIBLIN . I am niece of Thomas Scott, and live at Wendon, in Essex, about forty miles from London. On the night of the 25th of Jan. my uncle's house was broken open and robbed—I saw the thieves—there was a carving-knife, a taper-stand, and a toast-rack taken—these are them—I bought the knife myself—it was bruised at one end—I was obliged to give evidence at Chelmsford, when two of the thieves were convicted—my uncle is very aged.
JAMES BRANNAN ( police-sergeant G 20.) I went to the prisoner's house, in Nelson-street, on the 26th of Feb.—there was a lodger there—I spoke to him in his back room up stairs—he was called as a witness for the prisoner before the Magistrate, and made a statement—I found this carving-knife and taper-stand in a box under some clothes in the front room up stairs, and the toast-rack in a little box in the same room—the prisoner went away on that occasion—I saw him next day at the station—when he saw me come in, he held out his hand, and said, "Do you want me?"—I said, "You know we do, or else you would not run away"—he said, "I did not run away, I walked away; now what do you want me for?"—I said, "For being concerned, with others, in breaking and entering several houses in the borders of Cambridgeshire and Essex"—he said, "Lord bless you, I never broke into anybody's house in my life"—I said, "We took a deal of property from your place"—he said, "I can account for that, it is all mine, don't alarm yourself"—I am sure he said that—Clark was close to him—there were several examinations on the separate cases before the Magistrate.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. How long have you been in the police? A. Nearly fourteen years—I did not state in the last case that the prisoner said that all the property was his, because I was ordered to stand down—Harbour was called by the prisoner—he is outside—we took away from the prisoner's house, besides these things, some plated candlesticks and some bridle-bits.
GUILTY . Aged 44.— Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18440408-1250]
1250. CHARLOTTE SINCLAIR was indicted for stealing 1 pair of gloves, value 1 s. the goods of George Clark.
MR. Jones conducted the Prosecution.
GEORGE CLARK , linen-draper, Upper Whitecross-street. On Saturday afternoon, the 9th of March, the prisoner came into my shop and asked for black kid gloves, white kid gloves, and gauntlet gloves—I showed her some black kid gloves at 1 s. a pair—she asked for some better, and as I walked to the top of the shop I looked round very short, and saw her take a pair of kid gloves out of the paper which was opened, and put them down into her lap—I returned and opened the other kid gloves—she then asked for various others—I said, "I have no others"—she said, "You have nothing good enough or large enough"—she then went away without purchasing any thing—I followed her—she turned to the right out of my shop, and then to the left down Old-street—mine is the last house in the street, except Mr. Hammond's—I returned into the shop and rang the shop bell—my wife came down—I then went to the kid gloves and counted them over, which I had done previously—I found one pair missing—there had been nine pair, and there were only eight—I followed the prisoner—as I crossed the road I met her—she said, "I presume you are the gentleman whose shop I was in"—I said, "Yes"—she said, "I have left my umbrella"—I said, "I have not
noticed it, walk back"—the took the umbrella and left—a young man two shops off said something to me—I then followed the prisoner—I saw a policeman coming—I overtook her near Mr. Sinclair's in Old-street, and said "I want you"—she said "What for?"—I said "For stealing a pair of gloves"—she said "I have no gloves of yours"—I said "No, you have thrown them away"—I took her back—the policeman was with me—she was very desirous of having some conversation with me—I said I would not—I saw her fumbling about her arm, and said, "What have you got there?"—she said, "Nothing of yours"—it was a little basket with a pair of black kid gloves in it, which I thought were mine, but they were not—she said she was very respectable, and should not be guilty of any thing of the kind—I ultimately gave her into custody—a man named Parks was brought into my shop while the prisoner was there—he produced a pair of black kid gloves—they were mine—he said in the prisoner's presence that he saw her distinctly throw them away in the area close by my door—I swear that no other person was in the shop while I was showing her the gloves—these are my gloves, and those I missed that day—a neighbour named Brown was there when the prisoner was there, and said something to me.
Cross-examined by MR. CHAMBERS. Q. Brown beard every thing that occurred on the prisoner being brought back? A. Yes, he attended before the Magistrate, his deposition was taken in writing, and read over to him—he did not sign it, because the Magistrate said he would not be required—I only showed one packet of gloves at first to the prisoner—she did not try any on—we do not allow ladies to try gloves on unless they are expensive ones—she was sitting—I do not know whether she pulled her own gloves off—I do not think she did—I Should think I was twenty-three feet from her when I turned and saw her take the gloves—I do not remember that I said, before I was examined before the Magistrate, that I turned and saw her take something from the packet of gloves on the counter—I think I can swear that was not my expression—there was nothing to take but gloves—she had no purse or reticule that I saw—she had a muff and a little basket—I did not see a purse at all, unless it was at the station—the searcher had something, but really I did not notice it—the packets of gloves are not ticketed—we have them by the dozen, unless they are good gloves—I thought by seeing a person of her appearance take a pair of gloves I should do right to follow her into the street and give her in charge—I went out and met her coming back to the shop—when she first went out she walked backwards and forwards a little confused, and then crossed diagonally into Old-street—a young man, a young lady, myself and my wife, attend to my shop—there is a pork-shop next door—she did not go near that shop—before she came back for the umbrella she walked backwards and forwards, as if she did not know which way to go—I am sure she did not go near the pork-shop after she returned for the umbrella—she threw her muff on to the counter when she saw I looked at her—it had been in her lap previously—I saw nothing of the basket at the time—we do not sell many kid gloves—we buy them at wholesale houses in the City—she was remanded in the first instance for the depositions to be taken on the Wednesday, then remanded till Saturday, and then finally admitted to bail—I am certain there was no other person in the shop—I never said I really could not recollect whether there was or not—it was not after I bad heard a person examined by the Magistrate that I said there was no one—my answer to the Magistrate was taken down—the officer went into the shop with me, and after that the carter came—Mr. Mountcastle's lad brought him in—the carter offered to show where he picked up the gloves—he said, "Who is to pay me my expenses—I am a poor man—I cannot lose the time"—I said
his expenses would be paid—the officer did not ask if I was going to give her in charge—I did not say "Decidedly not; I did not see her take them"—the carter said something about throwing the gloves down the grating or area—the officer did not whisper any thing to me at that time—there is an opening under the window at Mr. Hammond's shop, and an iron grating drops down inside; and as you enter my shop door this little opening is just at the left, hardly a yard off.
MR. JONES. Q. Have you ever expressed any doubt as to what you saw the prisoner take up? A. No—I did not give anything to Parks for attending but what I asked the Magistrate if I might give him, merely to pay his expenses.
JOHN PARKS . I am carter to Mr. Maskin, of Maidstone—I was going up White Cross-street on the afternoon of the 9th of March, and saw the prisoner near the corner of White Cross-street—she stooped and hurled a pair of black gloves away—she was going towards Mr. Clark's shop—he was walking in front of her—I got out of my cart and picked up the gloves at the side of Mr. Hammond's, the ironmonger's, window just by the grating—his shop is the corner, and Mr. Clark's is the next door—some one came after me, and brought me back to Mr. Clark's shop—I gave the gloves to him.
Cross-examined. Q. How far had you got with the gloves? A. About three or four rods—I thought as the lady was dressed in a respectable way, she did not want them; in fact, I did not know what they were, till I got up into the van again—I did not know who was the owner, and whether the lady wanted them or not—I was driving off down White Cross-street—I must have gone by the pork-shop—I do not know whether there is a grating under the pork-shop window—it was two or three minutes after I took the gloves that I was brought back—there were some persons in the street.
JAMES MARLEY . I am in the employ of Mr. Britton, of Upper White Cross-street—there is only one shop between ours and Mr. Clark's—in the afternoon of the 9th of March I saw Parks go past, and my attention was drawn to him—he got out of his cart and picked up the gloves at the other side of Mr. Clark's door, towards Old-street, just by the grating of Mr. Hammond's shop—I have measured the distance from Mr. Hammond's grating to the pork-shop, and it is 10 yards—I told Mr. Clark what I had seen—I attended at the police-office, and was not called.
---- BROWN. I keep a manufactory in Upper White Cross-street—I was near Mr. Clark's shop on the afternoon of the 9th of March—I saw Parks leave his cart, stoop down, and pick up a small black parcel near Mr. Clark's door—I think it was near Mr. Hammond's—I would not swear positively on which side he picked it up—he went on down White Cross-street—I told Mr. Clark what I had seen, and Parks was fetched back—I saw the prisoner at Mr. Clark's, under charge—Mr. Clark said she had stolen a pair of gloves—she said no she had not, she was too respectable to do anything of the sort; and from her respectable appearance, I called Mr. Clark to the end of the shop, and asked if he was certain—he said he was positive he saw her take them off the counter—she wanted to speak to him in private—he said, "I have nothing to say to you"—I went to the station-house.
Cross-examined. Q. Were you standing at your own door? A. No—I was crossing from Old-street into White Cross-street—I noticed Mr. Clark when he came out—I went into the shop after the things were picked up.
WALTER WHITE ( police-constable G 168.) I received the prisoner in charge with these gloves from Mr. Clark—I saw him receive them from Parks.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you hear every word that was said between Mr. Clark and the prisoner? A. No—I heard Mr. Clark say he charged her
with stealing a pair of gloves—I heard what was said—I did not bear Parks say, "Who is to pay me for my trouble"—Mr. Clark said be should be paid his expenses—I do not remember my asking whether he was going to give the prisoner into custody—he said he should give her into custody for steal­ing the gloves—I do not recollect whispering to Mr. Clark—nothing of the sort occurred.
HARRIET HAYWARD . I am searcher at the station at Featherstone-street. The prisoner was brought there—I searched her, and found in the pocket of her dress, half a pound of soap and a new pair of mittens; and while I was taking the soap out, she took the mittens out of her pocket, tore the ticket off them, and threw it under the cell—I examined her basket, and found a new pair of black gloves, a new pair of mittens, an orange, seven samples of loaf-sugar, five pieces of cheese, a bottle of ink, and 4 s.
Cross-examined. Q. These lumps and pieces were all wrapped up sepa­rately? A. Yes—two lumps in each paper—she said they were samples—one of them was marked 7 1/2 d.
MR. JONES. Q. Was the 4 s. in a purse? A. Yes.
GUILTY . Aged 39.— Confined Six Months.

1252. MARY ANN WINGATE was indicted for stealing 31 yards of satin, value 27 l.; 15 yards of velvet, 15 l.; 1 scent-bottle, 1 l. 1 s.; 1 necklace, 5 s.; and 5 yards of linen-cloth, 10 s.; also 1 shawl, 1 l. 1 s.; 3 sheets, 12 s.; 2 table-cloths, 10 s.; and 6 table-napkins, 10 s.; the goods of Georgiana Charlotte Theobald, her mistress; to which she pleaded

[trialAccount t18440408-1253]
1253. FREDERICK RADFORD, FREDERICK SHAW , and JAMES SHAW were indicted for a burglary in the dwelling-house of Jacob Worster, about the hour of one in the night of the 2nd of Feb., with intent to steal, and stealing therein, 36 shells, value 1 l. 1 s.; 230 brushes. 3 l. 10 s.; 3000 leaves of gold, 7 l. 10 s.; 25 packets of metal, 1 l.; and loz. weight of gold-dust, 4 l.; the goods of Jacob Worster, in his dwelling-house.
MR. ROBINSON conducted the Prosecution.
JACOB WORSTER . I am an oil and colourman, and live in New Compton-st., in the parish of St. Giles-in-the-Fields; it is my dwelling-house. At nine in the evening of 2nd Feb., I locked the premises up with a padlock—I have a side door into the private passage—there is a shop door and a private door, both into the street—the outside private door was fastened, as usual, with a latch—it could be opened from the outside with a key, not without—there was an inner door to the shop from the passage—it was fastened with a padlock—I came down next morning a little after seven, and it was wide open—the padlock was on the ground—I got into the shop, and found a great number of the drawers taken out of the nest, and left on the counter empty—I missed all the gold, a great quantity of brushes, some gold-dust, and other things—some of the brushes I had had in the evening before—what I lost was above 20 l. worth—the prisoners Shaws have come to my house for gold-books—I buy gold-books, and sell them—this is part of the property I lost that night—here is one of the brushes that 1 had the same evening—I marked it as I had it in, with my own private mark.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. I believe when you sell brushes you
do not rob off the private marks? A. No—these brushes came in between seven and eight in the evening—they were perfectly safe in my premises at nine, when I closed, and had not been undone—there were three dozen of these; one dozen were gone, and two left—that was in another drawer, which they had not opened—I cannot say exactly how many brushes were produced by Weston—I gave information, and on the 1st of March I was fetched to the station, and a great quantity of brushes were produced—these gilt shells were produced by West—they are worth 7 s. a dozen, and the brushes about 1 l.
JAMES ADDAMS . I am an artist's brush-maker—I make brushes for the prosecutor—these brushes are particular, and are made for gilders alone—they are different to what I make for artists—I have no other customer but the prosecutor, that deals with gilders—I sold him three dozen the evening before—these are two of them—I am positive.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. What do you know them by? A. Being bound with finer thread than usually, and made on shorter and more taper handles than in general—I made only three down, and Mr. Worster had them all—these seven other brushes are of my make.
MR. ROBINSON. Q. Of those that were produced by Weal, do you see any there that you made for Mr. Worster? A. Here is only one that I can speak positively to.
GEORGE WESTON ( police-sergeant Z 3.) On the 1st of March I searched Radford's premises, and found twenty gold shells and forty-eight brushes is his box—he gave Pocock the key of it—when I went in I charged him with another offence—he made no answer.
WILLIAM WEST ( police-constable F 106.) On the 1st of March I accompanied another officer to a house in Mercer-street, Long-acre—I there saw Frederick Shaw's wife—I found twenty brushes in the drawer—Frederick Shaw was not present when we went, which was about eight o'clock in the morning—he came in about nine—Pocock said he must consider himself in custody—I do not know what his reply was.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. How many examinations were there? A. Two—James Shaw was not in custody at first—I saw him in the Court when the other prisoners were charged—I do not know that he was there to give evidence for his brother—I did not hear him, nor any one on his behalf say so—I saw him standing outside while I was inside, on the 1st of March—he was taken, I think, about a week ago, about the 6th of April—the second examination was about a week after the first—I saw James Shaw in the street then—I saw him in the Court on the first occasion, but I was inside, beyond the door, and he was outside—he was not called as a witness on either side.
WILLIAM POCOCK ( police-constable F 81.) On the 1st of March I went with Weston, and assisted in searching the room—in a drawer under the bed I found 112 brushes, and in removing the bed, a paper parcel fell down on the floor between the bed and the wall, which contained twenty-eight more brushes, and two balls of string—they appeared close between the bed and the wall—the drawers were not locked—James Shaw came in while we were in the room—I asked him if he knew Frederick and Edward Radford—he said he did—I told him they were in custody for robbery, and he was charged with being concerned—he asked what robbery I meant—I said, Mr. Ward, of Compton-street, and Mr. Smith, bookbinder, Long-acre.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Were you present during both examinations? A. Yes—I did not hear the gentleman tell the Magistrate that
James Shaw was there—I heard it stated that then were respectable witnesses to be called for Frederick.
JOHN HOLT . oil and colourman, East-street, Manchester-square. About eleven o'clock on Saturday evening, the 3rd of Feb., James Shaw came into my shop as we were about closing business—he said, "I have a job, or rare lot of Dutch metal, will you have it of me?"—I said, "This is a very late time to offer goods, or to tease me about them, I would rather not be troubled with them now"—he pressed me to take them—I asked what he wanted for it—he said 16 s.—I said I did not want it, but said, "I will take it of you, I suppose I shall sell it some time, I will give you 14 s. for it which I did—this is the metal—he said he had a quantity of brushes to dispose of, and some gold shells—I did not purchase them—on the, following Monday he brought some brushes—I believe this to be the parcel—they were found in Frederick Shaw's bedroom—I opened the parcel and looked at them—this particular one, bound with copper, attracted my attention—I had not seen any like it before.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. You had been a customer of his father's? A. Yes—I knew him well—the brushes, were similar to these—I am quite positive be spoke about gold shells.
WILLIAM CORN . I am foreman to Messrs. Kelsey, of Falcon-square. In, the early part of Feb. last, James Shaw brought a quantity of brushes to sell;—I said I had not time to look at them—he said he took them for a debt—he called again and I had not then time to look at them—he called again and took them away, and said the party had come to an arrangement, and he would take them back—in a few days he brought them again, and I refused to have them—they were a similar sort to these, and bad the name of John Addams on several of them—I swear that this camera hairbrush was one of the parcel—there is a mark on it.
JACOB WORSTSER re-examined. I know this camel's hair brush—it was made for a particular customer which I have—it was safe on the 2nd of Feb., and gone on the 3rd—out of the forty-eight found I identify these two that came from Bedford's box, and these other seven that I speak to, came from Frederick Shaw's—I believe the gold shells to be mine—they have no mark on them—they were tied in a brown paper when they were taken—I have not seen that paper again.
Witnesses for the Defence.
MART ANN MOORE . I am servant to Mr. Frederick Shaw, the prisoner's father—he lives in Denmark-street—on the 27th of February, Radford came there with a blue bag—he asked if Mr. Shaw was at home—I said No, James was at home—he said "James, I can't sell these brushes, will you buy them?"—James Shaw said, "Let us look at them," and there were a great many brushes turned out of the bag on to the parlour table—James said, "How much do you want for them?"—I did not hear what Radford said, but James said, "I will give you 25 s. for them"—William, the apprentice, was cleaning the window, and Mr. Osborn was there—I cannot say whether any bargain was made, but two hours after I saw the brushes there—James took them away either into his bed-room or into the, shop—I think I should know the bag again—it was a small bag lined with bed-tick.
MR. ROBINSON. Q. I believe you are married? A. Yes—I live with the elder Shaw merely as a servant—I swear I do not live with him. at his wife—I am separated from my husband.
HENRY DOWELL . I am a carpenter, and live in Denmark-street, next door to Shaw's. On the 28th of (I forget the name of the month)—it was
this year, and two days before Frederick Shaw was taken—I was standing at my door—James Shaw came to me—he said, "I have had a few words with my father," and asked me if I would come and help him carry some things—he took me up into his bed-room—took out some gas pipes, a brass lamp, and a blue bag, and we took them to Mercer-street, Long-acre—we came out—had a drop of drink, and I left him—it was between twelve and one o'clock in the day.
MR. ROBINSON. Q. Have you never been in the service of Mr. Shaw? A. No—I know him by occasionally seeing his sons now and then—I have called on his sons—I am not on intimate terms with him—I have been told by several persons that old Shaw has been taken up, but I do not know it of my own knowledge—I was never taken up myself nor charged with anything.
COURT. Q. Did you see what you carried? A. Yes—he took a blue bag out of his box containing brushes—I cannot say how many—there was a string to the bag—the mouth was open and I saw a great many brushes—I dare say I saw fifty—I am not sure.
MART ANN MOORE re-examined. I am servant to Shaw's father—Radford first came on the 3rd of February to Mr. Shaw's with a bundle under his arm—I do not know what was in it—he went into the shop—James Shaw was there—I did not go into the shop—Mr. Woodford was in the shop, and the apprentice, who is always there.
GEORGE WOODFIELD . I am a brush-maker, and live in York place, Somers-town—I was in Mr. Shaw's shop on the 3rd of Feb.—Radford came in and brought a bundle—he said he had got a job lot of brushes and Dutch leaf—he asked James Shaw if he would purchase them—he said "No, but I will take them to one of my father's customers in the evening after I have done work"—I heard nothing said about a price—I left Radford there—I saw the brushes and the Dutch metal opened, but I did not minutely examine them—I waited there to see the elder Mr. Shaw and came away.
COURT. Q. Did you hear that the Shaws were taken into custody? A. I heard of it afterwards—the elder Mr. Shaw's apprentice was in the shop when Radford called, and no one else—the apprentice was at work—it was between eleven and twelve o'clock in the day—I am a brush-maker—I have lived in the neighbourhood where I now live for thirty-five years—I do not keep a shop—I have known old Mr. Shaw thirty-five years—I have since learned that the Shaws were at work at Mr. Seaman's up to the time of the robbery.
MR. PAYNE. Q. Are you sober? A. That I will be judged by the company—I have had some porter and a draught of water—I cannot tell how much spirits was in it—that remains with the publican, how much he put in—the elder Shaw came to ask me to give evidence last Saturday at my residence—he did not offer me any thing for coming.
MR. ROBINSON. Q. Are you not a relation of old Mr. Shaw? A. Yes, and the prisoner's uncle.
COURT. Q. What was the apprentice doing that morning? A. He was at work at gold-beating—James Shaw was in the shop at work likewise—the old man was not there—I am quite sure there was nobody else in the shop—I cannot tell how long Mary Ann Moore has lived there—sometimes I go there once in three months, and sometimes once in six months—I went there that day, as I had a little order, which I took home, and did not get my money—I went to borrow some money of the elder Shaw—I did not say any thing about it when I arrived—the female opened the door—I asked if Mr. Shaw was at home—she said, "No"—I said, "Do you expect him in soon?"—she said, "I don't know, I don't think he will be long"—I said, "I will go down
to the shop and sit down"—I stopped about an hour and a half—I was talking to James Shaw and to the apprentice—I smoked a pipe during the time—I had nothing to drink—I cannot call to mind what I was talking about—to the best of my belief Moore came down once, I cannot tell for what, as I was sitting in the chimney corner partly all the time—nobody came in but the young woman, and she did not stay above a second or two—she did not speak to me when she came down—I left Radford there when I went—I had not been there many minutes when Radford came—he did nothing but saunter about the shop—he did not sit down nor smoke a pipe, or have any thing to eat or drink—he was in the shop all the time—I recollect this was on the 3rd of Feb., because I was disappointed of receiving some money, and I went to try to borrow some to get over Saturday night—I had not been there for a month or two before—I am not in the habit of going very often—I should say the girl was there when I went there before, but I did not see her—one of the nephews opened the door—I have been there eight or ten times during the last twelve months—I believe the girl was there all those times—old Mr. Shaw is not married—James lives with him—he is not married—the apprentice and the maid, and James Shaw and the old man are in the house, that is all.
COURT to MARY ANN MOORE. Q. How long have you lived at Mr. Shaw's? A. I should say three months—I do not know whether it is three months—I have seen the last witness there two or three times—I saw Radford come on the 3rd of Feb.—I think it was about dinner time—we dine about one o'clock, and he came before dinner—Mr. Shaw was not at home that day—James Shaw dined there, and George Shaw, and William the apprentice—I am sure Radford came before dinner, because I had the meat in my hand, which was not cooked, and when his knock came I laid it on the table—it was a steak—I opened the door for Radford, and he asked if James Shaw was at home—I said, "Yes, in the shop"—I think it was about an hour after that that we had dinner—I did not take any notice—I think Woodfield came before Radford did, but I am not sure—I let Radford in, but I think James Shaw let Woodfield in—after Radford came I was dressing the dinner—I cannot tell how long Wood field was there—I do not see persons go out—I never go into the shop—I am quite sure I was not in the shop that day—when the apprentice is wanted I always call him—I did not let Woodfield or Radford out.
WILLIAM THATCHER . I am apprentice to Shaw's father—I have been so upwards of five years—I know Radford—I have seen him at my master's frequently—I remember Radford coming with a bundle, on Saturday, the 3 d. of Feb., when my master was out—he saw James Shaw in the shop where I was at work—the bundle contained Dutch metal and brushes—Mr. Wood-field was in the shop during the time—Radford asked James Shaw to purchase them—he said he did not know what to say about them, he would take them to a customer of his father's, Mr. Holt in East-street, Manchester-square—Radford left them and went away—I believe Radford came again on the Monday following—I cannot say to a day, it was the beginning of the next week—I saw James Shaw return the brushes to Radford on that occasion—he had sold the Dutch metal, but could not sell the brushes.
MR. PAYNE. Q. What time did Radford come that day? A. I cannot say to an hour—I suppose it was between six and seven o'clock in the evening—Mr. Woodfield was there when he came—I do not know what he came about—I suppose Woodfield remained there a couple of hours—he was sitting down in the shop—I cannot tell what they were talking about—we were all in conversation together—Radford talked—Woodfleld was waiting for old Mr. Shaw—that was all he did—he sat part of the time on the stool by the side
of me, at the cutting-stand, not in the chimney-corner exactly—he had nothing to drink—and he did nothing but sometimes sit, and sometimes walk and talk to us—Radford went away first.
MR. ROBINSON. Q. Did you see Mary Ann Moore during that time? A. I saw her up stairs once, and she came down once for some coke or coals—I do not remember her coming into the shop.
COURT. Q. Who came first? A. Wood field came first—he staid about two hours, and Radford about one hour—I recollect the day, by setting down my work; and I received a letter in the morning from my mother—I believe it was on the 1st of March that I heard of the charge against these prisoners—I was at Bow-street to give evidence, but was not called—I got up about seven o'clock that morning—if I recollect right, old Mr. Shaw was out all day—James Shaw was at work and me—no one else—I worked till breakfasttime, eight o'clock—I breakfasted up stairs—James Shaw and I breakfasted together—I worked from breakfast till dinner, and James Shaw was with me—there was nobody else with us at work—Radford came down in the shop, but I cannot recollect whether it was before dinner—there was only me and James Shaw at work—we dined at one o'clock—we had potatoes and meat—cold mutton to the best of my recollection—Moore was all this time in the parlour up stairs—I saw her come down once before dinner for coals or coke—I continued to work after dinner till about four or five o'clock, when I had tea up stairs—there was Moore and James Shaw, and I—I went down after that to the shop—Woodfield then came, and then Mr. Radford arrived—I am sure it was after tea that Woodfield came—I cannot say to an hour, but I think it was directly we came down from tea—it was not before tea to my recollection—I am sure it was towards the evening—he was there about half-an-hour before Radford came, and Radford went away first—I do not remember that Radford or Woodfield had any thing to drink while they were there—neither of them did any thing—no pipes or tobacco was called for—there was no smoking—I have known Moore about nine months I think—she had been living with Mr. Shaw that time—there was no servant there before her—there was a woman who used to cook and that, but did not live in the house—I suppose we may term Moore a servant, she sleeps in the house, but I do not. know, who she sleeps with—there is a sister she sleeps with—I believe it is her sister—there are two girls in the house—her sister's name is Emma Moore—they both may be called servants, they do the duty of the house—I believe on my oath that those two women sleep together—they both do work of all kinds—they do for the house between them—Emma came first—I do not recollect how long she has been there—I do not know how it was we wanted a second servant—it is very seldom that Emma comes into the shop—I believe Emma was not at home on the day Radford came—the was visiting some of her friends.
RADFORD — GUILTY . Aged 28.
JAMES SHAW — GUILTY .— Transported for Fifteen Years.
FREDERICK SHAW — NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18440408-1254]
1254. FREDERICK RADFORD and FREDERICK SHAW were again indicted for stealing 110 rings, value 60 l.; 73 brooches, 38 l.; 16 lockets, 10 l.; 44 swivels, 8 l. 10 s.; 180 tubes, 9 l.; 38 ring-tips, 4 l.; 42 brooches, 4 l.; 13 seals, 5 l.; 3 chains, 2 l.; 26 watchhooks, 4 l.; 1145 watch-keys, 43 l.; 4 jewel-boxes, 3 l. 5 s.; 4 watch-guard chains, 5 l.; and 2 bags, 1 s.; the goods of Ephraim Smith and others, in their dwelling-house.
MR. ROBINSON conducted the Prosecution.
EPHRAIM SMITH . I carry on business as a jeweller, at No. 4, Carlile-street,
in the parish of St. Ann, Sobo—my mother and two brothers are in partnership with me—it is our dwelling-house. On the 24th of Feb. I left my property all safe about seven o'clock in the evening—I was in the kitchen having my tea—on the following morning all the property stated was gone—it was worth about 200 l.—this key and seal is part of it, and this bag was made to fit a box—I am confident it is my bag.
EMMA SMITH . I know this bag—I made it.
GEORGE WESTON ( police-sergeant F 6.) On the 1st of March I searched Radford's lodging, and found this key and seal in his box—he did not say anything about it.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. It was when you went about the brushes you found them? A. Yes, in the box which he gave Pocock the key of.
WILLIAM WEST . On the 1st of March I went to Mercer-street, Long-acre. Frederick Shaw came in after we were there—I found this bag there—I apprehended Shaw—he said nothing relative to this bag, but, on the way to the station, he said he came honestly by the brushes.
MR. BALLANTINE called
HENRY DOWELL . I took a blue bag to the house of Frederick Shaw—it contained some brushes—I do not know whether it contained any other articles—it was very much like this bag, and somewhat about this site—I took it by the desire of James Shaw—Frederick Shaw was not at home when, I left them—there was no one at home but his wife—I was before the Magis-trate, but was not examined.
RADFORD — GUILTY . Aged 28.— Transported for Ten Years.
FREDERICK SHAW — NOT GUILTY .

CAROLINE GREEK . In August last I resided at No. 22, Cavendish-square—I left my house in care of my housemaid, and went to Tunbridge Wells—I cannot swear to this being my property.

[trialAccount t18440408-1256]
1256. FREDERICK SHAW was again indicted for feloniously receiving, on the 9th of June, 2 gas branches, value 3 l. 15 s.; 1 gas standard and burner, value 12 s.; and 2 lamp-burners, value 1 l. 10 s., the goods of James Slater, well knowing them to have been stolen.
JAMES SLATER . I am a gas-fitter and brass-manufacturer, and live in Denmark-street About the 8th or 9th of June I lost from 20, to 22 l. worth of property, which was kept in a store-room at the top of the house—the two gas branches, two burners, and these two gas-pipes, are part of the property I lost—they were worth 2 l. or 2 l. 10 s.
WILLIAM POCOCK ( police-constable F 81.) I searched the prisoner's premises on the 1st of March, and found this property under the drawers of the press bedstead—he did not say anything about it—I found 112 brushes under the bed in the drawer, and in a paper parcel between the bed and the wall, two balls of string and twenty-eight brushes.
WILLIAM WEST . I searched the prisoner's premises, and found twentytwo brushes in a drawer, and this bag—I did not say anything to him about this property.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Do you know the prisoner is married? A. Yes.
MR. BALLANTINE called
HENRY DOWELL . Q. Did you receive any communication from James Shaw which could lead you to believe that these goods were stolen? A. No—I left no charge with them—James Shaw and I went and had something to drink.
COURT. Q. How did you carry these brass articles? A. They were tied in two aprons, with the brass pipes—I did not carry any other bag but this one, in which the brushes were..
NOT GUILTY .
Fifth Jury, before Edward Bullock, Esq
[trialAccount t18440408-1257]
1257. MARY JOHNSON was indicted for stealing 10 yards of printed cotton, value 7th the goods of Thomas Cockett.
WILLIAM BOYCE . I am in the service of Thomas Cockett, a linen-draper, in Holborn. About a quarter to twelve o'clock, on the 12th of April, the prisoner came in the shop, and asked for some printed dresses—she was shown a great many—I beard what she said, and saw she declined purchasing some things—I saw her purchase four yards of lining—she was then about the centre of the shop—I was directly opposite her on the other side—I saw the young man measure the lining off for her—it came to 1 s. 2 d.—she tendered a half-crown, but previous to that I had observed a projection under her cloak, and spoke to one of our apprentices, who told my employer—when she had received the parcel, and was about leaving, my employer went up to her, lifted her mantle, and taxed her with having some articles which were stolen, and he took from her these ten yards of cotton print—he asked what could induce her to do it—she said, "I wish to compromise the matter, and will pay you anything you think proper to set upon the dress"—the policeman was sent for, and she was given into custody—this is the dress—I saw it taken from under her shawl—it is my employer's.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Has Mr. Cockett any partner? A. No—the young man who served the prisoner is not here—I presume he showed her from sixty to a hundred dresses—she did not purchase any dress—I think it was not threatening for rain at the time—it rained that evening, but it was some hours afterwards—the article the prisoner has on now is what my master lifted up—it is what is called a cardinal; it comes down to her knees—she had no shawl on over that—she had a little shawl handkerchief in a small parcel, wrapped up—she gave it to the young man to enclose in her parcel, and he did so—I do not know what she said, as I was not present—I saw the young man fold up the shawl in the parcel with the lining, and it is here now—they were both folded in one piece of paper—I and my employer took this dress from the prisoner—it was just as it is now, as we keep it in the shop, not wrapped in any paper—she appeared very much flurried indeed when she was charged with having stolen this—her observation was, she did not know what tempted her to do it, and she offered to pay any sum—she said she would pay any sum, rather than go through the disgrace of having it known that she was charged with robbery.
THOMAS SIMWELL ( policeman.) About a quarter to twelve o'clock yesterday I was called to Mr. Cockett's shop, in Holborn—I received the prisoner
on a charge of stealing a cotton dress—she said she was willing to pay for it if Mr. Cockett would allow her.
Cross-examined. Q. She seemed very much flurried? A. Yes—she was searched, and, I believe, about 30 s. was found on her.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 28.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury,— Confined
Six Months.
