[trialAccount t18440408-1266]
1266. JAMES BOYD was indicted for embezzlement.
JONATHAN IRONMONGER . I am a timepiece-stand maker, and live in St, John-street, Clerkenwell; the prisoner was in my employ about a week. I sent him to Sampson to receive some money—: in consequence of something which was told me, I asked if he had received the money—he said he had received 11 s.—I sent him to pawn an article at the shop of Mr. Noyes for 1 s. 6 d.—he was to pay out of the money 8 d. to a person for two dials, 2 1/2 d. for grinding glass, and 4 1/2 d. for a small vice.
LAZARUS SAMPSON . I live in Houndsditch—the prisoner came to me—I paid him 12 s. on account of his master.
Prisoner. He did not; be told me to receipt the bill; I did so; he took it, and put down half a sovereign and a shilling; he did not give me a farthing more. I went with my master the next day to the shop. Witness. I am sure I gave him a half-sovereign and 2 s.—I am positive I did not make any mistake—it was about nine, or half-past eight o'clock.
JOHN SPENCER . I live in Wilderness-row. On 26th of March the prisoner pawned a vice for his master, for 1 s. 6 d.
JOSEPH PRIOR ( police-constable G 65.) I took the prisoner.
MART ANN IRONMONGER . The prisoner paid me a half-sovereign and 1 s. from Lazarus Sampson—he said he went to Mr. Sampson, who said it was too much money—it was only 11 s.—he made him receipt the bill first, and then gave him 11 s.—he told me, that at once—he went to Mr. Lazarus with his master.
Prisoner's Defence. I had the 1 s. 6 d. in my side-pocket, and was looking at some countrymen; I went to feel for my money; I lost it, and was afraid to go back.
JONATHAN IRONMONGER re-examined. The prisoner never came back—he was to have brought a vice from Mr. Wray's—he made no difficulty about going back to Mr. Sampson's—he said he never got but 11 s. and that Mr. Sampson claimed a reduction—I sent him on Tuesday night, and the next day I went down to Mr. Sampson—I did nothing in it after that—I bought perhaps there might be some mistake—Mr. Sampson has not shown me the receipt.
LAZARUS SAMPSON re-examined. I have not brought the receipt.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18440408-1267]
1267. GEORGE RITTMAN was indicted for breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Elizabeth Todd, on the 1st of March, at Willesden, and stealing therein, 3 candlesticks, value 3 s.; 3 spoons, 12 s.; 1 clock, 5 s.; and 1 comb, 2 d.; her goods; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
ELIZABETH TODD . I am a widow, and live at Kensall-green—the prisoner is my grandson—he lost his mother when he was about three years old—I and my late husband brought him up—he married about two years ago, and ceased to live with me—he was brought up as a carpenter, and I allowed him to occupy an outhouse of mine as a workshop—before this matter happened, he used sometimes to come pretty often, and sometimes stay away a long while—a young man and his sister lodged in my front parlour, and my servant occupied the kitchen—on the night of 29th of Feb. I retired to bed, with Snell, about nine o'clock, or a little before—I locked the kitchendoor, and took the key up stairs—I had used a comb before I went to bed, and put it into a table-drawer in the kitchen—there were three silver teaspoons, three brass candlesticks, a Dutch clock, and a comb, safe when I went to bed—next morning Snell came up stairs—I went down stairs, and found the place had been broken open, and these things gone—there is a shutter to the kitchen window—it was made for a room up stairs, and the prisoner had altered it for the kitchen window, four or five years back—he would know the shutter very well—I informed the police—two or three days after, the prisoner came, about eight in the evening—I was very much alarmed, and told him I felt very ill—I asked him to go for a doctor—I said that for the purpose of sending for a policeman—after he was gone, I sent for a policeman, who came directly—the prisoner returned—he was searched, and this comb found—I have had it five or six years—it is the one I had safe on the
night of 29th of Feb.—I said, "The man who took this comb most have robbed me; now I know who it was robbed me"—I also saw a chisel taken out of the prisoner's pocket.
Cross-examined by MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. How old are you? A. Seventy-two—the prisoner married before my husband's death—I did not very much ap-prove of his marriage—when I first gave him in charge, I said it was for annoy-ing me, but they said that was not sufficient, and after the comb was produced I said it was for robbery—my servant locked the kitchen door, about nine o'clock, and the window was fastened—there were no drawers in the kitchen—there were tables there, and chairs, and a sofa—there were some knives and forks, no table spoons—I had seven silver tea spoons in all, but the others were up stairs in the bed-room—I only kept three down stairs—we always count the tea spoons—we counted them after tea that night—we had three then—only I and the servant took tea—the brass candlesticks and the clock were on the mantel-piece—I wished the prisoner to go for a doctor with the intention of his going away—he asked me if I would let him stop there all night—I said no I could not, I was too much frightened—I said nothing about the robbery to any one, before the comb was produced—Mrs. Henry is my niece—she has come down lately for a day or two and staid a night—I have no other relative besides her and the prisoner, that see me often—I am sure I used this comb about nine o'clock that night—Snell mostly combed my hair.
MR. DOANE. Q. Is the prisoner an illegitimate relation of yours? A. Yes—Mrs. Henry appeared before the Magistrate on another concern—I gave information to the police about this robbery the third day after it occurred—I had not said a word about it to the prisoner till the comb was found—I did not mention it to the police before the prisoner came to stop that night.
SARAH SNELL . I am Mrs. Todd's servant I have lived with her three months—on the night of the 29th of Feb. I was with her in the kitchen—we went to bed between eight and nine o'clock—I locked the door, and gave the key to Mrs. Todd—it was my usual custom—I slept with Mrs. Todd that night, and got the key from her—when I came down to the kitchen, about seven o'clock, I unlocked the door—there was some resistance from something placed against it inside—I found the shutter down and the window open—when I went to bed it was fastened as it usually was—the window was down, the shutter shut, and bolted with two little brass bolts—I did not observe any marks of violence, except that the two pieces of wood that supported the upper sash were taken out from outside—we had three tea spoons in use down stairs—we had used two of them the night before, and the third was in the mustard-pot in the cupboard—a Dutch clock was gone in the morning before I had combed Mrs. Todd's hair, not the night before—I saw it safe in the morning, and put it into the table drawer with the brush—I found the comb was gone—the prisoner had not been there that day—there had been no one there that day that I know of that would have taken the comb—we had no visitors.
Cross-examined. Q. You had a lodger? A. Yes—I believe Mrs. Todd used the comb that evening—I cannot say whether I saw her or not—I was in the kitchen with her some time before, getting coals and things, but she might do many things that I did not see—the window looks into the front garden—I saw the two pieces of wood standing outside the window—I only saw that two nails had been drawn—I cannot pretend to say this is the comb—it was like it—I cannot say that I know it, but my eyes are bad.
THOMAS HICKMAN ( police-constable G 204.) On the evening of the 7th of March I was sent for to Mrs. Todd's—it is in the parish of Willesden—I found the prisoner there—I asked Mrs. Todd what she charged him with—she
said coming to her house, annoying her, and putting her in bodily fear—I searched the prisoner—I saw him put his hands behind him—I proceeded with the search, and found this comb and chisel, and two or three other things on him—the comb was placed on the table—Mrs. Todd said, "It is my comb, and now I know who robbed me"—that was all she said—she gave him into custody—the chisel I found on him was bent—I afterwards examined the window and the shutter—I had these two pieces of wood that supported the sash given to me by Mrs. Snell—I found they fitted into the frame of the window to support the upper sash—I think Mrs. Snell gave me the pieces of wood on the Saturday afternoon, and then I observed some marks on them—I had kept the chisel in my possession from the time I found it—upon comparing the chisel with the marks on the wood I found they corresponded—I also found some marks on the window sash which corresponds with this chisel—I should think it would require some force to press these supporters out, and such as would bend this chisel—it is bent as if it had been used for something of the kind—I noticed that when the shutter was up the bolt was a little above the lower sash—if a person was endeavouring to effect an entrance from outside I should think the lower sash must be lowered in order to get at the bolts—they being above the lower sash, it would not be necessary to open the lower sash to do it—I should not think that the bolts are visible from the outside—shortly after the comb and chisel were found the prisoner asked me to give him the comb—Mrs. Todd had made the remark that it was hers before that—I said to the prisoner, "You hear what Mrs. Todd charges you with?"—he said, "I merely came to ask her"—Mrs. Todd interrupted him by saying he used her cruelly—I asked where he lived—he said somewhere in Long-acre—I asked where—he said he should not tell me.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you get this piece of wood after you had the chisel? A. Yes—I showed these marks on the piece of wood to Snell when she gave it me—these shutters fitted very close—there might have been a space of a sixteenth of an inch, but very trifling.
COURT. Q. When was your attention first called to the marks on the pieces of wood? A. I think on the Saturday morning I discovered the marks—I did not apply the chisel till the afternoon—the chisel was then at the station—I made one fresh mark at the police office—this is it.
SARAH SNELL re-examined. From the time I found these pieces of wood till I gave them to the policeman I made no mark on them.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you ever seen the prisoner use the comb? A. Yes, when he has come to the house.
COURT. Q. Had he any means of getting this comb? A. Not that I know of—I had been in and out that day on errands.
ELIZABETH TODD re-examined. The prisoner had not been at my house the day before this happened—I had not left my house that day, and he did not come.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you been up stairs in your bed-room that day? A. I do not think I had—I usually sit in the kitchen.
THOMAS HICKMAN ( police-constable 204.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Cole the solicitor.
JAMES HAWKINS . I am clerk to Mr. Cole, the solicitor for this prosecution—I obtained this certificate from Mr. Clark's office—( read.)
ELIZABETH TODD . I was present when the prisoner was tried in this Court in Oct. 1838—I was called as a witness on his behalf—he is the same person.
GUILTY . Aged 27.— Transported for Ten Years.

1269. WILLIAM PERRY was indicted for stealing 12 boxes of Dr. Freeman's Never-failing Ointment for the Itch, value 9 s. 6 d.; 12 other boxes of ointment, 9 s. 6 d.; 3 boxes of Scots' pills, 2 s. 5 d.; 2 pots of honey-cream, 2 s.; 2 gally-pots, 6 d.; 6 bottles of Medicamentum Gratia Probatum, 3 s.; and 6 bottles of Dutch drops, 3 s.; the goods of William Henry Sutton and another, his masters; and BENJAMIN EGLINTON and PETER JORDAN for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen; to which
PERRY pleaded GUILTY . Aged 40.—( See page 984.)
MESSRS. CLAEKSON and DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
CHARLES THAIN ( City police-constable No. 19) I know the three prisoners. Ferry lives in George-street, Shoreditch, Eglinton keeps a chandler's shop in Blossom-street, Norton-falgate, Jordan lives in Staralley, Fenchurch-street—I have seen Jordan and Eglinton together frequently—about a quarter past one o'clock, on the 11th of March, I saw Jordan leave his house in Star-alley, and go up White Lion-street, Norton-falgate—Eglinton was standing at the corner of White Lion-street—Jordan passed by him without taking the least notice of him—I had seen them together before that day, at Eglinton's house—Jordan turned up Blossom-street and went into Eglinton's house—at the time Jordan passed Eglinton he pointed his hand along White Lion-street, and Perry was crossing from the City of London Theatre—I should say he could see Eglinton when he was crossing—Eglinton went into a public-house, and Perry directly after him—that was all I saw that day—about one o'clock, on the 19th of March, I saw Perry leave Bow Church-yard, where the pro-secutor's warehouse is—I followed him to the Black Dog in Long-alley, Moorfields—he went in at a sort of private entrance they call the "bottle department"—he remained there nearly five minutes, and came out with Eglinton—they parted in Crown-street, and Perry went along Long-alley—on the 23rd I saw Perry leave Bow Church-yard, about twenty-five minutes past eight o'clock in the morning—I followed him down Bow-lane—he went into a coffeeshop in Great St. Thomas the Apostle—he afterwards came out and went down Garlick-hill—I continued having my eye on the coffee-shop in Great St. Thomas the Apostle—I saw Eglinton come out of the same coffee­house and go towards his house—I went to the Plough, in Plough-yard, Shore-ditch—after I had been there a little while Perry came in and went into the tap-room—she remained in a few minutes, and then Eglinton came out, and then Perry—they parted about the door-way—I then went after Eglinton and took him—I told him I was a City of London policeman, I should take him for receiving stolen property belonging to Mr. Sutton, of Bow Church­yard, from Perry the porter to the firm—he said, "I never received any stolen property that I am aware of, or yet to my knowledge"—I searched the house, No. 9, Star-alley, Fenchurch-street (I had seen Jordan go in and come out of that house on several occasions) I found three boxes of Scots' pills with this writing on them as it is now, "William Sutton and Son, late Dicey and Son, Bow Church-yard."
Cross-examined by MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. You saw three persons together on the 11th of March? A. Not together, but in the same street—I had been watching for five or ten minutes—I had been watching on other occasions—when Eglinton came out of the coffee-shop on the 23rd, he went towards Perry's house and his own house—they are both in the same direction.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Was Jordan with you when you went to No. 9, Star-alley, and found the Scots' pills? A. No; I found them in a long hasp-box in Jordan's bed-room, as I was told.
SAMUEL COOMBS ( City police-constable. No, 36.) I was employed with Thain on the 23rd of March—I saw Perry at the Black Dog, Long-alley, Moor fields—after he left I went to Sutton's, and found him there—I took him in charge for stealing property from his employer, Mr. Sutton—he said, "Me? I never took anything"—as we were going to the station, he asked who gave the information—I afterwards searched his house, and found some pots of Urmit's honey-cream, and six bottles of Dutch-drops.
JAMES ALLAN SHARWOOD . chemist and druggist, Bishopsgate-street. Jordan has been a chemist and druggist, and I believe he now is an agent, and carries things about in the trade—in January last he brought me a paper, and said he could supply me with these, or anyother patent medicines, at considerably less than the trade price—I believe this paper to be in Jordan's handwriting—the first thing he offered me was some of Simcoe's essence of linseed—having made a communication to the trade, I saw Mr. Sutton, and in consequence of what passed, I gave orders to Jor­dan, first for a dozen of Rowland's Macassar oil—I gave 9 s. for half a dozen—the trade price is 24 s. a dozen—I purchased a dozen of Simcoe's essence of linseed for 8 s.; the trade price is 9 s. 6 d.; and on the 6th of March I purchased a dozen of Scots' pills; and on the 19th half a dozen of Freeman's ointment—the price is 13 s. 6 d. retail; 10 s. 6 d. is charged by the house, with a discount of five per cent., or seven and a half per cent, according to the term of credit—I had refused to purchase it on the Saturday previous—after that I had an interview with Sutton and Son, and, in consequence of that, I pur­chased them on the 19th, and gave Jordan an order for a dozen more—he brought them about a quarter-past twelve o'clock in the day on the 20th—I paid him 8 s. for them—I ordered another dozen—these are the dozen I bought on the 20th—they are marked by me on the outside, "Paid 8 s.," and the figures "20 | 3 | 44."
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. How long have you known Jordan? A. I have personally known him within the last six months—there was not the least concealment in the transactions—I have often seen catalogues of druggists' sales—they do not generally contain patent medicines—Jordan has always borne a good character in the trade.
Cross-examined by MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. Has Eglinton bought things at your shop? A. I have seen him there, but have never supplied him with anything myself.
WILLIAM COLLINS . I am warehouseman to William Henry Sutton and Son. In consequence of directions from the firm, on Sunday, the 17th of March, I marked thirteen dozen and ten boxes of Freeman's ointment for the itch—at eleven o'clock on Wednesday morning, the 20th, I missed a dozen—they were safe at nine o'clock—Perry was at the warehouse between nine and eleven that day, he went up and down to the cellar in the course of that time, and I watched him go out—these Dutch drops and honey-cream, bear no private mark, but my employers are the only vendors of the cream—I never knew them to sell anything retail—these boxes of Scots' pills have the name of "Sutton and Co." on the wrapper—the articles mentioned by Mr. Sharwood are such as my masters dealt in.
Cross-examined by MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. Do you know Eglinton? A. I have known him by sight—I know that he has been a chemist and druggist by trade.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. What is the value of that dozen of
ointment? A. 9 s. 6 d.—it is an article generally sold—they are kept by other town houses.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Are you able to state that these dozen boxes of ointment formed part of the thirteen dozen and ten that you marked on the 17th of March? A. I can, distinctly.
GEORGE JONES . I am managing clerk to the prosecutor—the three boxes of Scott's pills are worth 2 s. 5 d., the half-dozen Dutch drops 3 s. 3 d., and the pots of cream 2 s. 5 d.
JAMES GELLATLY ( City police-constable, No. 7), gave the same evidence as in page 984.
EGLINTON and JORDAN — NOT GUILTY .
Sixth Jury before Edward Bullock, Esq.

1271. JOSEPH BARNARD LAFLEUR was indicted for stealing 1 watch, value 20 l.; 1 watch-chain, 20 s.; and 1 seal, 5 s.; the goods of Jules Joseph Perrot.
(The prisoner being a foreigner had the evidence interpreted to him.)
JULES JOSEPH PARROT ( through an interpreter.) I live in Lower James-street, Golden-square—the prisoner has been in the habit of coming to me to beg for money—about a quarter before two o'clock on the 1st of April I had a gold watch, a chain, and key on the chimney-piece in the room—the prisoner came and begged for a pair of trowsers and a waistcoat—I went to my bed-room to fetch them, and left him is the parlour alone—I came back in about half a minute and found him close to the chimney—I gave him the trowsers and the waistcoat, and he went away—when I dressed I went to the chimney-piece and my watch was gone—there had been no one else there, and I saw the watch safe about five minutes before he came.
Prisoner. He could see in the second room where I was standing. Witness. No, I had to go in a closet to get the clothes—I am a ballet master.
Prisoner's Defence. The policeman said Mr. Perrot wanted me; I said I would call on him to-morrow; I went with the policeman to the house, and not finding him at home I said I would return at ten o'clock to-morrow; the policeman followed me and arrested me.
NOT GUILTY .

1273. JAMES MARKS was indicted for stealing 2 firkins, value 3 s.; and 561b. weight of fat, 3 s.; the goods of James Ray Elliott, his master.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
JAMES RAT ELLIOTT . oilman, Edgware-road. The prisoner was in my employ for about six months—on the 16th of March, from information from my apprentice, I sent for the officer and gave the prisoner in custody for robbing me—he said, "Me rob you; this is all through you, Dusty meaning Mead, another servant—I went with the officer and Mead to No. 6, Little Earl-street—Mead pointed out the closet in the front room—I there found two firkins and a cloth, and in the cloth was a small piece of fat—the large firkin I knew to be mine, and the other I believe to be mine.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Do you mean to swear you had either of these firkins in your possession? A. Yes—I know the large one by its being red inside and the name of M'Adam on the bottom—this firkin was sent to M'Adam, was returned to Pilcher, and then sent to me from them with putty, and I had in my shop a firkin marked like this—I do not mean to swear to that other one, not marked with a name or a mark—firkins have no mark on them, only the weight—I never saw a name on a firkin before in my life—the name is put on this firkin in ink—this is it—it is a direction to him, not as a mark of ownership—there are no other marks on it, except the lead that is contained inside—I cannot say exactly how long before I had seen this firkin safe on my premises—I have seen it within six months—I never heard before that the prisoner had threatened one of the persons in my employ to make a charge against him to me for ill-using a horse, but for not cleaning him—I have brought both the firkins here—I found them in a closet at the back of a room in Little Earl-street, Marylebone—I know the prisoner lodged there—I did not see him there—I took Mead to point out the house, and I also found some work which I had given to the prisoner for his wife to do—the larger firkin stood in front of my shop, and the smaller one at the back—the prisoner was principally employed in the factory, but he was employed in the shop sometimes.
MB. CLARKSON . Q. Had the prisoner access to the place where these firkins were? A. Yes, he was there most of his time—I have lost a great quantity of fat.
JOHN MEAD . I am in the prosecutor's employ. About two months ago there was a barrow outside my master's shop—about a quarter to ten o'clock at night I was sent to take it down to the factory—as I went I trod on a bundle of fat outside the factory-gate, about the middle of the court—I put my hand down to the bundle, and found it was fat—I took the barrow into the factory—after the prisoner had shut up that night, I saw him come down the court and take up the bundle of fat—he put it on his shoulder, and I followed him with it to a little court facing his house, in Little Earl-street—I then went home—I knew he lodged in the front room on the first floor, at No. 6, Little Earl-street—I think there was about half a hundred weight of fat in the bundle—the prisoner was at the time employed in the factory in melting—there was fat in the factory.
Cross-examined. Q. Why did you not mention this to your master? A. I mentioned it to one of the apprentices, who asked me what it was about, and what I had seen the prisoner do, and what I had to say against Marks—I had not said anything against him to anybody then, but the apprentice knew it, because he was in the factory one Saturday afternoon, about a fortnight after, when Marks blowed me up, and said the horse was not half dressed; and I said if he did not mind, I would say something to stop his mouth—I did not see any firkins.
MB. CLARKSON . Q. You did not speak of it for a fortnight? A. No, not till he blowed me up—I was afraid be would knock me about.
CHARLES COWDERY . I am apprentice to the prosecutor. I was in prison for three weeks on a charge of misconduct—I saw the prisoner come into the factory with the horse, and he said to Mead, "You have not half cleaned the horse, I shall inform your master"—Mead said, "If you say anything about me, I shall say something about you"—I was not with my master when the firkins were found—I know the smaller firkin—it is my master's—I saw a cloth in the firkin, and about a quarter of a pound of fat in it.
Cross-examined. Q. You have been in prison? A. Yes, for abusing my
mistress—I have been twice in prison—the prisoner hat found fault with me, but not very often—I have heard him find fault with Mead—he has hit him.
GEORGE BISHOP ( police-constable D 30.) On the 16th of March the prosecutor charged the prisoner with robbing him—he said, "Me rob you!"—I told him he must go with me on a charge of stealing a quantity of fat—he said he knew nothing about it—he wanted to go back, and appeared to wish to speak to the prosecutor—I took him to the station—Mead was there, and the prisoner said to him, "This is all through you, dusty"—I went with the prosecutor to the prisoner's lodging, and found two firkins, and this apron, with this fat in it—the," prisoner said the firkins and the apron were his own—on the way to the police-court, he said he hoped Mr. Elliott would be as lenient as he could, or words to that effect.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18440408-1274]

1275. ISAAC LEVY was indicted for stealing 1 bag, value 1 d.; 6 half-crowns, and 4 shillings; the property of David Andrew Barnard, from the person of Rebecca Barnard; and that he had been before convicted of felony. (The prisoner being deaf and dumb, had the evidence communicated by signs.)
REBECCA BARNARD . I am the Wife of David Andrew Barnard, a copperplate printer. On the 2nd of March, about seven o'clock in the evening, I was walking on Finsbury-pavement, and a female felt my pocket—I turned, and saw her—I walked slowly on purpose for her to pass me, but she did not—I went on till I came to a picture shop—I stopped there a minute or two—I saw the prisoner, another man taller than him, and the woman who had touched me before—they were till three conversing together—I walked on about a dozen doors, and then I saw my gown move in front, with a sort of waving motion—I turned, and saw the prisoner with my gown tail in one hand, and my bag in his other hand—my bag had been in my pocket not a moment before—it had six half-crowns in it, and four shillings—the prisoner ran across the road—I called, "Stop thief," and saw him stopped in about a minute or a minute and a half—I did not lose sight of him.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. What time was this? A. About seven o'clock at night—I have never seen my bag since—a gentleman's servant stopped him first, but he did not hold him a second—I had never seen the prisoner till I was at the picture-shop—not a soul passed me at the time—I did not perceive any persons coming the other way—I was on the left hand side, going towards the City-road, near to Chiswell-street—I live in Pool-street, New North-road.
WM. GRIGG WATERS . I am a shoemaker. On the evening of the 2nd of March I was going up South-street, and heard a cry of "Stop thief"—I saw the prisoner running—a coachman stopped him, but he did not hold him a second—the prisoner knocked him down, and ran on again—I stopped him—he got hold of me by the throat, and tried to trip me up—a young woman came up, she went round me and said, "Let the poor deaf and dumb boy go"—I did not make signs to the prisoner, he made signs to me, and I understood him—I did not see the young woman make any signs.
Cross-examined. Q. Did not she make signs to the prisoner? A. No—a policeman came up and took him—when we were in the station I made
signs to him to know if he was a Jew—I spelt the word "Jew" with my fingers—he nodded his head first, and shook it afterwards—I am a shoemaker—I have made shoes for Mr. Dawson in the Old Jewry, for Mr. Cannon, and other persons.
SAMUEL CHIVERS ( police-constable G 201.) I took the prisoner.
JURY to REBECCA BARNARD. Q. Have you a shadow of doubt on your mind about the identity of the prisoner's person? A. No, none.
COURT. Q. Have you any doubt that he is the person who had your gown in one hand, and the bag in the other? A. I have not the least doubt of it—I am confident he had the bag in his hand—I did not hear the three persons speaking together—the female stood against the window, the prisoner in the middle, and the taller man outside.
GEORGE WARDELL ( City police-constable 325.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's conviction, which I got at Mr. Clark's office—( read)—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Transported for Ten Years.
NEW COURT.— Tuesday, April 16 th, 1844.
Sixth Jury, before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18440408-1276]
1276. WILLIAM CAPE was indicted for embezzlement, MR. PARRY conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN STEGHALL . I live in Southampton-street, Bloomsbury. On the 25th of Oct. the prisoner called on me for money for the proprietors of the "Medical Times"—I paid him 1 l. for them—he signed this receipt in my presence.
MICHAEL JAMES COOK . I am advertisement-agent to the "Medical Times" in Princes-street, Lincoln's-inn-fields. On the 17th of Feb. I paid the prisoner 8 s. for his employers, Messrs. Healey and Withers, at their office—I took no receipt—in the evening the prisoner told me not to mention it to Mr. Healey that he had not entered it in his books, as he was short of cash.
GEORGE WITHERS . I am one of the proprietors of the "Medical Times." The prisoner was our clerk from Oct. 1842—he was discharged on Saturday, the 17 th of March this year—he had authority to receive money on our account—it was his duty to enter the sums immediately in this book, and to account for them to Mr. Healey or myself—on the 25th of October, 1842, there is no entry in the book of 20 s. received from John Steghall, nor on any other day—he has not accounted to me for any such sum—there is no entry on the 17th of February, 1843, of 8 s., received from James Cook, nor on any other day—he has not accounted to me for that.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. I suppose you only judge by the book? A. Yes, by balancing the book—it is not entered at all in the book, which is generally balanced and the money paid every two or three days—there could be no mistake of 1 l. in six months—it was his duty if he did not, to pay it to me or Mr. Healey, to place it in the till—there are other persons besides him in my employ who receive money, in the prisoner's absence, and show their account to the prisoner when he returns, and put the money in the till—I have no recollection of the prisoner having made over money to me or Mr. Healey, and I have forgotten to enter it, and been told of it afterwards.
MR. PARRY. Q. Was it the duty of the other persons to pay you money received by the prisoner? A. No.
THOMAS PRESS HEALEY , Esq., barrister-at-law, King's-bench-walk, Temple. I am part proprietor, with Mr. Withers, of "The Medical Times"—The prisoner was oar clerk until the 16th of March, 1844—he has not accounted to me for 1 l. from John Steghall, or 8 s. from Mr. James Cook—I discharged him on the 16th of March this year—I had not then discovered these sums—there were some other matters that I spoke to him about, and I distinctly asked if he had received any other monies that he had not-entered in the book, and he distinctly said, "No"—He explained the non entry of the 8 s. in the book by stating that it had been paid in wages by him, and debited in the book, it does not appear so debited—I discovered about the 8 s. after he was in custody, before the Magistrate—in the event of his having paid 8 s. in wages, he should have entered 8 s. less in his account—but I find this has not been the case—he has entered the full amount of wages paid.
Cross-examined. Q. What day was the money received? A. The 17th of February—on that day he made up his payments, and 2 l. 15 s. 11 d. was the sum total of the day's expenses, but there are no particular items to make up that aggregate—it is an aggregate of the two or three days preceding, as well as that day—if the 2 l. 15 s. 11 d. is cast up correctly, the 8 s. cannot be included, because each item in the book is marked, and this 8 s. would have been marked as having been received—1 l. 7 s. was paid on Saturday night to the prisoner and the boys for wages.
MR. PARRY. Q. He paid 1 l. 7 s. every week out of the till? A. Yes. This receipt book ought to contain an entry of this 8 s.—it does not contain it—if he had paid it in wages it oughtto have appeared 8 s. less in this book—he ought to have taken. 19 s. out of the till, and debited himself 1 l., 7 s.
WILLIAM WEST ( police-constable Z 106.) I took the prisoner—he said he hoped I should take him to Mr. Healey before I took him to the station, for if there was any thing deficient, if it was 50 l. or 100 l. it would be made all right.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18440408-1277]
1277. THOMAS COLLINS , was indicted for stealing 1 pocket-book, value 1 s.; and two 50 l. Bank-notes; the property of Thomas Baring and others, from the person of Joseph Thomas Martin.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
JOSEPH THOMAS MARTIN . I am clerk to Thomas Baring and others, of Bishopsgate-street. About half-past three or four o'clock in the afternoon of the 6th of April, I had been for my masters to Drummonda' at Charing-cross, I then went up Pall Mall to the London Joint Stock Bank, and came back to Bouverie's in the Haymarket—any one passing might see me go into these bankers'—as I passed the front of the National Gallery, I felt something at my pocket behind, where I bad my pocket-book, containing, among other things, two 50 l. notes, and bills of exchange to the amount of 3,000 l.—I put my hand behind, and found my pocket-book was gone—I turned sharply round, and saw the prisoner by the side of me, a few paces off—I saw my pocket-book drop from the bottom of his frock coat down his legs—I immediately seized him and the book, and accused him of picking my pocket—he denied it—I said, "You have "—he said, "I did not; I am a respectable roan "—I still kept hold of him—he said, "I am a respectable man; don't lay hold of me in this way, to be gazed at by the public; I keep my account at the London and Westminster Bank, in Oxford-street"—he proposed to have a cab, to go up there with me—I declined that—he said, "If you will not go, let me go in some shop, and I will give you my address, and I demand your card"—he said, "Do you know the consequence of collaring a respectable man in the
street?"—I said, "Yes"—we went into a bootmaker's shop at the end of the National Gallery—I told the prisoner I had no objection to go into a shop—my only object was to gain time—a man, having the appearance of a respectable man, remonstrated with me, and said I ought to go with him to the London and Westminster Bank—the prisoner and another man offered to give their address—I had no objection—the prisoner gave the name of Mr. Brown, Sherbourne-street, Alpha-road—it was written down by the person who went in with him—I came to the door to see if there was a policeman coming, and left the prisoner writing this address with his friend—a policeman came up, and I gave the prisoner into custody—I kept the book back till I got to the station, and then gave it to the officer—some bills of exchange were returned to me.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Have you been long with your employers? A. Sixteen years—mine is an inside pocket, and that is the way I usually carry my book—I know a little show near the National Gallery—I think this happened about twenty yards eastward of that—I will swear it was ten yards off—I was walking—there were not many persons walking about at the spot where I had my pocket picked—there were persons walking by there—the prisoner's coat was unbuttoned—I am quite sure of that, because when he faced me, I saw his waistcoat—I have not enquired at the London and Westminster Bank whether he had an account there—I did not go to the address the prisoner gave me—the policeman had the case, and I thought it was out of my duty to do so—the prisoner was three or four feet from me when I turned round—the book was in ray left pocket—the nearest person to me was some yards from him.
WILLIAM HOLDEN . I am in the service of the Right Hon. Mr. Grenville, of Hamilton-place, Piccadilly; I have been so eight years. At a quarter to four o'clock, on the 6th of April, I was walking near the National Gallery—I saw a pocket-book falling from the prisoner's person—he had a frock coat on—his coat was open, and the pocket-book fell from inside—the prosecutor picked it up, and accused him of picking his pocket—he said he had done no such thing, he was mistaken; that he was a respectable man, and referred to his bankers—he said it was impossible for him to do it—his hands were in his pockets—I had not seen him before.
Cross-examined. Q. Were you behind the prosecutor? A. No, before him—I never turned—I was meeting the prosecutor—I did not observe the cage of birds and beasts near the National Gallery—there was no such thing near where we were, or within twenty yards or more than that—I had not passed anything of that description to my knowledge—there were a great many persons about, but no one near me—two or three boys came up—they were the first I saw—I did not see a little boy run round the prisoner—I swear that—there was no boy within three yards of him—the prisoner was two or three paces from the prosecutor when I first saw him—I and the prosecutor have not been conversing as to the evidence in this Court—he has asked me what I saw of it, or something of that kind—I have not been describing what I saw—I mentioned how the pocket-book fell.
MR. DOANE. Q. How soon after you saw the pocket-book fall were you examined about it? A. I should think about three quarters of an hour.
JOHN BICKERSON ( police-constable A 22.) I received charge of the prisoner from Mr. Martin—he delivered to me this pocket-book, containing two 50 l. bank-notes—the prisoner said he had not committed a felony; he was a respectable man, and he could prove that, if I would go with him to his bankers close by—I took him to the station in Vine-street—I searched him, and found 4 l. 10 s. in gold on him, 2 s. 8 d. in silver, and one penny—he gave me his name, as Thomas Collins, 22, Sherbourne-street, Alpha-road—after we
had been before the Magistrate, we went there—I found there a portmanteau, a chest of drawers, seven or eight satin and silk velvet waistcoats, four or five pairs of trowsers, some of them quite new, not soiled at all, a dress coat, and an almost new short jacket, with a great many bottom on it—I at first took it to be a page's dress.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Produce it. A. I have not got it here—I did not inquire at the London and Westminster Bank. (Harriet Thompson, Church-street, Paddington; George Brown, tailor, Jermyn-street; William Fair, hatter, Union-street, Bow-road; Charles Townsend, solicitor, East-street, Walworth; James Hancock, Gunshot-wharf, Tooley-street; deposed to the prisoner's good character.)
MR. DOANE called
THOMAS HENRY THOMPSON ( police-sergeant D 4.) I have known the prisoner for four or five years, since he was quite a lad—I have frequently seen him in the street with thieves and pickpockets—I have spoken to him—I have seen him in company with persons who have been convicted—one of them, named Francis, is in Newgate now.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. How long have you been a sergeant? A. Nearly fifteen years—I have never known a charge against the prisoner, and never had him in custody—the last occasion I saw him in company with thieves, was with John Francis, in the neighbourhood of Lissongrove—I cannot mention the date—it was at Marylebone theatre—I went there to look after some one to take him—I did not watch the prisoner, but I mentioned to Mr. Morgan what company he had got in the boxes—I have seen him at many other times—I have seen him as late as two o'clock in the morning coming out of the Harp—I cannot say how often—I have seen him out till two in the morning.
GEORGE BISHOP ( police-constable D 30.) I have been a policeman nearly twelve years—I have seen the prisoner in the neighbourhood of Lisson-grove for the last twelve months, almost every day, in company with Francis, who was tried last Tuesday in the other Court.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Have you seen him late at night? A. I cannot say I have.
GUILTY . Aged 20.— Transported for Ten Years.

1278. LAURA SMITH was indicted for stealing 1 box, value 2 s.; 1 knife, 1 s.; 2 sovereigns, 1 halfsovereign, 3 crowns, 14 half-crowns, 50 shillings, and 20 sixpences; the property of John Shephard, in his dwelling-house.
MR. PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN SHEPHARD . I live at Hounslow, and keep a beer-shop—I had a box about a foot square, containing 5 l. 10 s. in silver, and 2 l. 10 s. in gold—I kept that box in a large wooden box on the landing-place, just by my bed-room door—the inner box was locked—the prisoner lodged at my house for three months—on Saturday night I put 30 s. away in the box, which made up 8 l.—it was safe then—on the Thursday I went again—the lid of the large box was open, and the other box and the money gone—the prisoner was in a state of distress the whole time she was in my place—she paid me about 3 s. for the whole time she was there—there is a lock produced, which appears to have been burnt—it is the lock of my box—I have tried it with my key, and it fits very well—this pair of hinges I believe to belong to my box—the prisoner left on Friday, 23rd—my house is open at different times of day—on a Sunday, at proper times, a person who knew the house, could get into it, and get to my box—there is a front passage, and a side one, to
accommodate my lodgers—this knife was in the box where my money was—I have had it in my possession fourteen or fifteen years.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE? Q. Have you ever used the knife? A. No, not for many years—it was fit to use—the prisoner was never in my service—I took her in out of charity some time in October—I had two men lodgers and a female servant—I put the money into the box about eleven o'clock on the Saturday night, and then went to bed—a person could get to my box without coming into my bed-room—the prisoner left on the Friday morning, between seven and eight, to go to Mr. Brett's, where she was servant—I am sure she did not go on the Wednesday—she was at Mr. Brett's four or five weeks before she left me—I had no words with her—Mr. Brett lives next door but one to me—I knew where to find her—I gave information to the police directly—I found the prisoner at Blackwater-college—females come into my house the same as they do to other houses—no girls come there, and go to a room with gentlemen—I cannot swear that they never have done so, and paid me—they have not done it to my knowledge.
MB. PAYNE . Q. Is your's a house of ill-fame? A. Not to my knowledge—I am sometimes out—I keep no beds for that purpose.
SARAH SHEPHARD , milliner, Hounslow. Between ten and eleven o'clock on Monday, the 26th of February, the prisoner came to my shop and asked if I could make her a dress and a cardinal-cloak by Friday—she brought me the materials, and I made the dress—this is it—I sold her a bonnet and a pair of stays—she paid me a sovereign, and 6 s. 6 d. in silver—she had scarcely any clothing—she said she was going to York to see her sister, that her mistress had given her leave—I asked why she had so few clothes on, it would give her cold—she said she took them off to wash—I asked where she lived—she said as kitchen-maid at the Mess at Hounslow-barracks, and the money was for wages her master had paid her.
THOMAS BRETT , tailor, Barrack-road, Heston, The prisoner was in my service about seven weeks—she left on Saturday morning, the 2nd of March—she was living with me on the 26th of February—about eight in the evening of that day I saw her making up a flannel petticoat—she told me and my wife that Betsy Murray had given her these articles, 17 yards of silk to make a dress, some black velvet to make her a cloak, and other articles—on that day she was dressed very indifferently, and when she left she had the clothes now produced on—I do not keep the Barrack Mess—Betsey Murray is an unfortunate person, and has a great deal of money, and dresses in the first style—a few days before the prisoner left I saw this knife in her possession—she said, "I have found a little box with 6 1/2 d. in it, and an old knife, by the privy, and I have put it on a shelf ( pointing to a shelf)—I went to look for the box just now—it was gone, but the knife was left "—I went to look for the box, but she said, "No, it is gone; but the boys may have the knife"—I looked at it, and said, "It is a saw; the boys shan't have this "—on the 3rd of March I found a lock on the wall that goes round the copper in my kitchen, and a long bit of iron by the side of it; but I did not take notice of it—I flung it on the dunghill—this is the lock.
Cross-examined. Q. The prisoner slept at the prosecutors during part of the time she was in your service? A. Yes; she slept at my house the last eight nights—she was the first to mention about this knife to me—I have seen loose girls occasionally going into the prosecutor's beer-shop, and sitting in the tap-room with men—I have seen Betsey Murray pass in the road—she has been pointed out to me—I believe she is an unfortunate girl—I accommodate my neighbours with the use of my copper—other persons besides my
family have access to it—about this time two soldier's wives came in to wash—they generally came in two or three times a week—they have access to my premises, and to the privy—Phillips and Eldridge use the copper, also the other women who lodge in my houses.
ELIZA PHILLIPS . I make use of Mr. Brett's copper—about half-past nine on Wednesday morning, the 20th of Feb., I was using it—I lighted the fire—I left about twelve to get dinner—when I came back after dinner I saw a small box broken up, and burning in the fire—it had just caught light—I shut the furnace-door, and went in to get some coals—the prisoner was there when I came in, and no one else but her and me—I did not put the box on the fire—when I came back with the coals the box was all in a blase—I put some coals in—the prisoner turned and looked at me—I did not speak to her as she was a loose character.
MART ELDRIDGE . I use Mr. Brett's copper for washing. On Thursday, the 29th of Feb., in clearing the furnace to make my fire, I found this lock and two pieces of hinges—I laid them down at the corner of the kitchen, and Mr. Brett's little boy laid them on the copper—I did not see him do it—I heard the boy tell his father that he laid it there.
HARRIET GIBBONS . I live next door to Mr. Shephard. About the middle of the day of the 5th of March I saw my litte boy pick up this lock by the dunghill—he gave it to me, and I gave it to Mr. Shephard.
CHARLES JECKS ( police-constable T 20.) I took the prisoner at Blackwater, in Berkshire. I told her I took her for stealing a box containing money from Mr. Shephard—she said she did not do anything of the kind, what money she had laid out for clothes she got by prostitution.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Justice Maule.
[trialAccount t18440408-1279]
1279. MARY FURLEY was indicted for the wilful murder of George Furley.
MR. PRENDERGAST conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN FAIRFIELD . I am master of Bethnal-green workhouse The prisoner was an inmate of that workhouse—she had two children—one was about three years of age, and the other one year and ten months, whose name was George—we called it George Furley—both the children were with their mother in the house—she left the workhouse finally on the 20th of March—she left by her own consent—she applied to the guardians and received 6 s.—she asked for it and received it—I heard that she had other money in her possession, but I did not know it myself—she left on a Wednesday, and had the child George Furley with her—I saw her next on the following week after her leaving the house, 1 think on the Tuesday, at the time of the inquest—the surgeon was there—I then saw the child dead, whom I had known in the workhouse by the name of George Furley—during the time the prisoner was in the workhouse she was allowed to have the care of that child—it was always with her day and night.
MARY ANN BUCK . I live in Booth-street, Spitalfields. The prisoner came to my place on Wednesday, the 20th of March, with her son, a little boy about a year and ten months old—she called him by the name of George—while she was with me she said she should drown the child and herself—she said she should drown both the children—she only staid one night at my. house—I do not remember that she said anything else particularly about that child—she beat it once—I did not observe any thing particular in her conduct—she seemed as if she was very unhappy—she left me on Thursday morning, and left the child with me all day—she came in the evening to fetch
the child—she did not appear in such good spirits when she came the last time—she said she had been treated very kindly in the workhouse.
COURT. Q. Was she sober when she was with you? A. Yes—I had known her for years—there was nothing remarkable about her—she gave the Union a very good character, and said they behaved very well to her.
MART BUTLER . I live at No. 2, Booth-street The prisoner came to me on Thursday afternoon, the 21st of March, between two and three o'clock—she had her child Charles with her—she said she would leave him with me, and she went and fetched the younger child George—she remained with me three nights—on the Sunday morning she got up and asked me to give her a drop of water to wash the baby—I gave her some—she said she was in a great deal of trouble; and addressing the baby at breakfast time she said, "This day will end it"—she went away between ten and eleven o'clock—she took the younger child with her, and left the elder one with me—she said before she would return back to the Union at Bethnal-green with her children, for the ill treatment the children had received, she would make away with herself and her two children—the child that was left with me was sore, and George had a breaking out on the face, and one on the leg, about the size of a sixpence.
CAROLINE GIBBS . I have been an inmate of Bethnal-green workhouse more than twelve months. I remember the prisoner being there—she was there when I went, and was there during the whole time I was there—I observed her young child, called George Furley, while she was there—I have not seen it since—the child was in very good health, except now and then it had a breaking out—she bad the care of it herself during the whole time—I was at the workhouse on the day she left—I did not hear her say any thing about herself or the child on that day.
EMMA WEAVER . I saw the prisoner shortly before the death of her child—she came to our house between twelve and one o'clock on the Sunday—the child George was with her—I have not seen it since—she dined with me on that day, and left about twenty minutes after eight o'clock in the evening with the child—she said many people talked but did not do, and it would be her fate and her infant's to have a watery grave—she mentioned that several times on the Sunday—she said she left the workhouse because her children were ill treated by them, and she told me she had been turned out of the workhouse.
THOMAS GARDNER . I am a lighterman. On Sunday night, the 24th of March, about nine o'clock, I was by the side of the Regent's-canal, near Mile-end-bridge—my attention was called to the back of the premises by the noise of a dog—I went there, and after waiting a minute or two I heard a moaning in the canal—I hastened to the place from whence the moaning seemed to proceed, and saw what appeared to be a bundle in the water—I supposed from the moaning it was a person in the water—I ran for a boat-hook, and pulled the supposed bundle to the side, and there found it was the prisoner—when I got her to the side and was pulling her out, she exclaimed, "Oh my child!"—I asked her where it was—she said, "In the water"—I felt down by her side, and the child was under her left arm, which was still in the water at the time—I had hold of her right am holding it up—I took the child, and laid it on the side of the canal, and then took the prisoner out—the child seemed to all appearance dead—it was quite motionless, and turned out to be dead—I ran with it to the Globe tavern—Mr. New, the surgeon, attended, and measures were taken by putting it in a warm bath to restore it to life—no time was lost, but it was all in vain—that was the child that was seen by Mr. New atthe Coroner's inquest—as soon as Mr. New pronounced the child
dead I went back again—the prisoner had then been removed into a back outhouse that we have got—some persons were with her—she appeared in great distress of mind, and complained of the ill usage of the workhouse, and said she would rather die than go back.
Q. You know the situation of the spot in the canal, could she by any means have fallen into it? A. No—to get there a person must walk a distance round a fence—they must first enter into a private yard, and go the distance of fifty yards down the yard, then go down a pair of steps, and walk along a twenty feet plank, to get to the water in that spot where I saw the prisoner—she did not tell me how she got into the water—it was Very dark.
COURT. Q. Could she not have got in on the other side of the canal? A. I should suppose from the situation she was in she could not—the canal is about sixty feet wide, and she was fourteen or sixteen feet from the wharf on the side I was—there is an upright wall there, and the water is about three feet and a half deep close to the side of the wall—the middle of the canal is the deepest, and that was about thirty feet from where she was—she could not have come there from the other side without coming through the deepest part, which is about five feet six inches—she must have been off her feet there, and she must have been off her feet where I saw her as it was about five feet deep.
---- HENRY COTTON ( policc-constable K 361.) On Sunday night, the 24th of March, I went to Mr. Gardner's house—I saw the prisoner there, and went to the station with her—she said the cause of her jumping into the water, was the ill usage.
COURT. Q. Did you ask her any thing? A. No—she said she had something very heavy on her mind, and I cautioned her that what she said I should give in evidence in Court against her.
MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Then do not tell us any thing she said after you told her that; did she say any thing before you told her that? A. Yes—she said she had jumped into the water through distress and ill-usage at the Union—Harrison saw her after that.
GEORGE HARRISON ( police-constable K 15.) I saw the prisoner at the station at Stratford—Cotton had brought her to the station—I did not hear him say any thing to her—I sat her by the fire.
COURT. Q. Did she appear sober? A. I should say she was—she did not appear to have been drinking.
SOPHIA MONTGOMERY . I was an inmate of the workhouse on the day the prisoner left, and am there now. During the time the prisoner was in the workhouse she had the care of this child George by night and by day—the child was in good health, all but a little breaking out on its face—it was well attended to—she did not in general treat the child well—it was dirty, and she used to beat it.
SOPHIA DOROTHY ROSE . I used to work for the nurse in the Infirmary at Bethnal-green workhonse. I saw the prisoner in the morning of the day she left—she had the care of the child that is dead—the child was supplied with every thing there that it could want, as far as the house allowed—the child seemed in general good health—I never heard the prisoner complain of the usage of the child, or of anything—I know that when she left the workhouse she had 14 s. besides what she had from the guardians—I saw her go before the guardians—she was not turned out—she left of her own accord.
COURT. Q. You say the child was supplied with all it wanted, as far as the house allowed? A. Yes, if it wanted anything particular, if the doctor ordered it, it would have it—it had bread and butter allowed it in the morning, and the same in the evening—the same at all the children had, and milk
and water—on meat days, which are three days in a week, it had its regular meat at well as the other children; and on other days it had bread and milk, or bread and rice—it had as much of these things as it could eat, and that was the case with all the little children—they had their allowance—that child had as much as it could eat, and sometimes it left—it was a stout child—there were sometimes twenty children in the nursery—I had one child in the workhouse, which lived to be eleven months old—it was a thriving child—it died of the measles.
SOPHIA MONTGOMERY re-examined. I have four children in the workhouse, all very well treated, as well as the house will allow.
COURT. Q. Are they allowed enough for them? A. Yes—they thrive upon it—my children get as much as they would wish—they have milk and water, and bread and butter in the morning and evening—three days and in the week they have meat, and on other days soup or rice-milk.
CAROLINE GIBBS re-examined. I have three children in the workhouse, one is four years old, one three, and one twelve months—the treat meat of those young children, and the other children in the workhouse, and the prisoner's child, was such as they ought to have—her child had four ounces of bread and half a pint of milk and water for its breakfast; and on meat days for its dinner, four ounces of meat, and three potatoes; and in the evening again, bread and butter, and half a pint of milk and water—young children do not have soup—they have rice-milk—the prisoner herself gave her child its food—it was a fine fat child.
THOMAS NEW . I am a surgeon, and live in Mile End-road. I was called on Sunday evening, the 24th of March, to this child—it appeared to have come by its death by drowning—I have no doubt at all about that—the usual means were resorted to to restore it, but in vain—I inspected the body after it was opened—it was a perfectly healthy child in every respect, and a remarkably fine child as ever I saw in my life—every part of the viscera was perfectly healthy, and every part was firm and strong—I am not a parish doctor, nor connected with any parish Union.
COURT. Q. Was there any breaking out on the child? A. There were two or three eruptive spots about it—there seemed a dislocation of the tether—that might have been for some time—the child would have limped.
Prisoner's Defenee. I went to Whitechapel workhouse in great distress; I was there upwards of four months, and had the best of treatment; from there I went to Bethnal-green; I had not been there longer than a month, before my little boy, four years old, had a very bad head; I complained to the doctor, and it went to the Infirmary, but it never got, to say, properly well; it was shaved five times, and the third time the barber was rather intoxicated, and cut some of the flesh of the child's head, and that troubled me; it was ill a long time, but it got well; but its eyes got bad, and it was nearly blind; after that the body was in a dreadful state; I spoke to the doctor about it, and thought it proper the child should have a little mutton, instead of the hard meat; and on soup days I wanted it to have a little meat, which was not allowed, and then it was up in the Infirmary again; and there it was all the time I was there, and it came out with breakings out all over its body, and I brought it out so; the other little child I put too soon on its feet, and its knee-pan gave way; I spoke to the doctor, whether it should have an iron; he said it would do more hurt than good, and he told me to bandage the leg up, and to use ointment; they talked about an over-haul, and many of the inmates said they knew of mothers and their children being turned out at a moment's notice, without a farthing; I made application to the Guardians, and got a loan of 6 s.; I left, and went about to seek for employment, but
could not get any sufficient to maintain myself; they wanted shirts made for 1 3/4 d., or 2 d. a-piece, and I could not make more than two in a day; I had two half-crowns and a shilling, which I took the greatest care of; on Wednesday I left my child with Mrs. Bock, with an intent to buy some muslin to make dress-caps for sale; I then pot my hand into my pocket, and missed my money, which was in a green silk purse; I did not know what to do, but sooner than go back again, I would endure the greatest trouble; not that I was ill-treated myself, but my dear child would, I knew, suffer as my other child did; my child was dirty, and, like other mothers, I have corrected it, but I never ill-used it, nor kept it short of food; I had a nurse-child upwards of six months; when I had it the back had grown out as large as an egg; I used to wash it, and attend it in a proper manner, and it became as fine a baby as ever was known, and if I had been a bad mother to my child, they would not have let me have that child; I did not jump into the water, but fell over a plank; if my baby had been taken from me, I know how it would have been served, and that trouble was greater than I could bear.
GUILTY , Aged 36.— Death.
[trialAccount t18440408-1280]
1280. HENRY WILLIAM ALBERT was indicted for forging and uttering an indorsement on a foreign bill of exchange, with intent to defraud James Butler M'Rae.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
JAMES BUTLER M'RAE . I carry on business as a wholesale warehouse-man in Ave-Maria-lane, Ludgate-street, in partnership with my brother John. I know the prisoner; he calls himself a wine-merchant, and does a sort of agency business—he was indebted to us—he came, on the 20th of Dec., and offered me this bill of exchange—( looking at it)—he said he would take part goods and part cash for it—I told him if he would leave the bill, and call again, we would make inquiries about it—he said it was a very good bill, a bill as good as Rothschild's, as good as the Bank of England's—I saw him again on the 22nd of Dec—we had then made inquiries, and said we would take it—we gave him part goods, and a cheque for the remainder, which was 34 l. 5 s. 11 d.—I saw him put his name on the back of the cheque when he received it.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. You have known him some years? A. About three years—we had bad dealings with him before—there is an old account standing now—I always understood him to be a German; he did not speak English very perfectly—after we were in possession of the bill, Mr. Wilkinson came to us—the prisoner brought a friend with him to talk about the bill, and I refered him to our solicitor—his friend promised to go to our solicitor the following morning—he said he could explain the matter—I accompanied Mr. Wilkinson to the prisoner's lodging, in Watling-street, one morning—I did not hear where he had removed to—I saw a servant, who said he had been gone out some minutes.
JOHANNA ROSING . I am a merchant, and have lived at the Due de Orleans, at Paris, for two years; I am a native of Bremen, and did live there; I do business in Paris for the house of Bolte and Co., at Bremen. On the 1 1th of Nov. last I bought four bills of exchange, on Hamburg, for them—on the next day I enclosed those bills in a letter, which I sealed with my seal, and addressed it to Messrs. Bolte and Co., Bremen, and, being indisposed, I sent my boy with the letter to the post-office—this was on Sunday, the 12th of Nov.—I have had communications since from Bremen on the subject of these bills—this is one of the bills ( looking at it) that I enclosed in the letter—I am very well acquainted with the houses of business in Paris, and
I have inquired; there is no such firm as Bolte and Co., at Paris—this bill is not in the same state as when I sent it; a false indorsement has been added to it, which follows my indorsement to "Bolte and Co., Bremen"—"Pay to the order of Mr. H. W. Albert, value in account. Paris, 30 Nov. 1843. Bolte and Co."—I indorsed it "In account," because I had an account with Bolte and Co.; and now this false indorsement is "Value in account," as if Bolte and Co. had some account with Mr. Albert; that is very wrong—these other two bills ( looking at them) are part of the same purchase, and have my indorsement the same as the other—here is on one of them, "H. W. Albert," and on the other, "J. H. Albert."
Cross-examined. Q. Are you agent of Bolte and Co. at Paris? A. Yes, and am well known as their agent there—I heard something of a person named Frederick—I said I should be glad to meet with him, because his named was mentioned, and I heard that the prisoner said he had received the bills from Frederick—I do not know whether Frederick is in custody in Paris.
FERDINAND ROSINO . On a Sunday in Nov. my father gave me a letter directed to Bolte and Co., Bremen—I put it into the post in the same state as when my father gave it me.
AUGUSTS THEODOR PLUMP . I am a merchant, of the firm of Bolte and Co., living at Bremen, and carrying on business there—they have no house of business or establishment at Paris, or anywhere except at Bremen—Johanna Rosing is their agent at Paris—we expected a remittance of some bills from him in Nov. last—the bills did not arrive, and, not receiving them, we drew on him on account, on the 1st of Dec., for part of the sum—this bill would have been worth about 65 l. in English money last Nov.—this indorsement of "Bolte and Co." on it is not my writing, nor the writing of either of my partners—no person has any authority to draw or to indorse bills of exchange for the firm but my two partners and myself—this indorsement of Mr. Rosing to us, "Value in account," means that he stands in account with us, and so he makes us remittances for it—it is a transaction of account, not a cash transaction—that is always the way of doing business amongst merchants—these other two bills were never received by our house, nor is there any indorsement of ours, or by authority from us, upon them—the name of "H. W. Albert" was never known to us—we never had any transaction with him—we know nothing of "J. W. Albert," which is on the other bill.
FERDINAND DAVIS BLUM . I live in St. John's Wood-road, and am clerk to Konigswater Brothers, of London. We bought this bill of exchange of the prisoner about the 12th of Dec. last—it is worth about 63 l. in English money—he asked if we would buy it of him—we had previously refused to buy a bill of him, as he was a stranger; and when he brought this be brought a person to introduce him, whom we had known in business—here is on this bill the supposed indorsement of "Bolte and Co., pay to order of H. W. Albert, value in account"—that imports that there had been transactions between the house of Bolte and Co. and the indorsee—a person in the prisoner's situation would know enough of the language and practice of bills, to be aware of what it meant—when the bill was at maturity it was not paid.
Cross-examined. Q. Are you speaking of-what the prisoner said to you, or did he speak to anybody else in the firm? A. He spoke to Mr. Konigswater—I can undertake to say that I heard all that passed—I had no other employment at that moment, and was listening to what was spoken—the name of Frederick was not mentioned to us by the prisoner when we bought this bill—I cannot say whether we saw the prisoner at any other time about this bill; but in speaking of another bill he named Frederick, and gave his address in Paris.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. He produced another bill? A. Yes, at the end of Nov., previous to the 28th, for 1616 marck 2 schillings banco—it was drawn by the same party as this bill is, and appeared to have been indorsed first to Rosini, and then by him to Bolte and Co., and by Bolte and Co. to Albert—we refused to buy that bill, because the prisoner was unknown to us—he called several times, and mentioned parties as references whom we did not know, and we refused to buy.
JOSIAH WILKINSON . I am one of the attornies for this prosecution. I am concerned for Mr. M'Rae, and also for Bolte and Co.—in consequence of what was learnt on the subject of this bill, I went several times to the prisoner's lodgings—I at last saw him the day before he was taken up—he spoke English—I told him that this bill was returned to my clients, with the statement that the indorsement, "Bolte and Co.," was a forgery—he said it was perfectly right, and he could refer me to Mr. Johnson, of Cecil-street, Strand, who would tell me it was all right, and would be paid—I went immediately to Mr. Johnson, who told me he knew nothing at all about it—I communicated that to the prisoner on the following day, and took him into custody.
Cross-examined. Q. You say he spoke English; does he not speak it very imperfectly? A. He spoke it as well as either of the witnesses we have heard to-day, in my opinion—he spoke English fluently for a foreigner—he came to my office, and I spoke to him about this bill—he referred me to Mr. Johnson, and then he left, and the next day the officer took him, near my office—he lodged near my office—I went to endeavour to see him on the day he was taken—I did not expect to see him—I did not understand, when I parted with him the day before, that I was to meet him again the next day—my belief is that I was not to meet him—he gave me a card; but I think if he had said he would meet me again I should have recollected it—I put down the name and address of Mr. Johnson—I gave directions to take the prisoner—I had been several times to his lodging, and was told he was not at home—I then went another time, and as I was knocking at the private door he ran out of the shop—I believe he ran out to avoid me—I followed, and begged him to go to my office, and when there he referred me to Mr. Johnson—I knew then that this indorsement was forged, but I thought it better to see Johnson before I gave the prisoner into custody.
Indorsements read. —"Payez a l'ordre de Monsieur H. W. Albert, valeur en compte, Paris, le 30 Novembre, 1843. BOLTE & Co, H. W. ALBERT. J. M'RAE. "
JAMES BUTLER M'RAE re-examined. My brother put his name on this bill—this is his writing. (The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 45.— Confined Two Years.
(There was another indictment against the prisoner.)
Fifth Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.

1281. WILLIAM WATTS and STEPHEN DANIELS were indicted for ripping and cutting 198lbs. weight of lead, the goods of Joseph Hoppe, with intent to steal it.—2nd COUNT, for stealing the same lead, being fixed.
THOMAS BONE . I live in Adam and Eve-court, Whitecross-street. I went to the top of the house at eight o'clock in the evening, and saw the prisoner Watts on the roof—he ran along—I saw the lead of the gutter all doubled up—there were four cuts in it, but it was not wholly cut off—it was doubled up from the coping—it was Mr. Joseph Hoppe's house.
Cross-examined by MR. CHARNOCK Q. It was not your house? A. No,
I live at No. 9, and this was next door—it is an old house—it was dark, but I had a candle in my hand.
WILLIAM HOOKER . I live at No. 13, Adam and Eve-court. At half-past eight o'clock that night I went through my trap door to the top of my house—I saw Daniels in the corner of the gutter, and asked what he did there—he said, "Pray forgive me this time, I will never come here any more"—I took him to the trap door to take him down—he insisted on going down first, and when he got down he took away the ladder and left me there—I found the lead was cut and rolled up from the coping and the tiles.
Cross-examined. Q. How many months before had you been up there? A. I may have been there about six weeks ago—I do not know whether Daniels removed the ladder by accident—he did move it, and I dropped down—the lead was entirely separated from the other.
JAMES HAYWARD ( policeman.) I saw Watts drop from a shed about seven houses from Bones' in Adam and Eve-court—he was endeavouring to conceal this knife, and dropped it behind him—I asked him where he fell from—he said, "From the top here," pointing up—he was stunned.
HENRY LEE ( policeman.) I saw the lead—it was cut off so that it could have been carried away without using a knife again—the cutting appeared to be quite fresh.
Cross-examined. Q. Can you swear whether it was cut a week or ten days before? A. It was quite fresh—to the best of my belief it was cut at that very time—it was cut away, and the coping of the wall was taken off—the lead was rolled up and entirely cut off from the other. (Watts received a good character.)
WATTS — GUILTY . Aged 17.— Confined Two Months.
DANIELS — GUILTY . Aged 17.— Confined Four Months.

1287. JOHN WHITE was indicted for stealing 9 shillings, and 1 groat, the monies of William Golding.
JOHN ROBINSON . I am errand-boy to Mr. William Golding, a chemist, in Upper Albany-street, Regent's Park—about half-past ten o'clock in the morning on the 5th of April, the prisoner came and desired my master to send a black draught and a pill, and asked how long—it would be before he sent it—my master said half an hour—the prisoner said, "Before that"—my master said he would send it as soon as he could, and he told him to send change for a half sovereign—my master gave me the draught and pill, and 9 s. 4 d.—I met the prisoner—he took the things and the change, and gave me what I supposed was a half-sovereign, but I looked and saw the feathers on the reverse side—I ran after him, and called "Stop thief"—the policeman caught
him—I have not been to No. 4, Augusta-square, where the prisoner ordered the things to go to.
Prisoner. Q. Are you sure I came to the shop and ordered them? A. Yes—I know you by your dress—I lost sight of you, but got sight of you again in Hampstead-road.
ALFRED JOSEPH JOLLEY . I am clerk to Mr. Mortlock in Oxford-street. I was in Hampstead-road on Good Friday—the prisoner passed me, and as he passed he threw a white paper over a garden wall—I got over and got it—there was nine shillings and a 4 s. piece in it—I am sure he threw it.
GEORGE CLARK ( policeman.) I took the prisoner, and have this medal from Robinson—I have been to No. 4, Augusta-square—the prisoner it not known there.
