1288. JOHN BROCKLEY was indicted for stealing 3/4 lb. of soap, value 3 d., the goods of John Matthews.
VOLTAIRE WHITE . I am sixteen yean old, and am in the service of Mr. John Matthews—he keeps a tallow-chandler's shop in King-street, Hammersmith. On the evening of the 18th of March I went on an errand, and on my return, I stopped for a minute at my master's window—I saw the prisoner in the shop—I saw him take a piece of soap from the box, and put it into his right-hand coat-pocket—I went into the shop, and stood as a customer—my mistress came in, and the prisoner told her to serve me first—she said, "He does not want serving—he is my own boy."—I said, "He has taken a piece of soap"—he took it from hit pocket, and said, "Weigh it, I meant to pay for it."
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did not he go out of the shop after you went for the policeman? A. Yes, and went over the Suspension-bridge—we went round to his house—he was not taken at his house, but on the other side of the bridge—the scale is very near the box of soap—I swear that it was not out of the scale that he took the soap—I don't know that my master had a quarrel with the prisoner, about a man be had turned out—I have not noticed him to deal at the shop before—I have been there five months—he made an attempt to get away—I believe he was coming towards the policeman, and he has been on bail ever since.
COURT. Q. Could you tell whether, when the prisoner had the soap in his hand, before he put it in his pocket, he could see you, or was aware that he Was observed? A. He did not notice me till I was about to enter the shop—he had put it partly into his pocket before I entered, and when I entered, he pushed it in in a more flurried manner—I did not examine the pocket—I do not know whether it would require any. force to get it in.
AMY MATTHEWS . I am wife of John Matthews. I left the shop on the 18th of March, for a short time, and on my return I saw the prisoner and my lad in the shop—the prisoner said, "Serve him first"—I said, "He does not require serving, he is my own boy"—my boy then said, "Take that piece of soap out of your pocket directly, sir "—the prisoner took it out, and said,
"Weigh it, I intend to pay for it"—I am rare he took it out of hit right-hand coat-pocket, and he handed it to me—I said, "If you intended to pay for it, why did you put it in your pocket?"—he said" Don't make a fuss about it, weigh it, and I will pay you for it"—I placed it on one side—I told the boy to fetch a policeman, and the prisoner followed the boy shortly after—this is the piece of soap—it is common white soap—I know it by these holes in it, which I had noticed.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you a good many pieces of soap in that box? A. Yes—it is an inferior kind, but there was no better in that box.
WILLIAM GRAY ( police-constable T 195.) I found the prisoner, over Hammersmith Suspension-bridge, at near seven o'clock that evening—I told him he must go with me to the station—he said, "For what?"—I said, "Concerning stealing a piece of soap"—he said, "I suppose it is concerning the soap the man gave me"—I found nothing but 7 3/4 d. on him.
Cross-examined. Q. Where did he live? A. In Queen-street, Hammer-smith—he was coining towards his own house, from the opposite side of the water—he surrendered here on Saturday.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18440408-1289]
1289. ELIZABETH BEST was indicted for stealing 1 parasol, value 3 s. 9 d., the goods of Frederick Tomkins.
FREDERICK TOMKINS , linendraper, Charles-street, Middlesex Hospital. On the 11th of April, between twelve and one o'clock, the prisoner came and asked for a gentleman's hat-band—I referred her to Cole—in about three minutes Cole came to me, and stated something—I went round the counter, and saw a parasol under the prisoner's shawl—I said, "I must detain you, you have some of my property"—she made no reply—she was standing near the door—in a minute the parasol slid down on the floor—she said, "Oh dear me, it has hung to my shawl"—I replied, "That Is impossible, because you have got it underneath your shawl"—it had stood between five and six yards from where the prisoner stood.
JOSEPH COLE . I am shopman to Mr. Tomkins. The prisoner came and asked for a hat-band—I served her—it came to 6 3/4 d.—she gave me half-a-crown—we were proceeding to the other end of the shop to fold up the hat-band and give her change, and about half way down the shop I observed her halt—I instantly turned round, and saw her hand on a parasol—there were a great many lying together on a paper on the counter, about half way down, the shop—they might project an inch or two, so that a person going by might brush them if they went near enough to the counter—she would not have to pass the spot close to where they were lying—there was a thoroughfare of about four feet—there was a counter on one side, and rolls of flannel the other side, and four feet space to walk—I observed her halt and put the parasol under her shawl on her left side—I did not make any observation to her—she continued her walk to the end of the shop—I communicated what I had seen to Mr. Tomkins—the prisoner bad then arrived opposite him—he went round to her, and said, "I must detain you, you have some of my property on your person "—Mr. Tomkins requested me to go after a policeman—I did so—I observed the parasol fall on the floor—I saw her place the shawl quickly over it—she had not time given her to apply for the purchase of it—she was immediately charged with taking it—she was charged with stealing it about fourteen feet from where it was lying—she stood still a minute with it under her shawl, to give time to Mr. Tomkins to come round the counter—she observed him coming, and instantly turned her head the other way—he charged her with having it—he did not say "Have you got it?"
MR. TOMKINS re-examined. She made no remark about purchasing it—she had not got her change, nor the article she purchased—the value of the parasol is 3 s. 9 d.—the counter is thirty-seven inche high, nearly an inch higher than ordinary counters.
GEORGE PIPER ( police-constable E 1.) I took the prisoner—she denied having stolen the parasol, but said it hung, to her shawl—a small inch-measure was found on her, but no money.
Prisoner's Defence. I did not steal it; in passing the counter it hung to the shawl I have on.
GUILTY .— Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutor,— Confined Three Weeks.

1293. SELINA SEARCH was indicted for stealing 1 pair of boots, value 1 s. 6 d., the goods of Joseph William Clark.
EMMA SHEPLEY . I am sixteen years old, and live in Villiers-street, Strand. On the 9th of April I was in Mr. Clark's, the pawnbroker's shop, in Long-acre, in one of the tide boxes—the prisoner was behind me—there was a looking-glass opposite—I saw the prisoner get on a box, take a pair of boots, and place them under her shawl—I told Simons—she dropped the boots, I believe—I saw him take them—there was twenty minutes between her taking them down and her being taken into custody.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Where were you standing? A. in the box—the glass is on a chimney-piece—I went to pawn something—I did not see the things drop—I heard them—I went into the shop to call the young man—I did not know the prisoner before.
JOHN FREDERICK SIMONS . I am in the service of Joseph William Clark. From information I received from Shepley, I called a constable—I told the prisoner she had stolen a pair of boots—she dropped them on the floor, from under her shawl—I picked them up, and gave them to the constable—when she was given into custody, she said, "You must be mistaken, young man"—they had hung on a line, about eight feet from the ground—the box on which it is represented she must have stepped is two feet high, and then they would be within her reach.
Cross-examined. Q. How do yon know them? A. By our private mark—( looking at them)—they were fastened to the line, swung over by a string—Shepley was about a yard and a half from the boots—a short person could see in the glass what passed—a tall person could not—there was nothing to prevent a person in the shop from seeing the woman on the box.
WILLIAM BOYCE ( police-constable F 71.) I went to the shop, and saw Simons stoop down and pick up a pair of boots—I took the prisoner into custody—she said Simons was quite mistaken.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you swear you saw the boots drop? A. I am quite certain—they could not have dropped from the string, they would not have fallen on that spot, but a yard and a half from it. (The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 32.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury and Prosecutor.— Confined Three Weeks.

1294. CHARLES ROURKE and JOHN SAUNDERS were indicted for stealing 1 coat, value 1 l.; 1 pair of gloves, 6 d.; 1 watch, 1 l.; and 1 watchstand stand, 3 s. 6 d.; the goods of John Dimock Aspland; and that Rourke had been before convicted of felony.
CHARLES DUGARD . I am a livery stable-keeper at Dalston. On the 3rd of April, about a quarter to eleven o'clock in the forenoon, I was standing at the corner of a public-house near my premises—I went to a window, which commanded a view of Mr. Aspland's place—I saw Rourke go to the side gate—Saunders stood outside—I fetched the man who had given me the information, and went back with him to mv own house—in a few minutes Rourke came out again, with a coat on, buttoned up to the top—Saunders came up to him, pulled up the collar, and smoothed down the back—I went out and met the prisoners—Rourke's hands were in his great-coat pockets—I seized him—he tried to get away, and fell—I fell with him—I held him—my companion seized Saunders—they were given into custody, and searched—while Rourke was being searched, I saw a watch drop from him—the constable took the coat off his back directly he came—this is it—( produced.)
ELIZA ALLEN . I am in the service of John Dimock Aspland, of Richmond-row, Dalston. This watch, the watch-stand and coat are my master's property—I had seen the coat in the passage, and the watch on the mantel-piece about a quarter of an hour before—they could get to the room by climbing over the wall and getting in at the kitchen door—I did not hear the kitchen door shut—it was shut after the prisoners—they came into the kitchen and through the passage.
JAMES BRANNAN ( police-constable N 69.) On the 3rd of April, I saw the prisoners together in the Kingsland-road close to the prosecutor's house—I afterwards went to Richmond-road—the prisoners were given in my charge—Rourke had then a different coat—I took it from his back—the prisoners were searched in the public-house—I saw Rourke drop a watch—I picked it up—this latch-key was found under the sleeve of Saunders's coat—the gloves were in the pocket of the coat.
Rourke's Defence. I was passing the house, and wanted to go to the water-closet; I saw the gate open, went in, and saw the coat there doubled up; I came and told the young man; he told me to put it on; 1 found the watch too.
Saunders's Defence. Rourke went down the turning and came and told me there was a coat lying there; he went back and put it on; it was all over dust and ashes.
MICHAEL COMBY ( police-constable H 138.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner Rourke's former conviction from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—I was present at the trial—he is the person described.
(Miles Costello, of No. 5, Green-bank, Tooley-street, and Mrs.——, gave Rourke a good character.)
ROURKE — GUILTY . Aged 18.— Transported for Seven Years.
SAUNDERS — GUILTY . Aged 17.— Confined for Six Months.

1295. THOMAS BROWN was indicted for stealing 1 kettle, value 4 s., and 1 copper, value 14 s.; the goods of William Gardner, and fixed to a certain building.
WILLIAM GARDNER . I am a broker, and live in Rose and Crown-court, Moorfields. On Sunday the 7th of April, between three and four o'clock, I saw my copper fixed in my washhouse—my copper tea-kettle was there safe—next morning I found the copper had been pulled down and taken away, and the kettle was missing—these are them—( produced.)
WILLIAM TILLIER ( police-constable G 28.) On Monday morning at half-past six o'clock, I was on duty in Old-street, St. Luke's, and saw the prisoner carrying a kettle—another lad was with him carrying a copper. I asked the
prisoner where he was going—he Said to No. 9, Goswell-street—I said that will not do—I took him to Bunhill-row—the prisoner threw down the copper, and the other the kettle—I pursued and took the prisoner, but the other escaped.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Was not what the prisoner said that he was going to Goswell-street? A. Yes—I asked him where—he said, No. 9—I swear I said that before the Magistrate—this is my writing—( looking at his deposition)—it is not here—he said the other met him and asked him to carry it for him into Gosling-street, and he would give him 3 d.—he afterwards said he met him in the Haymarket, Whitechapel.
MR. PAYNE called
HENRY PECK . I am employed in a wholesale Berlin warehouse in Cheapside, and carry on business for myself at Kingsland—the prisoner was in my employ, up to the time he was taken into custody, eighteen months altogether—I had given him a holiday on Easter Monday—to the best of my belief Old-street is a quarter of a mile from where the copper was taken from—he was on his way to me to ask for another day's holiday.
WILLIAM TILLIER , re-examined. I knew the other lad to be associated with thieves—he has kept out of the way—I know the prisoner by sight—I know no wrong of him.
NOT GUILTY .

1296. JOHN DRURY was indicted for stealing 1 pair of boots, value 2 s. 6 d. the goods of James Stocken.
JAMES STOCKEN . I am potman at the Craven Arms, Queen's-road, Bayswater. I missed a pair of blucher boots from the top of a beer-tray in the yard—they were safe a little before ten o'clock—I saw the prisoner in the tap-room that day after three o'clock—about six o'clock I taw the prisoner and John King together—King had got the boots—I asked the prisoner how he came by them—he said, "A man gave them to me crossing the fields."
JOHN KING . I am a potman at the Oraren Arms. I saw the prisoner in the tap-room—he had a flag basket with him—he went out—I afterwards beard Stocken had lost his boots—I met the prisoner in Queen's-road with the boots concealed under his jacket—I asked what he had got—he said, nothing belonging to me—I asked him to give me the boots—he said, "I have got no boots"—I said, "Let me look"—he unbuttoned his jacket, and took the boots out—I said, "They are not mine, they are my fellow-servant's"—he said, "B—your eyes, if you don't let go of me I will smash you"—I let go, but being surrounded with a mob of people he could not go—he was 100 or 200 yards from the Craven Arms—the boots were ten or twelve yards from the tap-room close to the bar parlour window.
Prisoner. I said if you came with me I would show you who gave them me. Witness. No, you said you would show me the man that gave you the handkerchief.
JOHN LODDER ( police-constable D 207.) I took the prisoner into custody—he was not much the worse for liquor—King delivered the boots to me—the prisoner tried to get away, but tome people came to my assistance—he tried to kick my legs.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner 11 Defence. A man in the Craven Arms bought half a gallon of beer; after I drank, I went to sleep, and never awoke till ten minutes after five o'clock; the men were then all gone out; just outside the door, I met one of them, named Peter Ashburn; he said, " Jack, if you go across the field you will find an old pair of boots in the hedge, and we will have some more beer," which I did.
JAMES STOCKEN re-examined. There were some more men in the tap-room—he was not in the tap-room after four o'clock.
JOHN KING re-examined. He told me nothing about the boots being in the ditch—he was coming away from the field, going away from the public-house.
GUILTY . Aged 25.— Confined One Month ,

1297. THOMAS KERR, SARAH ROSE , and SARAH LAMB were indicted for stealing 1 bonnet, 1 s.; 1 cap, 3 d.; 1 pair of boots, 3 d.; and 1 book, 1 s. ; the goods of Henry Kerr: and 1 pair of shoes, 6 d.; the goods of Ellen Kerr; and that Lamb had been previously convicted of felony.
JAMES WOOD ( police-constable M 227.) On the 8th of April I saw the three prisoners in East-street, Hoxton, coming from the City-road—the females had no cap or bonnet on their heads—they went to No. 13, East-road together—Kerr knocked at the door—the female prisoners then left him, and waited six or seven yards off—he was let in immediately after—I watched the women—after he had been in about a minute he came to the door, and let them in—when they had been in six or seven minutes Rose came out, and looked round to see if she could see any one—not seeing any one she went to the door again, and the other two came out, joined her, and all came away together—Lamb had a bonnet and cap on—I took another constable with me, followed, took them into custody sixty or seventy yards off, and brought them to the station—on Rose I found this bonnet, pair of boots, book, and collar, and a book, cap, and pair of shoes on Lamb—I said, "What are you going to do with the boots?"—she said she brought them from home, and was going to take them to pawn—she said she had them given her in St. Luke's workhouse, and the collar was her brother's, that she had brought from home—No. 13 is the house of Kerr's father.
Rose. The collar belonged to me; I did not mention my brother; I have do brother; he has said several times he will get me transported.
ELLEN KERR . I am the prisoner's sister. I live with my father, Henry Kerr, at No. 13, East-row, Hoxton—I do not know the female prisoners—this bonnet, cap, pair of boots, and book are my sister's—she is not here—I do not know why—on the day this happened I and my sister were gone to a tea party in the City—I returned home about ten o'clock—the persons up stairs were at home to let in my brother—all the family were out that evening—the collar belongs to my youngest brother—my brother always lives with my father—the family do not allow him to take any thing to pawn that I know of.
Kerr. Q. What do you swear to the boots by? A. Because there are no tags on them—there is no mark on them—they only appear like—I know the bonnet to be my sister's—I can confidently swear to it—my own is the same pattern—I never knew my sister allow you to pledge things.
Prisoner Kerr. This case is not prosecuted with my father's consent; she has come here to try to get me transported, if possible. My mother said, if I promised not to do any thing of the sort again, she would not do anything.
Rose's Defence. I met Kerr at the end of Pitfield-street. He asked us to go home with him: we would not: he said, "I don't care about your coming, to long as Lamb comes." I followed him. He said, "Wait at the corner till I see if any one is at home." He took us down in the kitchen, and said, "Don't make such a noise;" the bonnet was hanging up; he gave it to Lamb; he gave me the boots from under the table; he said, "My sister will not wear them." He took Lamb into the parlour, and wanted her to take the table-cloths out of the drawers; he said, he wanted to serve the old rogue out;—I ran out of the passage; he might have called a policeman if he did not want us in; he said, "Don't go out till we all go out together;" he said, "I can't get any money."
THOMAS BROWN ( City police-constable, No. 215.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner Lamb's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—I was present at the trial—she is the person.
KERR — GUILTY .* Aged 17.
ROSE — GUILTY .* Aged 17.
LAMB — GUILTY . Aged 20.
Transported for Seven Yean.

1298. ELLEN LEARY was indicted for stealing 2 half-crowns, 1 shilling, and 1 sixpence, the monies of William Verron, from his person.
WILLIAM VERRON , smith, Jubilee-place, Stepney. On the night of the 8th of April, between twelve and one, I was in a cook's shop in Whitechapel-road, and bad a basin of soup and potatoes—the prisoner was there, and asked me for one—I said she might take the whole—she followed me out, laid hold of my arm, and wanted me to give her something—she said I could go with her for 4 d.—I was there three or four minutes—she followed me out again, and put her hands inside my trowsers—she kept putting her hand in—when she had taken all she wanted, she said, "Good night," or, "Good morning," and walked off—I missed my money, followed her, saw a policeman, and told him—the money was two new half-crowns, one shilling, and a sixpence; but whether they were new or not I could not say—I said, "That is the woman"—she said, "Don't take me," then took out a bit of rag, threw out two half-crowns, and the policeman took them up—I was sober.
JANE SCOTT . I am the wife of William Scott, a police-serjeant—the prisoner was brought to Stepney station—I went to search her—she said she had nothing belonging to the man at all—I found two fourpenny pieces in her pocket—she said, "That is all I have, belonging to the man."
ROBERT BACKHOUSE ( police-constable K 37.) I went with Verron to Nelson-court, Whitechapel-road, between one and two o'clock, and found the prisoner there—the prosecutor charged her with stealing two half-crowns, one shilling, and a sixpence—she put her hand into her bosom, pulled out a white handkerchief, threw two half-crowns on the table, and said, "So help me God, that is all I took from him"—the prosecutor bad told me, they were new half-crowns—these are apparently fresh.
Prisoner's Defence. He went with me to the house, and wanted to take improper liberties with me; he gave me two half-crowns; he looked about, said he was a married man, and he was afraid his wife would find it out; he wanted the money back; I would not give it him; he went with me to the house where I first met with him, and sat by the fire; came back and gave me in charge before I could have spent the rest of the money; I never said, "So help me God, that is all I took from him."
ROBERT BACKHOUSE re-examined. I am sure she used the expression, "took from him."
GUILTY . Aged 29.— Confined Six Months.

1299. JOHN HARRIS was indicted for stealing 1 carpenter's tool, called a mortise-gauge, value 1 s. 6 d., the goods of John Wells.

On the 9th of April I saw the prisoner, and another, in front of the shop—in consequence of something a woman told me, I went after them, and came up to them in Adam's-row—they were still together I saw the person the prisoner was with, give him this mortise-gauge—I followed the prisoner—he ran—when I was close behind him, he threw the gauge under a cart—a sweep picked it up, and gave it me—I caught him in Crescent-street, Euston-square—he said, "Pray, forgive me this once"—he
was taken to the station—this is Mr. Wells's property—it was placed on a tray in front of the shop—my attention was drawn off by another party, and that enabled a person to take it.

1300. CHARLES JONES and EDWARD KELLY were indicted for stealing 1 eye-glass, value 10 s., the goods of William Wood, from the person of Sarah Wood.
SARAH WOOD . I am the wife of William Wood, who is in the Excise. We live in Lucas-street—on Monday, the 8th of April, I was in Stepney fair—I had a geld eye-glass round my neck suspended by a ribbon—I felt a tug at my ribbon, and a crash of the ribbon cutting—I turned and said to Jones, "You have got my glass"—he said, "No, I have not"—I said, "You have given it to somebody"—he said, "No"—I told the policeman—he took hold of them—Kelly said, "Here is the glass; I was just going to give it to the lady."
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. There were other persons by at the time? A. Yes—it was in a crowd—I did not observe any body push Kelly away at the time he said he was going to give it me—there was a commotion in the crowd—I did not turn round—he was in front of me—I took, hold of him—I did not see him take it.
COURT. Q. Did you hear his hand on your ribbon? A. Yes, and saw his hand moving—that was Jones—Kelly handed up the glass.
HENRY HUTTON ( police-constable K 60.) I was passing through the fair, and saw the prisoners and a third party—I saw the prosecutrix have hold of Jones by the arm—she said, "You have got my glass; give it me"—he said he had not—the prosecutrix said, "That other boy is with him"—I caught hold of Kelly—he said "Here is the glass; I was just going to give it to the lady; I have just picked it up"—I took them to the station—I found on Kelly 1 s. a box, and the glass, and a knife, and on Jones 3 s. and 1 s. 11 1/4 d. in copper, a box, tobacco-stopper, and a handkerchief—Jones said he knew nothing about it, he had just come out of a swing—the ribbon was cut—it was a stout ribbon—the knife I found was shut.
Cross-examined. Q. This was all the work of a minute? A. Yes—the third boy got away.
JONES — GUILTY . Aged 16.
KELLY — GUILTY . Aged 15.
Confined Three Months.

1301. JAMES ADAMS and FRANCIS COLSELL were indicted for stealing 1 bag, value 1 s.; half a bushel of oats, 1 s. 6 d.; and half a peck of beans, 8 d.; the goods of Robert Gamman and another, the masters of James Adams.
GEOROE MURRAY ( police-constable K 68.) On the 29th of March I observed the prisoner Colsell and a wagon in the Commercial-road—he had this bag, containing another bag with oats—he walked in a direction from the wagon—I said, "What have you got there?"—he said, "Ducks'food"—I asked where he got it from—he said, "That man driving the wagon gave it me to take home to Exmouth-street"—he called out to the prisoner Adams to stop—I went across the way with him—I said, "Do you know this man, have you given him this bag?"—he said, "Yes, I have"—I said, "You ought to know better, an old man like you robbing your employers"—he said he found the food in the loft, and was going to send it home—I asked if he had got any rabbits—he said he had not, he had ducks—I took them into
custody, and took Adams to hit master's wharf—he then said he found the food with oats in the loft—the bag that Colsell was carrying contained 28lbs. weight of oats and one nose-bag—I took a sample of oats from a bin on the premises of Messrs. Gamman, and compared them—in my judgment they are exactly alike—Mr. Gamman's name was on the wagon—I searched a place in Exmouth-court, where Adams said he lived, and found some split-beans—there was a pig and some ducks there—it was the place Colsell said he was going to carry them to.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. I believe Adams said he found the bag in the loft and took it away? A. Yes—the oats are here—I looked out the oats that I have produced as a sample.
ROBERT GAMMAN . I am in partnership with my father, Robert Gamman, coal merchant, Wapping-wall. The prisoner Adams was carman to the firm—he was sent out early in the morning before I was there—Colsell was employed by Adams to assist him—we allow him to employ who be likes to help him—the nose-bag produced is one of ours—we never allow our servants to take raw oats—I have compared these with the bulk, and believe them to be the same—I have been in the business eight years, and have been constantly in the habit of looking at these sort of things—I cannot say whether when our carman has his helper if he comes to the wharf, he might tell the other man to meet him—it generally requires two to go with the cart.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you mean to swear positively to these oats being your property? A. No—I have compared them—I believe them to be the same quality—I do not say they are the best oats—he had no business with five nose-bags full) of oats—he had four horses.
(The prisoners received good characters.)
ADAMS — GUILTY . Confined Six Weeks ,
COLSELL — NOT GUILTY .

1302. ALEXANDER CAMPBELL was indicted for feloniously receiving of a certain evil disposed person, 4 pieces of leather, value 4 s.: the goods of William Henry Palmer, well knowing the same to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
WILLIAM HENRY PALMER , boot-maker, Henry-street, Manchester-square. On 1st March I cut four pieces of leather, to make a pair of boots—I missed them—I questioned my son about them—he said he had given them to the prisoner—I have since seen the leather.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Had you promised to forgive your son, if he told you? A. Yes.
JOHN QUINLAN PALMER . I am fourteen years old. On the evening of the 1st March I was at Mrs. White's cookshop, Henrietta-street, St. James's—the prisoner was there—he asked me if I could get anything to sell—I said I could go and get four pieces of leather out of my father's drawer—he said, "Go, and get them," and he would sell them—I got them, and asked him to get as much as he could—he said the man would only give him 6 d.—he gave me the sixpence, and I gave him 2 d. out—he said he sold it to Mr. Bolls—I went to a coffee-shop, and bought a roll, a cup of coffee, and a pennyworth of butter—I did not get it at home, because ray mother would not give me any—she said there was only enough for the little children—my father said he would forgive me, if I told him the truth.
Cross-examined. Q. 1 think you have been before the Magistrate? A. That was through other boys leading me away—I had a month's imprisonment—I should not have taken the leather if I had not been hungry—I did
not know I was doing wrong—the leather was not taken out of the drawer before I met the prisoner—he was with me when I spent the money—he did not have any of the coffee—I gave him 2 d.,—I did not offer to tell my father till he spoke to me.
JOHN VORLEY . I live in John's-court. The prisoner came to me with some leather—I do not recollect when—it was a wet night—he wanted to know "whether I would buy it—I said, "No," they were of no use to me—they were not worth more than 9 d.—he took them away, came again in about ten minutes, and said, "I suppose I must have it, for they have been in the box long enough, and we want something for supper"—I went to my leather-cutter, Mr. Penton, and sold them for 1 s.—I got 3 d. on them—I considered the prisoner an honest young man—I knew him as a customer—in fact, if he had come to me, I would have given him employment—they are what they call cuttings, the fragments that are cut off chairs—you can buy it at 20 d. per lb., and I had given after the rate of 2 s.
SAMUEL CHESTER ( police-constable D 103.) I found the prisoner at the corner of Henrietta-street, St. James's-street—I told him Mr. Palmer gave him into custody—I said, "I suppose you know all about it?"—he said, "Yes, his father spoke to me about it yesterday"—I took him to the station—Mr. Vorley came to me with the leather.
WILLIAM HENRY PALMER re-examined. I saw the prisoner about this—I said, "You have been going on again with my young gentleman, I have caught you at last"—he said, "How the devil was I to know that he stole it from you? go to h—ll, and be b——."
Prisoner's Defence. It is my leather; it was all one piece before I put the knife to it; it is worth 3 s. 6 d. or 4 s. now.
NOT GUILTY .
Sixth Jury, before Edward Bullock, Esq,

1303. MARY KEENE was indicted for stealing 25 yards of linen cloth, value 15 s. 7 d. the goods of William Devas and others.
MR. HORRY conducted the Prosecution,
RICHARD SILLETT . I am porter to William Devas and Sons, warehousemen, Lawrence-lane, Cheapside. On the 22nd of March I delivered this parcel to George Garratt—the address on it is "Mr. T. Naylor, High-street, Mary le bone."
GEORGE GARRATT . I am in the service of Mr. Taylor, of Carlile-street, 3 Paddington—I drive an omnibus. On the 22nd of March I received a parcel from Sillett—this is the paper that was round it—the parcel has been untied since—I gave it to my conductor to keep it from getting wet.
WILLIAM SIOSTON . I was the conductor—I received a parcel from Garratt on the 22nd of March—I believe this to be it—I recollect the prisoner getting into the omnibus, about five minutes past eleven o'clock in the forenoon, at John-street, Oxford-street—she desired to be set down at Berkleyplace, or Berkley-street, Edgware-road—she went there, and then she went on to Burwood-place, which is a little further—she was she last passenger—I Jet her out, and as soon as I got on the omnibus again, and looked inside, I missed this parcel—I had left it inside, near the door, on the cushion—there had been persons in the omnibus when I put it in—I followed the prisoner, and found it under her arm—it was under her shawl—I saw one end of it—I said to her, "I am surprised that a person of your respectable appearance should be guilty of taking a parcel out of an omnibus"—she gave it up immediately, and told me she took it in mistake for a parcel of her own—she had
a parcel of her own when she got in, and the said the took that for her own—I said a great many robberies had taken place in omnibuses, and I felt it my duty to give her in charge.
Cross-examined by MB. CLARK SON. Q. Was it tied up as it is now? A. It has been opened, but the same paper is on it—I should judge it to be linen—she told me she had bought three dresses in Vine-street, Oxford-street, and it turned out that she had done so.
MICHAEL BRIGHT ( police-constable D 106.) I was called to take the prisoner—she said she had bought a parcel of three merino dresses in Oxford street, but she could not tell the shop—that she had a parcel when she went into the omnibus, and this parcel she must have taken in mistake—Knowles brought me this other parcel.
MR. DOANE called
EPHRAIM KNOWLES . I am in the service of Burrell and Co., in Vine-street—they are drapers—the prisoner has been a customer of my master's ever since I have been there, which is nearly five years—I remember the morning on which she was taken into custody, as she had come to our shop and bought three dresses—they were put up in this parcel—I was examined before the Magistrate on this prosecution—a person named Leland was examined and bound over, but he has not attended.
JURY. Q. Did she give any direction? A. She merely said if she did not call, we were to send the parcel, as she was going further.
NOT GUILTY .

1304. JOHN SPENCE was indicted for stealing 3 shillings, 5 sixpences, and 1 groat; the monies of Richard Reynolds, from his person.
RICHARD REYNOLDS . I live in the Commercial-road. On the 10th of April I was employed as waiter at the Prince of Wales public-house at Stepney fair—the prisoner and two or three others came around me, and pushed me about—the prisoner put his hand into my right waistcoat-pocket, and took 4 s. 10 d. in silver, which I had counted not five minutes before—I saw his hand come out of my pocket, and he passed it to somebody else—I put my hand to my pocket, and the money was gone—I immediately caught hold of him, and said, "You have robbed me "—I held him till the policeman came.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Were there not a number of persons scuffling about? A. Not all of them—there were some pushing about—the prisoner had been drinking—I am certain I was sober—the prisoner came and spoke some nonsense first, and then he and two or three others pushed me about—there were forty or fifty persons present, but not more than three or four standing up about me.
WILLIAM MARCHING ( police-constable K 379.) I took the prisoner—I found nothing on him but a knife.
GUILTY . Aged 26.— Recommended to mercy.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18440408-1305]
1305. HENRY BURGESS was indicted for stealing 1 gun, value 10 l.; 2 gun-cases, 1 l. 10 s.; 1 shot-belt, 3 s.; 1 flask, 3 s.; 1 punch, 1 s. 6 d.; 1 cleaning-rod, 3 s.; 2 screw-drivers, 1 s. 6 d.; 2 brushes, 1 s.; 1 wrench,1 s. 6 d.; and 1 gun-stock, 3 l.; the goods of Charles Eggand another: and THOMAS OLDHAM and THOMAS CONNER for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen.
CHARLES EGG . I am partner with my brother Henry; we are gunmakers, and live in Piccadilly. On the 1st of April, about ten o'clock in the morning I missed a gun, and case, and part of another gun—the gun-case had been on the rack in my. shop—I had seen them safe on Saturday the 30th of March.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARK SON. Q. Had you looked at the gun on the 30th of March? A. No, I saw the case there—I cannot rightly say when I had seen the gun in the case—perhaps ten days before.
WILLIAM JOHN BIRD . I am a broker, and live in Exmouth-street, Spafields. On the night of the 2nd of April, I saw all the prisoners—Burgess came first to my shop about half-past eight o'clock—I knew him before—he said, there was a double gun, and part of another gun to be sold—I asked whose they were, and where they were to be seen—he said, "Never mind that; if you will come with me I will shew you them "—I said I had not bad my tea, I could not go then, and said, "I don't like to go out after these things; can't you bring them to my house?"—he said, No, the landlord of the place where they were, had advanced 2 l. on them, and therefore he could not bring them to my house—I agreed to meet him opposite Day and Martin's, in Holborn, and I met him about nine o'clock—he told me to follow him—he took me to the Archers public-house, in Newton-street, Holborn, which, I believe, is kept by Oldham—I saw him behind the bar, serving—I saw Conner there, and two other persons, who have escaped—Burgess introduced me, and said I was, the person about the guns—after waiting some time, I went to the front of the bar, and stood there about two minutes—the other two men spoke to Oldham about showing me the gun—he said, "Presently, presently "—I waited about, and at last Burgess and the two men not in custody asked me to go into the tap-room—I sat there some time—at last one of the other two men told Connor to get a light—Connor got a light, pulled the tap-room door to after him, and shut me in the tap-room with Burgess and the other two men—I felt rather uneasy, and I opened the tap-room door, as if I was going out—I then saw a gun-case wrapped in a bit of serge come up through a trap-door in the passage—Conner took it up stairs—Burgess and I and the other two went up stairs to the back-room on the first floor—the gas had just been lighted—Conner brought the gun, and put it on the table—he then went and waited outside the door—I remained in the room with Burgess and the other two men—I examined the gun, and said that would do—this is it—( looking at it)—it was then wrapped up in the same piece of serge, and given to Conner—I and Burgess and the other two men walked downstairs—Burgess said, "Come on "—he and I and the other two men went to Parker-street, Drury-lane—one of the other two men tapped at the window of a room there—he said his mother was out, but he would go and get the key—he went to another house, next door but one, and brought down a key-bugle—we went into the room, and there they showed me part of a double gun in a mahogany gun-case, a gun-stock, and part of a lock—we then all went back again—Burgess wished me to purchase the things that night—I said I could not that night—I was to give twelve guineas for the key-bugle and the two guns—next morning, at nine o'clock Burgess came to me and I appointed to meet him again at Day and Martin's, which I did—he asked me what was the most I would give for them—I said I should not give more than 10 l. or ten guineas—he said he would go and see—he then went to the Archers—the other two men came out, and were walking up and down for some time—Burgess then came and told me it was finally agreed; they were all ready, and I was to give these other two men 9 l.—I said, "What about the 2 l. that Oldham has lent on the guns? I am not going to pay that "—he said, "You must, or you won't have them"—I then agreed to give the other two men 9 l., and Burgess was to ride home in a cab with me, and take the difference of the ten guineas—I was to give him 5 s. for his trouble—he then went to the house, and came and told me every thing was ready—I said, "Well, I had got the money"—he said, "Come up stairs"—I and he and the
other two men went up stain to the same room—I looked at the guns, and saw they were the same, and while I was looking at them, the policemen, to whom I had given information, came in—they fought desperately, and the other two men run away—Oldham came running up, and said, "What is the matter?" and Archer seized him.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Had yon ever been out with Burgess before? A. I do not think I ever had—I do not like to be certain—he was to have the difference between 9 l., and ten guineas, and 5 s. for helping to close the bargain—which would be 35 s.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Was not Connor the pot-man at the public-house? A. I think he was—he was sitting in the tap-room, looking me in the face—I made no bargain to give him anything—they did not lock the tap-room door on me—they did the door up stairs.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Had you ever been in that public-house before? A. I never had—all I saw of Oldham was at the bar, except on the morning of the affray.
WILLIAM BARTON ( police-inspector.) On Tuesday night, the 2nd of April, Bird gave me information—next morning I went with two officers to the Archers', in Newton-street, Holborn—I saw Oldham in the house—I told him I was an inspector of police, and that there was something wrong up stairs—I ordered one man to stand at the door, and let no one out—I went up stairs and found Burgess and two other men, two gun cases, a gun and a stock—there was a general rush—I secured Burgess—one of the other men jumped out of window, and the other ran down stairs—I ran out and met Oldham on the stairs—he asked me what was the matter—I told him he knew very well.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Had you known Bird before? A. Yes, for ten or twelve years, as a respectable tradesman.
Cross-examined by MB. PAYNE. Q. Connor was not taken at that time? A. No—he was taken on the Tuesday following—he was allowed to go on his own recognizance—we took Oldham as soon as we could get from Bow-street.
JOHN ARCHER ( police-sergeant E 8.) On the morning of the 3rd of April I went with inspector Barton to the house in Newton-street—I went up stairs—we found the back-room door fastened—we knocked, and it was opened—I took Burgess and another by the collar—one of the others got out of the window, and the other ran down stairs—I called on Oldham to assist, but he did not.
JOHN FINK ( police-constable E 47.) I went to Oldham's house, and was placed at the door—after some time Mr. Barton came down—I saw Archer with two prisoners—I went to his assistance, and was knocked down by one of the persons who made his escape.
NOT GUILTY .
NEW COURT.— Thursday, April 18 th, 1844.
Sixth Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant,

1306. JOHN KEBBLE and JAMES PEBBLE were indicted for stealing 9 scarfs, value 4 l., the goods of William Higgins and another; and that they had been previously convicted of felony.
WILLIAM OWEN . I am shopman to William and Thomas Higgins, of Bond-street—we sell scarfs, &c. Both prisoners have been in the habit of coming to the shop—I saw them early in Feb., and after they bad been there I missed some scarfs—I believe all these produced to be my master's—this one I know by a cut—I believe alt the others to be theirs.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Did not one of the prisoners buy the one that you know by sight? A. No, he did buy one—I know this most particularly by the cut—I had seen the men on the premises before Feb.—these scarfs were taken at different times—they might have been taken at one time without my knowing it, but I should think not.
ARTHUR NORTH . I produce a scarf pawned by James Keppel on the 10th of Feb.
WILLIAM BOWN , assistant to Mr. Rochford, pawnbroker, Brewer-street, Golden-square. I produce nine scarfs, one pledged by James, on the 22nd of March—the others were taken by our young man—I am certain he is the person.
JAMES HARMAN , pawnbroker. I produce three scarfs. The prisoner James pledged one by himself on the 12th of Feb.—the second time, the 15th of Feb., they were both in company, and pledged two scarfs, but which took the money I cannot say.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you recollect the two coming together? A. Yes I do, on more occasions than this.
JOHN FREDERICK SIMMONDS . I produce two scarfs pledged by the prisoner James on the 22nd of Feb.
( property produced and sworn to.) GEORGE GATIER ( police-constable F 23.) I produce a certificate of both the prisoners former conviction—( read)—they are the persons.
JOHN KEBBLE — GUILTY . Aged 28.
JAMES KEBBLE — GUILTY . Aged 20.
Transported for seven Years.

1307. EDWARD PROCTER and JOHN MELLSON were indicted for stealing 1 mare, price 12 l., the property of James Barford.
MR. PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
JAMES BARFORD , straw-plait dealer, Wilderness-row, Clerkenwell. I had a mare pony for sale at Robinson's depository, Little Britain. On the 31st of August, about four o'clock in the afternoon, it was brought into the yard—the prisoner Mellson came to me—he said, "Is that your pony?"—I said, "Yes"—he said, "It appears to be a very nice one; what do you want for it?"—I said, 14 l.—he said, "Is it quiet and sound?"—I said, "Yes"—he said, would I go along with him to his friend; and I accordingly went with him to the Red Lion, King Edward-street—he introduced me to Procter—Procter said, "I understand you have got a mare pony to sell"—I said, "Yes"—he said, "I should like to see it"—I returned to Robinson's depository, and fetched it—it was trotted up and down—Procter came out, and offered me 12 l.—I would not take it—he offered me 12 l. 10 s., and I closed for it—we went into the Red Lion—the pony was in the street at the time with a person who held it—Procter said he did not intend to buy a horse that day, and had not got cash enough; in fact, he bad not got any with him—I said I should not take anything but money—he said, if I would go with him to Newington Causeway, to his lodging, I should find he had money—Mellson said, "Don't let that binder you, 1 will give you the money for a note of hand"—he took out of his pocket a load of Bank of England notes, as I believed they were—I agreed to go to Newington for the money—I never agreed to take a bill or note of hand—we went to Procter's lodging—just before we got there, they both wanted to put the mare into a stable—Procter said it was his stable—they pulled a bell—a person came—I said, "I will not have the mare put in here, I will have the money first"—it
was taken and set right before Procter's lodgings, at the parlour window—we went into the house—the same man was with it—Mellson said, "I must go and get a stop "—I did not know what that was—Procter drew it up—he said, "I am very short of cash, I shall draw this note of hand for 12 l., if it don't make any difference to you"—I said, "I shan't part with the mare without the money"—in the mean time Proctor was drawing the bill—Mellson took a saddle and bridle down, and put it on the mare—Procter gave me a note of hand and half-a-sovereign—we all left the lodging—Mellson said, "Come along to the George, and I will give you the money for the note of hand"—I said, "Very well"—I bad not parted with the property at that time—I left the mare with Procter, standing in front of the window—I never intended to part with it without the money—Mellson went on just before me—the man sung out, "Who is going to pay me for taking care of the mare?"—Procter gave him 1 s.—I left Procter and him together, while I went with Mellson—he went in at the door of the George, and directly opposite there is another door leading into another street—he popped out there—I said to the landlord, "Is not there a gentleman resembling a horse-dealer here?"—he said, "He is just gone out at the other door"—'I did not find Procter or the mare—I looked out of the door Mellson had popped out of, and could see nothing of him—he must have gone very quick—I was not gone a minute and a half—Procter had gallopped off with the mare.
Crest-examined by MR. BALLANTINI. Q. Did yon give a receipt for the 10 s.? A. No—I put my name on this paper—this is my name—I did not read it—I am no scholar—I supposed I was signing a receipt for the 10 s.—I said I did not give a receipt, because I cannot write a receipt—I do not know that it was read to me—I believe it was—I have no doubt about it—I have told you the fact—Mellson got the stamp for this note of hand, not by my desire—it is a bill of exchange—it was read over to me—I signed it—I signed the bill at seven days, when I expected to be paid immediately, for security for Mellson—I have seen bills of exchange—I cannot read them—I have had them in my possession—I believe this is accepted by Edward Procter—I saw him draw it, and accept it—the amount is 12 l., and the date August 31st, 1843—I have been looking for the prisoners ever since—I could never get sight of them—I found them in custody.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Had you ever sold a pony before? A. No—I have sold forty or fifty horses—I saw Procter very shortly after seeing Mellson—I saw him draw the bill—I took it for security for Mellson—I took it on the day it was due, I think on the Saturday following, to No. 36, Market-street, Newington Causeway—it was not paid—I have set after them to give them into custody ever since—I have spent more than 50 l. about it.
GEORGB TEAKLB ( police-sergeant.) I produce this note—I took Edward Procter into custody on another charge, at Cheyne-walk, Chelsea.
WILLIAM WYATT . I keep a beer-shop in Market-street, Borough. I remember seeing Barford there seven or eight months back—I did not see either of the prisoners there.
PROCTER — GUILTY . Aged 26.
MELLSON — GUILTY . Aged 21.
Transport for Ten Years
[trialAccount t18440408-1308]
1308. JOHN PURDY was indicted for a conspiracy.
AMELIA CHARLOTE LLOYD . I live in Henry-street, St. George's. On the 11th of Jan. I had a covered cart for sale—Purdy came to me and said he was desired by his master to look for a covered cart of that sort—he thought that would exactly suit him—he would go and bring another gentle-man—he
said his master was a woollen merchant—he did not say where—I told him the price was 15 l.—in about an hour and a half he brought Procter, the prisoner in the last case—I inquired whether that was the clerk to the woollen merchant—he said yes—Procter said his master was out of town—he asked to look at the cart—I showed it him—they examined it—Procter told the prisoner to try it—he asked if he thought it was strong enough—he said yes—they did not bring a horse—he took hold of the two shafts—I borrowed a horse, and they tried the cart—he said it would suit, but his master was out of town, and would not be in town till the Tuesday following, he was a very particular gentleman and liked to do business with the proprietor—he asked if I was the proprietor—I said yes—he asked if I would have any objection to take the cart to the gentleman's residence—I agreed to do so when he came in town—he said he would let me know when the gentleman did come—on the Wednesday Purdy and Procter came again and asked me if I would bring it the following day—I agreed—Purday came at eleven o'clock next morning—he was to take me to the gentleman—he took me to St. Thomas's-street in the Borough—he got out of the cart and brought Procter to me—he began to scold me about detaining him so long, as it was one o'clock—I told him I bad had to run about to borrow the horse—he said "These things will happen in business "—he supposed he must excuse me to his master—he told me to follow him to his master—I turned to do so—he (Procter) said, "If you do not intend to take less than 15 l. you had better ask a little more "—I said I was much obliged—he said, "It will make no difference to me, he bought a horse yesterday that will exactly suit your cart"—I said I would ask 16 l.—he took me to his master, John Mellson, he knocked at the door, and Mellson opened it—he began to scold about being detained so long, as be had particular business in the City—he came out, looked at the cart, and asked Purdy if he thought it would suit, he examined the wheel and found some fault with it—I told him I had not brought him a new cart—he asked the price—I said 16 l.—he went towards the cart and saw some iron on one of the shafts—he examined it—Purdy said it made it all the stronger—Mellson turned round to Procter and asked if it would suit—he said exceedingly well—I agreed to take 15 l.—Procter said to Purdy, "John, you take the cart to the wheeler's"—I turned round to follow Procter to his residence, and left the cart—he said "Have you got a stamp receipt?"—I said, "Sir, I am provided with a receipt"—he said, "This is a common one, it is a sixpenny one "—" But it will do for 20 l " said I—he said, "I want an eighteenpenny one, I will get one"—we knocked at the door—he ordered me to sit down, turned to the servant and told him to go out for something—I suppose for the receipt—I said he was a long time gone—he said, "He will be here presently "—there was a little passage to go down, and I heard him say, "14 l. 10 s., how foolish"—I said "I will not take less than 15 l."—he said, "I will soon settle that, I will give you 10 s."—I said, "Excuse me, but I understood you to say 14 l. 10 s."—he gave me a small bit of paper, which was lying on the table, and said, "I will get you to write a receipt for the 10 s."—just as I was about to rise he put a bill on the table and said, "I will get you to write on this your name and residence"—I wrote it, and got up, I held my hand out for the money, and he put the bill into my hand—I said, "Sir, I expected the money"—he said, "This is the same as money, read it"—I said, "If I was to read it I don't understand anything about it"—I was very much confused and frightened—he said "This is as good as Bank of England money, he had just had a gentleman from the city there who owed him 200 l.," and if I did not understand it to pay it away—I came out with my note in my hand—as we were coming out Purdy met us about the spot where I had left the cart—his master
desired him to see me to the bridge—the cart was gone at that time—I had sold it for money—I had no intention to take a bill whatever—I saw no more of them—I have been searching for them ever since—I traced my cart to Mr. Dowsett's—it was sold, I understand.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. You were to agree to give something to Purdy? A. Yes, at the time this was going on about the bill he was not present—when it was over he was coming up—he came and asked me if it was to sell—throughout the whole he appeared to be acting under the direction of Procter and Mellson.
COURT. Q. The contract was made before you want into the house, and you thought it was for ready money? A. Yes.
MART DICKENS . I am single and lire with my brother John in St. Thomas's-street. On the 10th of January I had a bill in my window for lodgings—Mellson and another man cane and took a bed-room, and asked for the use of a parlour—they told me they were engaged in the City the whole of the day, till about five o'clock in the evening—I do not know who the other man was—I have not seen him since—H was not Purdy—they came on Friday evening, and said, they had a letter requesting their immediate attendance in the country as a relation bad died and left them some money, they must go off by the first train, and would be there the following night—he did not return to pay me.
Cross-examined. Q. Was not the person that came with Mellson the person that you have since seen at Procter? A. I have not seen him—I did not tee him before the Magistrate.
CHRISTOPHER HOBMAN . I am a horse-dealer, and live in John-street Harper-street, New Kent-road. On the 12th of January, about four o'clock in the afternoon, there was a cart in the street opposite my door—Mellson was with it—I have seen him before at fairs and markets—fee called on me, and asked if I would change with him for a lighter one—I said I could not—I bought it for 16 l.—I do not know Procter at all—I sent it down to Mr. Dowsett's yard—I left word there that I had such a thing to part with—Mrs. Lloyd's foreman, Joseph Buck came and saw it—I did not know Mellton a a woollen-draper—I think I should have known if he had been so—I alway considered him to be dabbling about telling horses—I never saw any woollen goods with him.
JOHN DOWSETT . I am a wheelwright. On Saturday morning, the 11th or 12th of January, Mr. Hobman brought the cart to me—Mrs. Lloyd's fore-man came and identified it.
GEOROI TEAKLE ( police-sergeant H 8.) On the 26th of Jan, Purdy. came to me, by appointment, on another charge not connected with this—I said I wanted him about a cart—he said, "What I did was on commission" I said, "I have no alternative but to take you before the Magistrate—he said, "I received 1 s. in the pound, 13 s. wat all that I had"—I know Mellson; but not as a woollen-merchant—I always considered him as a horse-dealer—I have seen him at Smithfield-market, and at fairs, in company with horsedealers.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe you called at hit lodging the night before and and saw his wife? A. She spoke to me—he asked me to let him go, and he would come to the office—I did not do to.
JOSEPH GLISSON BUCK . I am foreman to Mrs. Lloyd. On Sunday morning I saw the cart at Dowsett's—it is Mrs. Lloyd's cart, I am quite sure.
JAMES ALLISTON . On the 10th of Dec. last I saw Mellson—he represented
himself to be a wine-merchant, not a woollen-merchant—Purdy represented himself as the servant, and took me to Mellson as a wine-merchant.
WILLIAM ATKINSON . On the 28th of April last I saw Mellson—he represented himself to be Thomas Buckmaster's servant.
THOMAS WARMINOTON . In Oct. last I knew a person of the name of Edward Procter—he represented himself to be a wine-merchant.
GUILTY . Aged 30.— Confined One Year.
(There was another indictment against the prisoner.)

1309. NORMAN BUCKMASTER PROCTER was indicted for a conspiracy.
JOHN WRIGHT . I live at Thames Bank, Westminster. In July I had a bone and cart to dispose of—the prisoner came and asked if I had got it to sell—I said I had—he said he was sent by his master, a wine-merchant, in the Mile-end-road, he wanted a horse, cart, and harness—I drove it that way—he ordered me to pull up, and said if I would drive down there he thought his master would buy it—I said if he thought his master would give a fair value for it I would drive it there—I agreed to give him 10 s. if his master bought it—I went to No. 9, Percival-buildings—his master was not in—he said he would fetch him from the wine-vaults—presently a young gentleman, who I supposed to be his master, came up—it turned out to be Edward Procter—he said, "You are the gentleman that has got the' horse and cart to dis-pose of?"—he looked at it, and we agreed for twelve guineas—I expected to receive money and nothing else—neither bill nor money were mentioned—when we were at the stable he said, "If you will walk up to my residence I will settle with you"—I made several objections, because he wanted me to take a bill—the bargain was for guineas—I took the bill after some persuasion—I sold the cart for this bill ( produced) and 12 s.—the money was never paid—the horse and cart was all I got my living by—I should never have on any terms accepted the bill but for the representation of his master being a wine-merchant and a gentleman.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. The person you were introduced to was an older person than the prisoner? A. No—he appeared to me to be about twenty-four or twenty-five—I think the prisoner appears a little older than seventeen—the person who was introduced as the master did not appear to be several years older than the servant—I will swear that Edward Procter, who was introduced as the master, did not appear to be five or six and twenty, and this person seventeen—I will not swear they both appeared of an age—it was very near St. Martin's-lane that the prisoner told me his master was a winemerchant—I do not know the name of the street—I was first examined at Lambeth-street—it might be about the middle of July—I gave evidence against the prisoner on the 9th of Feb. last—I did not sign any depositions then—I was there several times—I first said the prisoner told me his master was a wine-merchant on the 9th of Feb—I was at Lambeth-street six times—I will swear positively I said something about his having told me his master was a wine-merchant on the first day.
GEORGE TEAKLE ( police-sergeant H 8.) I know Edward Procter—I do not know that he is a wine-merchant—I always considered him as a horsedealer—I have seen him frequenting the repositories, and Smithfield—he said he was a cattle-dealer—I have seen Norman Procter with him in or before July last—they were not taken before Feb., because I was not aware there was a case against them—the prisoner is the brother of the others.
Cross-examined. Q. Edward Procter is five or six and twenty? A. He
may be as much as three or four and twenty—the prisoner is not above seventeen.
GUILTY . Aged 17.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18440408-1310]
1310. THOMAS BUCKMASTER PROCTER was indicted for a conspiracy.
WILLIAM ATKINSON . I live in Little Warner-street, Clerkenwell. On the 8th of April the prisoner, a man named Joe, and Mellson, came to my shed— Joe said he had brought two friends that were just going to begin the cow-keeping line—the prisoner looked at the cow, and asked the price—I said, 18 l.—We went over the way, and had a pot of stout together—Mellson said he was the prisoner Thomas Buck master Procter's servant—he bid me 17 l. 10 s., for the cow—I agreed to let him have it— Joe said, "Come to my house at three o'clock, and take it to this cow-keeper's yard in the Mile-end-road"—we drove it to Mile-end—Mellson met us on the road, and we drove it into a cow-yard—we went to No. 9, Percival-buildings, Percivel-street, to take the money—the prisoner, Mellson, and Joe were there—we all went into the room together—the prisoner said he was disappointed in getting the money, but he would give me a bill—I said I was not in the habit of buying cows for bills, I paid ready money for my cows— Joe said, "It is right for 100 l.," meaning Mr. Procter was worth 100 l.—in consequence of that representation I agreed to take it—I should not have done so if Joe had not assured me he was worth so much money—he said Mellson was the servant—I had 2 l. 10 s. put down to me in gold—Mellson said he would get the bill cashed directly, he would go to a house in the Mile-end-road— Joe took me there—there was no cash there—I showed Teakle the cow-yard.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. You got 2 l. 10 s., did not you? A. Yes—I signed a bill and a receipt for 15 l., not for 17 l. 10 s.—this receipt is my writing—I got 2 l. 10 s. and this bill for the remainder—I am sure Mellson was there—it was as far back as April last—I have a distinct recollection that it was Mellson and not some other man, who said he was this man's servant—I had never seen him before—I have seen him since at Lambeth-street, and am sure he is the person—I have always been sure about it—I thought the bill was not right, and went to the house that night to see the parties—I did not go to the place where the bill was made payable, at the time it was due, nor after.
COURT. Q. For aught you know this may be a good bill, and will be paid to-morrow morning? A. I never presented it after it was due—I have made no inquiries about it since it was due.
WILLIAM PURKIS . On the 4th of July last I saw Mellson—he stated himself to be a wine-merchant—the prisoner was with him—he said he was his servant—I had a horse—Mellson asked me whether I would sell it—he left Procter with me to take the horse up to No. 9—he went along with me—as he was going along he said he was the servant, and persuaded me to sell the horse to his master—I cannot tell whether I should have sold it if I had not believed him to be the servant—they both over persuaded me—I believed Mellson was the master, and was a wine-merchant—I got 5 s. and a bit of paper for the horse—I went the day the bill was due—there was no money.
Cross-examined. Q. When and where was it the prisoner told you his master was a wine-merchant? A. When we were going along with the horse and cart—I am sure of that—I was questioned about it at Lambeth-street about twice—I did not say it at the first time—I cannot say how long afterwards I stated that he told me his master was a wine-merchant—it was about the
third time—I was had up about two months from the first examination, before the parties were committed—it was more than a month after—Mellson never told me he was the servant—Procter said so going along with the horse and cart, and also when I had the bill—Procter brought the bill, and put it on the table already written—I presented it at the same house I received it at on the day it was due—I did not understand that the bills are not payable till three days after date—I took it three or four times afterwards—I went a week after, and saw the landlady—they said he was out every time I went.
GEORGE TEAKLE ( police-sergeant.) I know Procter and Mellson—Mellson was not likely to keep a servant—he is a horse-jobber, and so is Procter—I have seen them together, acting as equals, not as wine-merchant and servant—I have made inquiries at each place—they left directly after.
Cross-examined. Q. Have yon known him several years as a cattle-dealer? A. Yes—I never had him in custody—I saw him frequently after I had been first employed, but I had forgotten he was concerned.
GUILTY . Aged 28.— Confined Twelve Months.

1311. JOHN PAYNE was indicted for a conspiracy.

[trialAccount t18440408-1312]
1312. WILLIAM PERRY was indicted for stealing 12 boxes of patent medicine, called Dr. Freeman's Ointment, value 9 s. 6 d.; and 12 other boxes of ointment, 9 s. 6 d.; the goods of William Henry Sutton and another, his masters: and BENJAMIN EGLINTON and PETER JORDAN for feloniously receiving part of the same, knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
MR. WILDE conducted the Prosecution.
CHARLES THAIN ( City police-constable, No. 19.) At ten minutes or a quarter past nine o'clock on the morning of the 21st of March, I saw the prisoner Jordan at the Eastern Counties Railway—I followed him to Eglinton's house, 1, Blossom-street—I observed that he had nothing in his hands, nor anything of a bulky appearance about his person—he went into Eglinton's house—a few minutes afterwards I saw Eglinton come through the passage from the direction of Shoreditch, or from Norton-Falgate, with a bundle tied up in a blue handkerchief under his left arm—he went round hit counter, untied the handkerchief, and handed a brown paper parcel to Jordan, who took the parcel, put it behind the skirts of his coat, came out of Eglinton's house, and went to Mr. Sharwood's, 55, Bishopsgate-street—a considerable distance before be reached the shop he withdrew one hand from the parcel, and about 50 yards before he reached the shop he withdrew the parcel, and went with it into Mr. Sharwood's shop, at exactly twenty-five minutes past nine o'clock—he remained there a few minutes, came out, and went towards Eglinton's house—on his way I observed him counting some money—I passed by him—I saw him go to Eglinton's house, and saw no more of him at that time—I have seen Perry and Eglinton together two or three times—I have never seen them near the prosecutor's house—I have seen them at Mr. Field's, the Black Dog, many times—I am not aware what Eglinton is by rade—he keeps a sort of grocer's and chandler's shop.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. What was the charge on the other indictment that has been tried? A. Perry was charged with stealing—I cannot say on what day—I did not indict them—I gave evidence on the 11th, 19th, and 20th of March—I only proved the connexion—I mentioned Monday, the 11th of March last, as the day the theft was committed—Eglinton's warehouse is rather better than a mile from Bow Church-yard, where the things were stolen from. ( See paye 945.)
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. You did not interfere with Jordan
at all? A. No—I did not examine his coat to see if he had an inside-pocket—I do not believe be had—I have been a policeman nine or ten years—I cannot say whether you have a pocket in your coat-tail inside—the parcel was placed right up his back.
SAMUEL COOMBS ( policeman.) On the 19th of March I sow Jordan and Eglinton together—I took Perry into custody.
JAMBS ALLAN SHARWOOD , druggist, 58, Bishopsgate-st. I have known the prisoner Jordan many years by name—I have bought things of him—I have bought patent medicines of him—on the 20th of March last I gave him orders for a dozen of each ointment—on Friday the 21st of March, at half-past nine in the morning, he brought me this dozen of patent-medicine—he asked me 8 s. for it, which I paid—that was under the usual price—I had a reason for purchasing at a reduced price, as I had communicated with Messrs. Sutton, and I bought them—9 s. 6 d., was the proper price—I had bought another dozen of him the day previous—I had also purchased half-a-dozen on the 19th—I had refused that half-dozen on the Saturday previous—there is a mark on it—I marked it at half-past nine on the 21st of March—I received these from Jordan in the month of January—it was the last of the patent ointment that he could supply—he stated that he could sell them at less than the regular price.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did you give evidence on a previous charge of receiving stolen property? A. Yes—I had no reason to doubt that he was carrying on a respectable business in the chemist and drug-gist line—I know that he has been acting as a seller of patent medicine on commission—I never saw him at a sale—I have purchased things of him, according to his representation, just after a sale—patent medicines are not gene-rally sold—there are spurious patent medicines—you do not always get the right article—there was no concealment about Jordan in any of the transac-tions—I never heard any thing against his character—the difference between the price Messrs. Sutton sell it at, and what I paid was 18 d., on the twelve boxes.
WILLIAM COLLINS . In March last, I was in the employ of William Henry Sutton and Son—I remember marking some medicines on Sunday the 17th of March—they were kept in a box in the cellar—at ten minutes past eight o'clock, on Thursday, the 21st of March, I saw them in the cellar—I saw Perry there that morning—he is a porter in their employ—the latest time I saw him come out of the cellar was about twenty-five minutes past eight—I cannot speak positively—he left altogether at twenty minutes to nine—all the men went out to breakfast—I saw him go out—I did not look outside the door—directly after he left I went down into the cellar, and missed one dozen—I know this ointment by these marks—I know Eglinton by tight—I had never seen him at our house—I have seen him in the neighbourhood, in Bow-lane—I did not see him on my master's premises on the morning of the 21st—I cannot say whether we have any real patent for these medicines—I do not know of any.
COURT. Q. You saw Perry come out of the cellar, had any body else been in the cellar that morning? A. Yes, James Jarman, one of the young men—I cannot be positive whether it was before or after—he is not here—he went down when I saw the things safe.
JAMES GELLATLY ( City policeman.) I know Eglinton and Perry—I have seen them together at the Plough, Plough-yard. Shoreditch—I saw them there on Wednesday the 20th of March, at half-past eight o'clock in the evening—Perry came to the door and looked in—the pot-boy said. "He is not here yet"—he then went away, returned in three or four minutes, sat down and called for a pint of beer and a pipe—Eglinton came in, and said to Perry, "I have a dozen more ordered; it is strange, it is not, that they should
be wanted, for last week the parties would not look at them, much more buy"—Perry said, "Between this and Saturday"—Eglinton said, "The parties are in a hurry for them"—they soon after left—I heard nothing more that night—I was there on the next evening, Thursday the 21st, the day in question, in plain clothes—I saw them both sitting there—I heard Eglinton say to Perry, "Anything to-night?"—Perry said, "No"—they were soon after joined by a man and lad, and all four left—on Saturday, the 23rd of March, I apprehended Jordan coming from Eglinton's house—I said, "I am one of the City police, I take you into custody charged with receiving stolen goods knowing them to be stolen, belonging to Messrs. Sutton and Co."—he said, "You must be mistaken; I know Sutton and Dicey well, and have laid out hundreds of pounds with them; I will give you my address, Nicholas-street, Hoxton, and No. 9, Star-alley, Fenchurch-street, where I sleep"—he said he might be found when wanted—I told him he must come with me—he said, "Young man, listen to me, and I will be a father to you"—I told him I had no desire to hear any thing he had to say—he said he had a relation in a dying state who he wished to see, and if I wanted 5 l. at any time I could have it—I told him I could not listen to any such proposal, he must come to Moor-lane station—he said, "Very well"—on our road he said he had known Eglinton for years—I had not mentioned Eglinton to him—I said, "I have not mentioned any name"—he said, "No, I thought you saw me come out of his house"—he said, Saturday being a busy day it would inconvenience him a good deal, but he would go to Sutton's if I liked.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. How long have you been a police constable? A. Since the 16th of February—I am twenty-two years old—before I went into the police I was servant to Mr. Stevens, a solicitor, in Queen-street, Cheapside—I get 17 s. a week—if my conduct is approved of I shall get a higher salary—I shall be promoted in the event of giving satisfaction—I do not expect to get up after this, not for six months—I took a memorandum of the conversation the same evening, about half an hour afterwards—I have it here—I was not desired to bring it with me—I was asked for it the last time—I took it down from recollection—I have referred to it pretty often since—I do not know that it is put down in the order that it passed—I do not know that the words exactly follow each other in the order I have told you to-day—I was put there to watch—I did not take the memorandum the first day, because I did not think of it—I did not put it down for the purpose of reference, but to refresh my memory—the pot-boy was here this morning.
Cross-examined by MB. PAYNE. Q. Did not Jordan say, being in his work-day dress, he should like to change it? A. Yes. I said he could not—he said, "I have known Eglinton for years, and have sold drugs for him on commission"—he might have seen me when he came out of the shop—I saw him come out of the shop at half-past three o'clock in the afternoon, and I took him—two of the prisoners had a hearing on Monday—Jordan was examined on Monday morning—it was in the street he said he would go to Sutton's if I liked—he appeared very indignant at the charge—he talked very fast and loud—I have not discovered that he told me a single falsehood—I have never inquired whether the places of residence are correct—he did not make any attempt to escape—he was going the contrary way till I turned him round—he did not fight me—he talked so fast I cannot remember all he said—I never heard any body say that he had a relative in a dying state—he said so himself before the Alderman—I will not swear he did not say he thought it strange Messrs. Sutton had not ascertained the loss instead of taking him prisoner—he spoke about the 5 l. directly after saying he had a relative in a dying state.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Have you ever shown this paper to any body? A. No—I did not mention the conversation to any body except the Magisstrate.
Jordan. It was my own daughter that was dying; that was the reason I offered him 5 l.
GEORGE JONES . I am manager to Messrs. Sutton. I never saw Jordan before—if he had been in the habit of buying patent medicines at our place I must have known him—patent medicines are never sold at a reduction.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Do you know Mr. Jordan who formerly carried on a large business in Whitechapel? A. No.—I cannot swear he has not done business with our house twenty-five years ago—I never heard till the last week or two that he was a large chemist and druggist in Whitechapel—I have been in the business eighteen years, and can only speak to that time—I will not swear he did not carry on business in Whitechapel.
(Joseph Low, goldsmith and jeweller; Francis King, tavern-keeper; Alfred; and Richard Divine, chemist and druggist, No. 76, Aldersgate-street; gave Eglinton a good character.)
(Thomas Williams, tobacconist, No. 87, Blackman-street, Borough; Samuel Cheshire, chemist and druggist, No. 144, Whitechapel; Samuel James Stokes, New North-road; and Henry Cook, cook and confectioner, No. 9, Star-street, Fenchurch-street; gave Jordan a good character.)
PERRY — GUILTY . Aged 40.— Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutors.
Confined One Year.
EGLINTON — GUILTY . Aged 45
JORDAN — GUILTY . Aged 49.
Transported for Seven Years
KENT CASES.
