[trialAccount t18440408-1316]
1316. ELIZABETH NICHOLLS was indicted for stealing 2 handkerchiefs, value 9 s. 6 d.; the goods of Richard Wade and others.
RICHARD WADE . I am a linendraper, and live in High-street, Deptford, in partnership with my brothers. The prisoner was in our shop on the 30th of March, and I received information—I told the young man to go on serving her and let her go—after he had served her she was attracted to the other tide of the shop by a young man who was folding some shawls—I got into conversation with her, and invited her to come out and look at our window, which she did—I said there was one article I should like her to see—I took her again into the shop to another part—I then jumped over the counter, and said my young man told me she had got some handkerchiefs, and asked if she had any objection to be searched—she said "No"—I said to my young man, "Are you sure she has got the handkerchiefs?"—he said, "Yes, I will show you the same pattern in another colour "—these handkerchiefs were then lying at the prisoner's feet—I am sure they had not been there before—that part was three or four yards from where she stood at first—they are mine.
Cross-examined by MR. WILKINS. Q. Is yours a large shop? A. Yes—there was another women with the prisoner—they sat at a counter, and White was showing them handkerchiefs—they would be taken out of the box to be shewn—there were other customers in the shop—there were not less than four young men in the shop—it was supper time—the other shopmen were on the opposite side of the shop, but they were in view—I do not know how the handkerchiefs got at her feet, but they were not there when I took her to the place—the other woman was standing by her side when I asked to be allowed to search them.
STEPHEN WHITE . I am shopman to Mr. Wade. On the 14th of March, a little after ten o'clock in the evening, the prisoner and another woman came to look at some handkerchiefs—while I was showing them some, my attention was aroused by seeing the prisoner try to draw the handkerchiefs from the counter—she then asked for some lighter ones, and while I was going to fetch them I communicated it to my employer, who told me to go on and serve her—I returned with the light handkerchief?, and the prisoner left the other woman, to decide on the handkerchief—I then saw the prisoner draw the handkerchiefs off the counter—shortly after she stooped, and I supposed picked them up, she made a wry face, as if she had a pain in her leg—after that she had some ribbon, which she gave to the other woman—Mr. John Smith was at the desk, and took the cash—he asked if I was certain about the matter, and I looked behind the counter and missed the handkerchiefs—these are the handkerchiefs I saw her draw from the counter—I swear to them—she was then invited by a young man on the opposite side to see some shawls, and then Mr. Wade took her outside—I looked at the place where she had been, and nothing was there—I looked at her dress, and nothing was clinging to it—she was brought back, and a place was cleared for her to go to—Mr. Wade said, "My young man has missed some handkerchiefs, will you allow me to search you?"—they said "Yes "—he searched the other woman first—I then saw the handkerchiefs on the ground in a crumpled state.
Cross-examined. Q. How many young gentlemen were serving in the shop? A. I did not notice—there might have been four.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18440408-1317]
1317. JOHN FEWKES was indicted for stealing 2 spoons, value 18 s., the goods of William M'Niel, his master.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
JANE STEVENSON . I am in the service of Mr. M'Niel, of Charlton; the prisoner came into his service on the 17th of March; he had been a week or ten days before working in the garden. On the 17th the plate was given to him to lay on the table, and after that I said I would wash it—he said, "Never mind, I will"—he washed it and took it up stairs—on wednesday, the 20th of March, my master left home, leaving my young master, the the prisoner and me in the house—I went to bed about a quarter-past nine o'clock, and about two next morning I was awoke by a call of "Murder and thieves"—I heard the prisoner say, "For God's sake, come and help me"—I got a light, alarmed my young master, and went down—I found the prisoner in the kitchen—he had his trowsers and his stockings on, but no shoes, coat, or waistcoat—I was so frightened, I do not well remember what he said—I saw no thieves—the prisoner was taken into custody the same morning—I missed these two spoons—they are my master's.
CHRISTIAN WILLIAM M'NIEL . I am the prosecutor's son. I was disturbed that morning—I went down, and found the prisoner is the kitchen—he slept in a room opposite the stable, which is one staircase above the kitchen—he said that three men had entered his bed-room window, that he had followed them down into the kitchen, and, as he entered, he was knocked down; that he rose up, and was knocked down again, and, on his calling for help, they ran away—I saw the poker, which he attracted my notice to, lying on the floor, nearly bent double—he said one of the men struck at him with it, but had missed him—I did not observe any marks of violence on him, as if he had been knocked down—I went down the moment I heard the cry—between my hearing it, and going down, he had not time to go up stairs and put on his stockings and trowsers.
THOMAS PINKCOMBE . I am the prosecutor's gardener. The prisoner was taken on Thursday morning, the 21st of March, and from that time I assisted my master in the stable till Fleming came on the 25th—from the time the prisoner was taken, till Fleming came, my master kept the key of the stable door, and no one could have access to it—I never went to the stable but when ray master was there—there had been a ladder in the green-house on the Wednesday night, and on the Thursday morning I found it under the pri-soner's window.
JOHN FLEMING . I was engaged by the prosecutor, as groom—on the 25th of March, I went into the stable, and under some fitter I found a silver desert-spoon, which I gave to Stevenson; and about the centre of the stall I found a silver table-spoon, which I took to my master.
WILLIAM M'NIEL . I received a spoon from Fleming—I had left home, but was informed of this robbery, and I returned on the Thursday—I gave the prisoner into custody—I let nobody go into the stable after that till Fleming came—no one could have access to the stable.
Prisoner. There were plenty of people employed about his premises besides myself. Witness. No one could have got the key but from my pocket, or from the key-basket in the parlour.
CHRISTIAN WILLIAM M'NIEL re-examined. I did not take the key from the basket—I did not put the spoons in the stable—the prisoner told the policeman four men attacked him, but he told me three.
JANE STEVENSON re-examined. I did not take the key from the basket, nor put the spoons in the stable.—no one could have taken the key without my knowing it.
JAMES PARRY ( police-sergeant R 8.) I examined the prisoner on the 21st March, to see if there were any marks of violence on him, and then were
none—there were no marks of violence on the house—there was some fresh putty on the window of the room which the prisoner said the thieves entered—they would have left marks on it, but there were none—he said there were four thieves; that one man came out of the room opposite, and attacked him, and when he went down some more men were there; that he was struck on the back, head, and knee—I asked him to let me see the marks, but there were none—there were some crumbs of bread, and meat, and salt in his room—he said he had had none, and no one else had had any in his room.
Prisoner's Defence. I know nothing about the spoons; I was remanded, and they were not found till I came back from Maidstone. It might have been a plot, or something. I waited at table on Sunday, and I took the plate, and gave it to the mistress, in the dining-room, that night. I heard the thieves go through the room; I got up and dressed myself, and went down; the men were in the kitchen, and they knocked me down.
GUILTY . Aged 26.— Confined Six Months.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18440408-1318]
1318. JAMES BOX and HENRY ADAMS were indicted for stealing 1 sixpence, 2 pence, and 1 halfpenny, the monies of George Sandiford; from the person of Frances Sandiford.
FRANCES SANDIFORD . I am the wife of George Sandiford, we live in Marsh-lane, Greenwich. On the 3rd of April I was at the Dockyard-gate at Woolwich—a ship was to be launched, and there was a crowd there—I did not feel anything, but I lost a sixpence, two pence, and a halfpenny, out of my pocket.
JOHN CARPENTER ( police-constable R 84.) I saw the two prisoners at the Dockyard-gate on 3rd April—I watched, and saw them attempt several gentlemen's pockets—I saw them go behind Mrs. Sandiford—Adams went close to her, and Box was behind him—I saw Adams put his hand into her pocket, and take something out—he was in the act of passing it to Box, when I seized him—some money dropped on the ground I picked it up, and with the assistance of Gladwin took both the prisoners.
WILLIAM GLADWIN ( police-constable R 122.) I saw Adams put his hand into the woman's pocket, and take out something, which he banded to Box—I seized Box, and he dropped a sixpence, two pence, and a halfpenny.
Adams's Defence. The constable took me; he took another lad, let him go, then seized Box, and said he was with me; I saw some money on the ground, and they took it up.
BOX — GUILTY . Aged 18.

1320. SOPHIA HARMER was indicted for stealing 1 sovereign, the monies of Edward Britcher; from his person.
MR. PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
EDWARD BRITCHER . I am organ-blower of Lewisham-church. On the evening of 21st March, Mr. Southorn, the churchwarden, paid me 1 l. 1 s. 6 d.,—he put it into a piece of paper—I put it into my side breeches pocket—I went to the Plongh, and called for a pint of beer—I was sitting outside the house—the prisoner came by, and asked me to give her a drink of beer—I let her have a drink, and she sat close by my side—I had my money safe in my pocket when I sat down, and while she was there I looked, and missed the paper all at once—she went into Mr. Griggs, then came and sat down by me again, gave me the paper, and said she did not like to rob me—she went away, and when I was going home, I looked in the paper, and there was only one farthing there—no one else had been sitting by me who could have taken it.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Did you speak to her about having lost your money? A. No—she was with me when I missed it—I had not accused another young woman before the prisoner was taken.
SIMON SOUTHORN . I am the churchwarden of Lewisham—I paid Britcher a sovereign, a shilling—a sixpence, and the sovereign being dull, I went to my cash-box, and took out a bright one—I put it into a paper, gave it to him, and he went away.
JANS GRIGG . I am the daughter of the landlord of the Plough, at Lewisham—Britcher was having a pint of beer—he took it outside, and in a short time after the prisoner came in for two farthings for a halfpenny, which my sister gave her—a short time afterwards Britcher came in, appa-rently, looking at something in his hand—he went out again in a very great hurry—I am certain it was the prisoner came in, I knew her before.
STEPHEN BROOKS ( police-constable R 50.) I took the prisoner in Mill-lane, on the morning after the robbery—I said to her, " Soph., do you recollect how many pints of beer you had with this man at the Plough?"—she said, "Only one"—I said, "Do you recollect how he paid for it?"—she said, "No"—he had described her dress to me—he said she had a light dress, a dark shawl, and a velvet bonnet; and that was the dress she had on—there was another young woman shown to him—I asked him if the girl had a black eye, because there was another young woman used to dress like the prisoner, but she had a black eye—he did not express any doubt about the prisoner.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he not accuse another woman? A. He accused two—he said, "It was them, or somebody like them"—I called them out of an oyster-shop, and then he said that he could not swear to them—but when he saw the prisoner he swore to her.
NOT GUILTY .

1322. JOHN JACKSON and DANIEL GLANVILLE were indicted for stealing 1 handkerchief, value 6 d. the goods of a certain woman, unknown, from her person.

JOHN SEAGER . I am a labourer, and live at Deptford. I have been in the police, but am superannuated—I was with White, and saw the prisoners go down the fair in company, about 150 yards—I saw Jackson take something from a woman's pocket, which appeared to be a handkerchief, and pass it to Glanville—I seized Jackson—he tried to get from me—I tried to get the woman, but the crowd was so great I could not get at her—I have been getting my living the last six months by attending at the police-court, and going on messages for Mr. Finch or the Magistrate.

1324. GEORGE HYDE and ELIZA CLARK were indicted for stealing 1 handkerchief, value 3 s., the goods of a certain man, unknown, from his person.
WILLIAM GLADWIN ( police-constable R 122.) On the 3rd of April I was at the Dock-gate, Woolwich. I saw Hyde put his hand into a gentleman's pocket, and take out a handkerchief—I had been watching him and Clark for five or ten minutes—I tried to seize Hyde, but lost him and the prosecutor—in the course of a quarter of an hour I took Hyde, and the hand-kerchief was found on Clark—I have not had any other cases where the
owner was not known—I took Hyde immediately I saw him, after he had picked the pocket, and Clark was with him then—in coming up the dock-yard they were alone, by themselves, and were talking—I had seen Hyde attempting pockets, and Clark covering him—they were remanded from Wednesday till Tuesday, to see if we could find an owner.
Hyde. I was charged at the station with attempting to pick a pocket, not with stealing. Witness. He was charged with stealing before the Magis-trate; I did not state that we both tumbled down—I said I was shoved down by the crowd—there was a score down at the time—I did not see Hyde five or six times afterwards—if I had I should have taken him.
JOHN CARPENTER ( police-constable R 84.) I saw Hyde before the gates were thrown open, attempt to pick several gentlemen's pockets, and at the time the rush was made, Gladwin told me he had taken a handkerchief—Clark was with him when he was trying the pockets.
ANN THOROGOOD . I searched Clark, and found this handkerchief in her bosom.
JURY to W. GLADWIN. Q. Can you swear that the handkerchief found on Clark was the one Hyde took? A. No, it was a light-coloured one—be-fore Clark was searched, she said she had no property—this handkerchief is marked with initials very different to the prisoner's names—Clark said it had been washed several times, and this has never been washed.
Hyde's Defence. As we were coming out, they shoved and this woman, and four more young men into the Dock-yard, and charged us with attempting to pick pockets; he did not take me for an hour and a half.
Clark's Defence. This is ay own handkerchief; I gave 3 s. for it; I took it off and put it down my bosom.
NOT GUILTY .

1325. MICHAEL BROWN was indicted for stealing 1 handkerchief, value 1 s. 6 l., the goods of William Watkins, from his person.
WILLIAM WATKINS , baker. At half-past ten o'clock in the evening of the 10th of April I was standing at a beer-shop—the prisoner snatched a hand-kerchief out of my hand—I seized him directly, and he passed it behind him to another one—I am sure he is the man—the other went down stairs—I have been out of work since last Saturday week.
Prisoner. Q. What did you tell me it was? A. I said, "Give me that handkerchief;" you said, "What handkerchief?" and that you would "break my b—y neck" if I asked for it.
Prisoner. He would not let my two witnesses come up stairs with me; when he came up he said "Where is Brown;" I said "Here I am," and he took me.
JOHN EWINS ( policeman.) There were two other persons going behind the prisoner to the station, but they would not come inside.
NOT GUILTY .

1327. GEORGE MOORE was indicted for feloniously assaulting Jane Arnold, and cutting and wounding her on the neck, with intent to do her some grievous bodily harm.
JANE ARNOLD . I live in Rope-yard-rail, Woolwich, and am nearly eighteen years old. I have been keeping company with the prisoner nearly two years—on the 3rd of April, about half-past nine o'clock, he came to my mother's house and stopped till half-past ten o'clock—when he left I followed him to close the door—he said he was going home to his mother's at Poplar on Thursday, and asked if I would write to him—I said I would by Monday or Tuesday—he wanted one before then, and asked if I had picked up with any body else while he was away—I said, "No"—he said was I going to give him up?—I said I was—he wished me good night and went away—he came again almost directly after, and knocked at the door—my mother opened it, and asked him to come in—he said no, he wanted to speak to Jane—I went out to him, and he said "You are going to leave me?"—I said, "Yes, George"—he said, "Oh you are, are you?" and he up and hit me with a knife across the neck—it was not a deep cut, it only just went through the skin—I was not much hurt—Mr. Downs attended me—I was going to give the prisoner up because he had got into bad company.
Prisoner. I had my arm on her shoulder; I was cutting my finger nails with a knife, and in putting my arm out to prevent her falling it scratched her neck. Witness. He would not have done it, only he was in liquor; I believe it was not done intentionally, only to frighten me.
CATHERINE ARNOLD . Last Wednesday night my daughter, who is a servant, came home about eight o'clock—the prisoner came about half-past nine o'clock, and stopped an hour or so—he was in liquor—I said, "George, we want to go to bed"—my husband had gone to bed—he jumped up and went out, and my daughter also—they had some words outside, and my daughter seemed very agitated when she came in—in two or three minutes he knocked at the door, and said he wanted to speak to Jane—he was very agitated, and looked very wild—I said, "George, come in"—he said, "No, I want to speak to Jane"—my daughter went out—in a few minutes I heard a scuffle, and heard her say, "Oh!" or "Oh mother!"—I jumped out of my chair, and saw her throat was cut and bleeding—I ran to the door and said, "Oh George, what have you done!"—I rushed in doors, bolted the door, ran up stairs and told my husband—he ran down in his shirt, and went to look for a policeman—a doctor was sent for—the prisoner had always behaved very kindly before, and she is still very fond of him.
GEORGE DOWNS , surgeon. I was called in to examine the prosecutrix's throat—there was a cut on the left side of the neck about two and a half inches long, merely through the skin—it was quite superficial.
AMY HOOPER . I am single, and live in Rope-yard-rail. Between ten and eleven o'clock at night the prisoner applied to me to lend him a knife, which I did—he was intoxicated—I did not know what he wanted it for—it was a small dinner-knife—I knew him before.
WILLIAM WATSON , labourer. I met the prisoner just at the dock-yard gate about half-past eleven o'clock the morning after this, and asked what he had been doing—he said he did not know—I asked what made him do it—he said he did not know—then he said he meant to do it to himself, but the knife turned.
WILLIAM GLADWIN ( policeman.) I apprehended the prisoner on Saturday, the 6th, two days after it happened—I told him it was on suspicion of cutting a young girl's throat—he said it was done accidentally, he was standing at the door with her paring his nails with the knife, that he had his arm round her neck, and hearing somebody coming he went to save her from falling and cut her neck.
JANE ARNOLD re-examined. The first time be had his arm round my waist, but not at the time he cut me—I did not observe the knife till he cut me.
GUILTY of an Assault. Aged 20.— Confined Six Weeks.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.

1326. GEORGE BAKER, GEORGE BASSETT , and JOHN GRANT were indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of James Lindsey, about one in the night of the 19th of Feb., at Lewis-ham, with intent to steal, and stealing therein, 2 pairs of spectacles, value 1 l. 10 s.; 2 milk-pots, 1 l.; 2 ladles, 1 l.; 8 spoons, 4 l.; 3 handkerchiefs, 6 s.; 3/4 of a yard of silk, 10 s.; 30 sovereigns, 8 half-crowns, 40 shillings, 30 sixpences, 6 groats, 30 pence, 40 halfpence, 60 farthings; and 1 30 l., 1 10 l., and 3 5 l. Bank-notes; his property; to which
BAKER pleaded GUILTY . — Transported for Ten Years.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
JAMES LINDSEY . I am a grocer, and live in the parish of Lewisham. On the night of the 19th of Feb. I was the last person up in the house—I went to bed about eleven o'clock—everything was then safe and fast—the skylight of the washhouse was quite safe—I came down about seven o'clock in the morn-ing—Dodd, my servant, gave me information—I keep the post-office—I found the post-bag opened, the letters taken out, opened, and torn; all my drawers open, and the things in them all out—I went to the washhouse, and found the lead-work and frame of the skylight completely removed—they had got in that way—the hinges were broken open, and the skylight entirely removed off—I know Bassett by his coming for dust occasionally—he had an opportunity of seeing the premises—he has been there several times.
ELIZA DODD . I am servant to the prosecutor. On the morning of the 20th Feb., about a quarter past seven o'clock, I came down stairs—I was the first that came down—I went into the shop, and found everything in confusion, the doors open, and things lying about—I went up, and told my master—I had 4 s. in my work-box, in the kitchen—that was taken, and the things all turned out on the floor.
REBECCA LINDSEY . I am the prosecutor's wife. I take care of my hus-band's money—on the morning of the 20th of Feb. I missed from the desk in the parlour a 30 l. Bank-note, a 10 l., and three 5 l. Bank-notes; and, among other money, I lost between two and three shillings' worth of farthings, in a little tin box in the till—I lost a great deal of other property, which was all safe before—I had occasion to look at the farthings to give change shortly before, and there were two very old-looking ones and a counterfeit shilling—those two farthings were left on the table in the kitchen—the shilling has been found by the officer—this is it—I know it well—I took it of an old man, who said he would change it for me again, but he had no other.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did you take it for a shilling? A. No, for a farthing—I know it—I have no private mark on it.
WILLIAM PERRING . I am a carpenter, and live next door to Mr. Lindsey. I was awoke out of my sleep on this night by my dog barking I heard a noise, which appeared to be in Mr. Lindsey's warehouse or washhouse, where the
skylight is—I fell asleep again—it was about two o'clock—I am quite positive it was before six—it was dark.
Cross-examined. Q. Are you quite sure you were not dreaming? A. Yes, I was awake for three quarters of an hour—I thought it was Mr. Lindsey up, going to market.
SARAH HOGG . My husband keeps the Lord Nelson, in Dock-street, Deptford, about two miles from Lewisham. On the morning of the 20th of Feb. the three prisoners were at our house—Bassettand Baker were there about a quarter after six o'clock, and Grant came about seven—I knew them before, and have no doubt of them—they used my house—I served them with some ginhot—Baker called for it, and paid 4 1/2 d. for it, all in farthings, except this coin, which is a counterfeit shilling—I made a mark on it—this is it—I took it as a farthing—I put the farthings on a shelf where there was no other money—Grant had not come then—when Grant came he drank with them—I gave the farthings to the policeman, and the bad shilling.
Cross-examined. Q. Where did you put the farthings? A. On the shelf at the back of the bar and the shilling with them—I put them there about a quarter past six o'clock in the morning—the policeman did not have them for two days, but he came and inquired about them the same day—I am positive they are the same—I took them off the shelf and marked them when the policeman came, which, I believe, was the same evening—I served in the bar that day—I do not know that anybody else did—my husband was in and out—nobody else serves—we do not admit people into the bar—we do not keep a maid—the potman was in the hospital at the time—from the time the farthings were paid to me till the policeman came, nobody but myself and husband were in the bar—I was there all day, and must have seen if anybody came—if I left the bar my husband was there—the policeman came about seven o'clock the same evening, and requested me to take care of them—I marked them then, and locked them up in the cupboard.
MR. DOANE. Q. Did it call your attention the men paying you entirely in farthings? A. Yes—I did not observe the counterfeit shilling among them till the officer came—I had no farthings, or money in the place where I put the farthings—I had taken one farthing away to send out change—with that exception, they tallied.
MARGARET DUNN . I live in Dock-street, Deptford. I know all the prisoners—Baker kept company with my sister—he came to me on the morning of the 20th of February, between seven and eight o'clock, and gave me 10 s. to take to my sister—while he was talking to me, Bassett came and called him—I heard him, but did not see him—he stood on the step of the door, and said, "Come along, Baker, you know where we have got to go to; make haste, Baker, you know where we have got to go to "—Baker said, "I am coming "—he gave me 4 d. to get something to drink for myself, and went away—he had got more money—I saw silver in his purse.
FREDERICK DOBSON . I am barman to Mr. Quarry, of Penny fields, Poplar. On the morning of the 20th of Feb., between seven and eight o'clock, three men came there—I believe Grant and Bassett to be two of them—they had a pint of porter, and drank together—it came to 2 d., and they paid for it in eight farthings—they asked me if I would take six pennyworth of farthings of them, which I did—they afterwards said, "We have fourpennyworth more," and I gave them fourpence more for farthings—they then asked if I could give them change for a 5 l. note—I do not know which of them it was—I can recognise Grant.
Cross-examined. Q. You had never seen Bassett before? A. No—I only think he is the man—I cannot tell which offered the farthings.
ROSE DAVIS . I am single, and live in Ship-yard, Temple-bar. On Tuesday,
the 20th of Feb., between two and three o'clock in the day, I met the three prisoners in the Strand—I knew them before—I remained in their company all the afternoon, and I went to the play with them all three—Bassett went home, and passed the night with me—next day I joined company with all three of them again, and went to the Grapes public-house—they gave me some gin there—Bassett gave me 10 s. when at home—they treated me at the play, Baker paid for us all—I saw Baker with two or three 5 l. notes on Wednesday the 21st, and five or six sovereigns—I did not see any with Grant—Baker was in my company all day long on Wednesday—on Wednesday night Baker asked the other prisoners if they wanted any money—they said they did—he pulled out some gold and silver, and gave each of them some silver, but I do not know how much—Bassett said he had some silver spoons on board his ship, and if he could get them away, he would make me a present of one or two—Bassett paid 3 s. for the bed we slept in, for each night—he slept with me two nights.
CHARLOTTE DUTTON . I live at the same house as Davis. On Tuesday, the 20th of Feb, I saw the three prisoners, and accompanied them to the Grapes public-house—I went with Grant, and passed the night with him, for which he gave me 10 s., and 3 s. for the room—he accused me of stealing a sovereign from him—I saw he had some sovereigns, but I did not notice how many—we were all together at the Grapes—Davis and several other girls were there in company—Baker treated all of us there—he treated pretty well every person that came—we remained there drinking at his expense till between five and six o'clock, when we went to the theatre—Baker paid for the lady he was with—Grant paid for me—we had a cab —I heard Bassett say he had sent the cabman to get change for a 5 l. note, and he had promised to give him 15 s. if be returned quick—we went to the theatre without waiting for the cabman's return—Bassett said, "Never mind, perhaps some day I shall see him in Newgate "—Baker and Grant heard that—I saw Grant with a 5 l. note, besides two or three sovereigns—he only staid with me one night.
Cross-examined. Q. You are an unfortunate girl, I suppose? A. Yes—it was in the Grapes that Bassett spoke about the cabman, just previous to our going to the theatre—I believe what he said was "We have sent the cabman for change for a 5 l. note, but never mind, we shall perhaps see him in Newgate before long"—it was not Bassett that said that, it was Grant—I was not there at the time it was sent—to the best of my knowledge it was Grant said it.
MR. DOANE. Q. Was it the man that slept with you? A. Yes, to the best of my knowledge.
SARAH DEN TON . I am an unfortunate girl, and live in Newcastle-court, Strand. On the night of the 20th Feb., about half-past one o'clock I went over to No. 15, and saw the three prisoners there, Dutton and Davis were with them—I went and slept with Baker at that place—in the morning I saw he had a great deal of money—I did not see Bassett or Grant with any.
EDWARD BROWN , I keep the Grapes, in the Strand, On Tuesday, the 20th Feb., between four and five o'clock in the afternoon, the three prisoners came to my house, and staid about an hour and a half or two hours—the three witnesses and several more girls were with them—they were treating them—the money was sent out by the waiter—I cannot say who paid—they went away—I saw them all again next day with the same three girls and several others—they staid at my house all that day enjoying themselves—they laid out altogether about 4 l. with me—Grant changed a 5 l. note previous to going to the play—I saw a cabman there, and he brought a 5 l. note to me—I kept it—I have spent it—I gave him his change for it—I gave Bassett or
Baker 3 l. previous to going to the play—I gave a sovereign to each—the note the cabman had, went on with the reckoning until it came to 3 l.—I gave them a sovereign each, as I had 2 l. left out of the 5 l.,—I gave him 3 l. previous to going to the play, and next day the reckoning came to 3 l. 10 s.—I saw two 5 l. notes, that is what I speak of.
Q. You have said you changed a 5 l. note for Grant, and they went to the play? A. I meant to say I advanced him 3 l.—I have not said I saw two 5 l. notes together—5 s. was to be given to the waiter—I paid the two 5 l. notes to Mr. Webb, a wine-merchant, at London-bridge, one of which I received from the cabman—Baker put a name on the back of the 5 l. notes—it is not here.
COURT. Q. Do you keep a house for thieves and prostitutes? A. I keep a house.
ELIZA DAVIS . I live at Deptford. On Sunday, the 18th of Feb., I saw Bassett—he gave me a Bible, and asked me to buy it for 1 s.—I gave him 1 s. for it—he said he had no money to pay for his night's lodging, and so he sold it.
BENJAMIN LOVELL ( police-sergeant). On Tuesday, the 20th Feb., I went to Mr. Lindsey's—I found the sky-light removed—in consequence of information, I went after the prisoners—I had seen them all three on Monday night, the 19th, about half-past ten o'clock, at the Riding-circus, Greenwich, about half a mile from the prosecutor's. On Tuesday evening, about eight, I went to the Lord Nelson, in Dock-street, they were not there then—I saw Mrs. Hogg, she showed me sixteen farthings, and among them a counterfeit shilling—she marked them—I afterwards received them from her—they have been produced. On the 26th I found Grant at the station, and said to him, "Well, John, I was pretty close to you yesterday, in Whitechapel, here you are"—he said he knew I was, and said he had been to my house to give himself up to me, and that he had nothing to do with the robbery—I had not said anything to him about the robbery—I had said, "I understand you were all down at Lewisham on Saturday"—he said, "Yes, I was"—he said they came down after the silver spoons that were hid in the Roebuck tea-gardens—that Baker got them out and sold them for 20 s.—I asked what he had done with the notes—he said he never saw them—he had on a new cap and pair of shoes—he said Baker bought them for him in Rosemary-lane.
JAMES DOUGLAS ( police-inspector.) On the 26th February I was at the station at Greenwich, when a man named Baxfield came with Grant—he said in Grant's presence that he had come to his house on purpose for him to go with him to Sergeant Lovell, to give himself up for the robbery at Lewisham, as he heard he was after him—he said he was not with him when the robbery was committed.
BASSETT †— GUILTY . Aged 21.
GRANT †— GUILTY . Aged 25.
Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18440408-1330]
1327. GEORGE WHITE was indicted for stealing 1 handkerchief, value 1 s.; the goods of William Holt, from his person.
WILLIAM HOLT . I live in Regent-square—I was at Greenwich fair on the 9th of April, and I lost my pocket handkerchief—this now produced is it.
GEORGE KEMP ( police-constable N 82.) I saw the prisoner take the handkerchief from the prosecutor's pocket—I had been watching him for twenty minutes, attempting to pick people's pockets—I secured him concealing it under his coat, and found another silk handkerchief on him.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. How long have you been in the police? A. Very nearly fifteen years—I know it is an offence to pick a
pocket—I have taken many scores for it—it did suit me to take the prisoner for the attempt—if I take a person for an attempt I take him before a Magistrate and he is not brought here—I get no expenses before a Magistrate—I do not get much here—I shall get nothing for this—that was not my reason for not taking him, I considered it my duty to wait till it was done—I am allowed a discretion—that is my instructions from Mr. Molineux, my superior—we are told not to bring any attempts if we can help it, they do not like it—the Magistrates commit for an attempt if the party is a known thief—that was the reason I did not take the prisoner—I saw him attempt a dozen pockets.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Confined Three Months.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18440408-1331]
1328. JAMES WILLIAMS , was indicted for stealing a handkerchief, the property of a man unknown, from his person.
GEORGE KEMP ( police-constable N 82.) On the night of the 8th of April I was on duty in a booth at Greenwich fair, about eleven o'clock—I saw the prisoner in company with another man—I saw them try several pockets—the mob was so great I could not get hold of them at the time—I saw them armin-arm—I saw the other man pick a gentleman's pocket of this white handkerckief—they were both together—the prisoner could see what he did—I saw the other give the handkerchief to the prisoner who put it into his pocket—I secured him, and took it out of his pocket—I found three pairs of gentlemen's gloves on him, two tobacco-boxes, two latch-keys, one door-key, and some money.
Prisoner. Q. Why not take the other man? A. I could not, the mob was so great—I could not get to the gentleman—it is a lawn handkerchief, marked "T. F."—I was obliged to have five or six policemen to keep the prisoner—there was about 500 people there, and 50 policemen clearing them out—we could hardly get them out at all—I had my stock torn off my neck.
Prisoner. I have come from Gloucester to look for work; the policeman thrust his hand into my pocket, and pulled out the handkerchief; who put it there I cannot say, I was not aware that it was there; almost every one in the booth were arm-in-arm, resisting the policemen clearing the booth.
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18440408-1332]
1329. ISAAC TURNER was indicted for stealing 3 sheets, value 8 s.; 4 table-cloths, 3 l.; 3 pairs of trowsers, 1 l.; and 1 ring, 30 s.; the goods of James Louttit Braimer, his master.
MR. HORRY conducted the Prosecution.
JAMES LOUTTIT BRAIMER . I am first-lieutenant in the Royal Marines, at Woolwich. The prisoner was in my service three months—I missed five table-cloths and a number of sheets, but three I can identify, also three pairs of trowsers, a gold ring, and other articles of wearing apparel—I have seen the four table-cloths, sheet, and gold ring, which are mine.
Prisoner. When I went into his service there were only three large table-cloths and two small ones. Witness. There were four—it was only about a week before I disembarked from the Camperdown —three large table-cloths were pawned by him, and I have one now in my chest.
WILLIAM TAYLOR . I am servant to Lieutenant Braimer. On the 25th of March I received these three duplicates from the milkman, and gave them to my master.
ANDREW DOUTHELL , milkman, Francis-street, Woolwich, The prosecutor is a customer of mine—the prisoner asked me to lend him 10 s. about the 22nd or 23rd of Feb.—I refused, but he pressed so hard, and said he was in great trouble, and he wished to send it to his friends—I lent him 10 s.—he said if I would he would fetch me some property, and he gave me these three duplicates which I gave to Taylor.
GEORGE STEVENS , shopman to Mr. Davit, pawnbroker, Woolwich. I produce three table-cloths, and other articles, pawned I believe by the prisoner—he was not dressed in regimentals at the time—I gave two of these duplicates for them.
GEORGE JAMES ESCHMAN , shopman to Mr. Moore, pawnbroker, Woolwich. This table-cloth was pawned at our shop on the 7th of March, with a pair of trowsers, for 4 s., by the prisoner, in the name of Braimer—he afterwards sold me a ring.
JAMES PARRY ( police-sergeant.) I took the prisoner into custody on the 29th of March, for robbing his master—he said he knew nothing about it.
SAMPSON DARKIN CAMPBELL ( police-inspector.) I know Mr. Jeremy's writing—I have no doubt this is his signature—( read)—" The prisoner says I bought the tickets but do not know of whom."
( property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I did not pawn the things; I bought the tickets; I cannot tell the person's name I bought them of.
GUILTY . Aged 25.— Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18440408-1333]
1330. DAVID TRUBY, JOHN WRIGHT , and BENJAMIN SMITH were indicted for stealing 1 handkerchief, value 6 d., the goods of a man unknown, from his person: and ANN WILLY , for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing it to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
GEORGE KEMP ( police-constable N 82.) On Tuesday afternoon, the 9th of April, about three o'clock, I was on duty at Greenwich fair, standing at the top of a show—I saw the three male prisoners in a crowd standing behind a gentleman—I saw Truby take a handkerchief from the gentleman's pocket, and pass it to Wright, and they all three went out of the crowd to Willy, who was a distance off—I saw them look at the handkerchief and give it to her—I got down from the show to go after them, and in doing so saw another man pick a pocket—I took that man to the station, and while there the three male prisoners were brought in for picking another man's pocket—when Willy took the handkerchief she put it under her clothes and into her pocket, where I found it when I took her—she said she had been out from her husband all night, sleeping with the men, and said, "See what it is to get into bad company, they brought the handkerchief to me "—I asked if she had any more about her—she said yes, put her hand in her bosom and pulled out two more—I cannot swear this is the handkerchief Truby took, but I believe it is—I found it where I saw her put it.
Smith. The property is mine; I bought them at 2 s. each, not knowing it was stolen; I have known Willy about five weeks by living in the same house; I met her about one o'clock, and asked her to take these handkerchiefs home for me; I was selling fruit.
Willy. When he took me he said, "You have got a handkerchief "I said, "Yes;" he said, "Who gave it you?" I said, "Those three young men;" I am innocent; I did not know it was stolen; I had been out with my husband at nine o'clock; I went into the fair about eleven; Smith said, "Will you take these handkerchiefs for me?" I said, "Yes."
GEORGE KEMP re-examined. Smith was with the other two, covering them—they all three went to Willy and delivered the handkerchief to her.
Smith. It was only I went to her; we were there three hours after we handed her the handkerchiefs; I was selling fruit. Witness. It might he an hour—he was not selling fruit—the person robbed was a respectable looking man—I did not ask him any questions—I could not get near him—there it no initial on the handkerchief.
TRUBY — GUILTY .
WILLY — GUILTY .
Confined One Year.
WRIGHT — GUILTY .
SMITH — GUILTY .
Transported for ten years.
(There were two other indictments against the male prisoners.)
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18440408-1334]
1331. WILLIAM HENRY HATTER and CHARLES THOMPSON were indicted for unlawfully breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Samuel Brightley, with intent to steal.
ANN BRIGHTLEY . I am the wife of Samuel Brightley, a bricklayer, and live in Crossfield-lane, Deptford. He is the housekeeper—on the afternoon of the 8th of April (Easter Monday,) about three o'clock, I left my house leaving no one in it—I bolted the back door, locked the front, and shut the window and shutters—I came home at half-past seven, and found people round the house—the back door was broken open, and the staple out—I did not lose any thing.
HENRY BRIGHTLEY . I am the son of last witness—I live with my father—on Monday the 8th of April, about seven o'clock, I was with Taylor at the top of the lane, about thirty yards from home, and saw the two prisoners go into the house—they went quietly in at the door—I did not see how they got in—I directly went and knocked at the door which was shut—I heard one say to the other, "Don't open the door"—I called Taylor, and told him to get over the fence, and get in the back way which he did—I heard them come down stairs—I got over the fence myself and saw both of them in the back yard—I heard them run up stairs again, and Hatter opened the door, he made use of a very bad expression, and said he would knock my eye out—he knocked me down—I got up again—he ran down Crossfield-lane, and down a turning where there is no thoroughfare—I there caught him, and gave him into custody.
GEORGE RANDALL . I live in Crossfield-lane, Deptford. I saw the prisoners come out of the prosecutor's house and run away—I did not know them before—I went after Thompson and took him—I did not say any thing—he offered me 5 s. if I would let him go.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did you lose sight of him at all? A. No—when I took him, he said, "I am done. "
SAMUEL BRIGHTLEY . I came home between seven and eight o'clock—I found a bag under the range—I took it out, and found in it four skeleton keys and a small wrench bar about a foot long—I gave them to the officer.
Cross-examined. Q. Who lives in the house besides you? A. No one—I rent it.
FRANCIS TAYLOR . I was with Brightley, and saw the prisoners—I saw Hatter come out of the back of the house, and assisted in securing him.
HATTER — GUILTY Aged 20
THOMPSON — GUILTY Aged 27
Confined six months.

1333. WILLIAM GOODWIN and JOHN BASHFORD were indicted for stealing 1 bushel of a mixture of chaff, oats, and beans, value 18 d.; the goods of Harvey Combe and others, the masters of Goodwin; to which

[trialAccount t18440408-1337]
1334. JOHN BRADLEY was indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Richard Neate, in the night of the 30th of March, at St. Mary, Newington, with intent to steal and stealing therein, 1 till, value 6 d.; 32 cheroots, 3 s.; 1 box, 2 d.; 1 bag, 2 d.; 1 clock weight, 2 d.; 2 sixpences, and 12 pence, his property.
RICHARD NEAVE . I am a licensed retailer of beer, and live in Villier's-street, Walworth Common, in the parish of St. Mary, Newington. I keep the house—on Saturday, the 30th of March, I locked up my house—between two and three in the morning I was aroused by the police, I came down, and saw the prisoner in custody at the foot of the stairs near the bar—I found the tap room window facing the street, wide open, and my till on the table, which I had left underneath the bar counter over night—I missed two sixpences—a bag was taken from a cupboard in the bar, which was found in an adjoining room—the house had been entered by the tap room window which was shut down close over night, and the shutters were shut, but not fastened—I had seen the prisoner in the tap room the evening before—he was not in the tap room when I went to bed.
Prisoner. I was in the house playing at cards; I was intoxicated; I fell asleep, and got under the seat, and was locked in; I could not make any body hear; I pulled down the shutters, and the policeman was standing outside. Witness. It is a very small tap room—alter all the customers were gone I went and took the glasses off the table—the gas was burning strong, and if he had been in the room I must have seen him.
HENRY PRIEST ( police-constable P 243.) I was on duty on Walworth Common on the 30th of March, between two and three o'clock in the morning—I noticed the prosecutor's tap-room window open—I got in, and found the prisoner in the back kitchen, in one corner, without his shoes—they were in the tap-room.
HENRY BURR ( police-constable P 175.) I know the prisoner—on the Saturday night, about a quarter or twenty minutes past twelve o'clock, I saw him and two others take a cab in East-street, and go in the direction of Villiers-street—about a quarter to two I met him again—I suspected, stopped, and searched him, and found on him a halfpenny, a tobacco-box, a lucifer-box, and a knife—he was not quite half a mile from Villiers-street—he was sober.
Prisoner. It was on Friday night, the 30th, I was going down East-lane, and met two young chaps and a cabman I knew; we asked him to give us a ride; we all three got in, and rode a little way; we then came back, and had some beer at the Bell; I saw the policeman about an hour and a half after, and he told me to go about my business.
RICHARD NEATE re-examined. A box of cheroots was taken from a cupboard in the bar, and found on the floor: and the bag and money were all removed from where I had left them.
GUILTY .* Aged 18.— Confined One Year.
Before Mr. Justice Williams.
[trialAccount t18440408-1338]
1335. THOMAS FREEMAN, ALFRED YOUNG , and JOHN MURPHY , were indicted for feloniously making and counterfeiting 3 penny pieces.
MESSRS. SCRIVEN and CHAMBERS conducted the Prosecution.
SAMUEL HARRIS ( policeman.) On the 29th of March, about five o'clock in the afternoon, I went into No. 12, Norfolk-street, Southwark, to search for stolen property—the street door was open—Annett and Church went with me to the door of the back attic—we found the door fastened inside by a piece of wood put into a sort of iron dowell—we forced it open, and, on entering, found the three prisoners there—Freeman was sitting by the side of the fire—I saw him put his hand on this pipkin, which was on the fire—he took hold of it, turned it over, and spilled some of the contents into the fire—I could distinctly see it contained metal—I went and laid hold of it, then went buck to the door to prevent their going out, and picked up this file—the other officers secured the prisoners—as I entered the room I saw Young with his arm throw something out of the window, which was open, and which looked into the back yard—after taking them to the station, we all returned to the back yard—we had been absent about twenty minutes—I observed the window through which Young had thrown something, and in the back yard of No. 19, Pepper-street, which is the next yard, I found a counterfeit penny piece—I did not see Murphy do anything—I saw Young distinctly throw something through the window.
EDWIN JAMES ANNETT . I went with Harris on the 29th to this house—I saw Harris, on going into the room, take the pot off the fire, and take it towards the door—Freeman was sitting nearest to the door, close to the fire, and had a mould in his hand, which I produce—I took up a penny piece which dropped from the mould in his hand, with part of the get on it—it was very hot, I could scarcely hold it—the moment I took hold of him he broke the mould between his finger and thumb—I saw Young get up, and throw some pieces of metal, apparently about the size of penny pieces, from his right hand, through the window, which was open—I saw them fall into the yard of No. 20, Pepper-street, about five yards from the house—when I entered the room Murphy was sitting close to the window—they all sat close together—it is a very small room—when I went in Murphy got up—I secured the prisoners, searched the room, and found these coloured penny pieces on the floor—I found this spoon on the window cill, and a bag containing blue—these pincers were on the floor, near where Murphy had sat—this bag was damp—I found this knife on the hob, with a piece of metal attached to it—I took the prisoners to the station, returned, and found four penny pieces, coloured like copper, in the yard of No. 20, Pepper-street, where I had seen Young throw them—Freeman was looking for something—I asked what he was looking for—he said, for the lock and key—I said, "Do you belong to the room?—he said, "Yes"—I saw a padlock in the room-Freeman showed me another padlock—he said that was not the one they locked the place with.
Young. Q. Where was I standing when I threw the things out? A. In the middle of the room, close to Murphy—I saw the penny pieces drop on the ground in the yard—Freeman was in his shirt sleeves.
SAMUEL CHURCH . I went with the other policemen to this house, and saw the prisoners there—I saw Young throw some pieces of coin out of window—I found a penny piece, and a bag of plaster of Paris, on the mantel-shelf over the fireplace.
MR. JOHN FIELD . I am inspector of counterfeit coin to the Mint. This pipkin contains metal similar to what the counterfeit coin is composed of—here is a small piece of a plaster of Paris mould, which has part of the impression of
the obverse side of a penny piece—here are six penny pieces, coloured, and finished for circulation; and two uncoloured, one as it comes from the mould, with the get, and another unfinished, but without the get —these must have been cast in a perfect mould—there is not sufficient left to enable me say it is the mould they were cast in, but they correspond with what is left of it—this blue is sulphate of copper, which will produce the colour of copper on the white metal, by dabbing it—here is an iron spoon, which appears to have been used to lade the metal into the pipkin—the moulds are usually made of plaster of Paris—the pincers would separate the get from the coin—these implements are all that would be required to make counterfeit penny pieces.
Freeman's Defence. None of these things belong to the room; a man named Adnam brought them, and asked me to take care of them; he put them into the fire; directly he was gone the policemen came up.
Murphy's Defence. I left my place, and went up to ask Freeman to let me leave my shirt and stockings there till I got a lodging; he asked me to sit down, and up came the policemen.
FREEMAN — GUILTY . Aged 22.
YOUNG — GUILTY . Aged 20.
Confined Twelve Months.
MURPHY — NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Justice Maule.
[trialAccount t18440408-1339]
1336. ANN THOMAS was indicted for feloniously and knowingly uttering counterfeit coin; having been before convicted of a like offence.
MESSRS. SCRIVEN and CHAMBERS conducted the Prosecution.
CALEB. EDWARD POWELL . I am assistant solicitor to her Majesty's Mint. I produce a copy of the record of conviction of Ann Thomas in Oct., 1841—I have compared it with the record—it is correct—( read,)
JOHN BURROWS ( policeman.) I was present in this Court at the trial of Ann Thomas in 1841—the prisoner is that woman.
JOSEPH WILLIAM WHITAKER . I live at the Noah's Ark, Park-street, Southwark. About a week previous to the 28th of Feb., in the afternoon, the prisoner came and had a quarters of gin, and gave me a bad shilling—I examined it, and put it on the counter, saying, "This is a bad shilling"—she immediately took it up, and put it into her mouth—I do not know whether the swallowed it or not, but I could not get it from her—I detained her for an hour and a half, could not find a policeman, and let her go—I am certain she is the woman—there was an old woman with her.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. How long was the shilling in your possession? A. Two or three minutes—the old woman went out, but I ran and prevented her—the prisoner did not offer to go—I think it was a week before the 28th of Feb.—I had a case here that day—I am satisfied of her being the person, and it was within a week of the time she was at Union-hall.
MR. CHAMBERS. Q. You happened to be at the Police-court? A. Yes, and recognized her.
SARAH PARTRIDGE . I am the wife of Samuel Partridge, of the White Hart. On the 28th of Feb. the prisoner came for half-a-quarters of gin with another woman—she gave me a bad shilling, which I bit, and said it was bad—the other woman gave me 2 d. for the gin—she wanted the shilling back, and was abusive because I would not give it her—I kept it till the 11th of March in my pocket—she then came again, and my niece served her with gin, which she paid a bad shilling for—I said she was the woman who came the other day—she said, "I am certain you are mistaken, but I am positive of her—she went out directly, and my husband followed her.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you never see two persons exactly alike? A. No—I am certain of her-the other woman was not a bit like her.
JAMES SCROGGINS . I was at Mr. Partridge's on the 28th of Feb.—I am certain the prisoner is the woman who had the gin and offered the bad shil-ling on the 11th of March I was in the house, and heard of a bad shilling being taken—I went out and saw the prisoner, and recognised her as having been there on the 28th of Feb.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you live in the house? A. No—I know the first time was the 28th of Feb., as here was a funeral that day—the prisoner was in the house between six and eight minutes—I stood in front of the bat—the prisoner tendered the shilling which was refused, and the old woman paid the 2 d.—I saw her for six or seven minutes—there were two or three men there.
SARAH NAILOR . On the 11th of March I was serving at my aunt's, Mrs. Partridge's, and saw the prisoner come there just before gas-light, for two-penny worth of gin—I served her—she put a bad shilling on the counter—I bit and bent it—my aunt said, "This is the same person who was here be-fore"—I went to my uncle, and he followed her.
Cross-examined. Q. Did not your aunt take charge of the shilling? A. No; I gave it to my uncle, not to my aunt at all—I showed it to her—I am sure she did not have it and give it to my uncle.
SAMUEL PARTRIDGE . On the 11th of March I received a shilling from my wife, and gave it to inspector Cowling—I received one the same day from my niece, and gave that to my wife—she put it into a glass behind the bar—I took it to the station—there was no other shilling in the glass—I followed the prisoner out about 100 yards, and gave her in charge.
Cross-examined. Q. When did you receive the shilling from your niece? A. I suppose about six o'clock—I gave that shilling to my wife, and she returned it to me—I was at home on the 28th of Feb.—I got the shilling uttered on the 28th of Feb. first on the 11th of March—I took it from the glass behind the door—my wife did not keep it in her pocket till the 11th of March.
MR. CHAMBERS. Q. When did you first see the shilling uttered on the 28th of Feb.? A. On the 28th—my wife bit it and put it into the glass, and there, to the best of my belief, it remained—I believe it was still there on the 11th, when the prisoner came, but I cannot say—I put the one my niece gave me, into my pocket, and followed the prisoner—I took the other, and aimed that in my band.
JOHN RICHARD COWLING . I received a shilling on the 11th of March from Mr. Partridge, which I produce.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you kept it ever since? A. Yes—I made a mark on it.
JOHN FROST . I received a shilling from Mr. Partridge on the 11th of March, and took the prisoner in charge.
MR. JOHN FIELD . These two shillings are counterfeit.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Recorder.

1337. JOSEPH WHITE SIMMONS was indicted for feloniously utter-ing a forged bill of exchange for 200 l., with intent to defraud Elisha Underwood; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution,
ELISHA UNDERWOOD . I formerly kept a beer-shop, but am now out. of business. I have known the prisoner about two years—I served him at his lodgings with beer—I now live in Chorley-place, Kent-road-the prisoner
came to my place in Jan. last, and said he had a bill for 200 l. on a very respectable man drawn by Shoppee, a builder, and the acceptor was a very respectable man, a wine and spirit merchant, named George Heron—he had known him some years—he asked me to take half of the bill, to discount half of it—I told him I never had any thing to do with bills, and he proposed taking me round to see two referees, one at Bankside, and one in Mark-lane, as to the respectability of Heron—I went round with him, and found Mr. Heron was a highly respectable man—I then proposed writing to Mr. Heron to know if it was his acceptance or not—he proposed to do so, and two days after produced this letter to me—I declined advancing any money till I went to Booth's, the distillers—the prisoner gave me this letter, and I went to Messrs. Booth, who confirmed Mr. Heron's being a respectable man—I returned to my house in Chorley-place, and then advanced him first 81 l., and then 12 l.—I was to have 100 l. when the bill became due, on the 19th of April—the prisoner wrote his name on the back of the bill—I went down to Uxbridge on the 29th of March, and saw Mr. G. H. Heron, produced the bill and the letter, and at his request deposited the bill with Mr. Wood bridge, an attorney, of Uxbridge—the prisoner wrote a memorandum for me at the time I advanced the money, which was for him to receive half the money—I have not got that memorandum—I gave it to him—he wrote it, and I signed it—I did not know Heron till I went to Uxbridge.
Prisoner. Q. Have not I paid you a portion of that 12 l.? A. You paid me 5 l. 10 s. back.
JOSEPH SHOPPEE . I am a builder, and live at Uxbridge. I know Mr. Heron, a wine and coal merchant, perfectly well—he is a very respectable man—this bill purports to be drawn by me—I never drew it, or authorised the prisoner or any body to put my name to it—I never saw it till it was produced before the Magistrate—( bill read.)
GEORGE WILLIAM HERON , wine and coal-merchant, Uxbridge. The prisoner was in my employ in 1841—the acceptance to this bill is not my writing—I never authorised the prisoner or any one to accept it for me, or knew of its existence—this letter was not written by me, or by my authority.
MR. WOODBRIDGE. I am clerk to the Justices. This bill and letter were deposited with me at Mr. Heron's request—I have had them ever since.
CHARLES OTWAY ( police-inspector.) I apprehended the prisoner on the 31st of March at Hercules-buildings, Lambeth.
( Letter read.) "26th Jan. Sir,—I have to acknowledge the receipt of yours of yesterday, inclosing a bill of exchange for 200 l., and requesting to know whether it is my acceptance, and will be duly honoured when presented. I return the bill, and beg to say it is my signature; I will take care it shall be provided for when due; I have been in business twenty years, and my father thirty years, and challenge any one to say either of our acceptances were ever returned. Messrs. Booth are my distillers, and for your satisfaction I refer you to them for my respectability; I trust you will then be satisfied, for when in town I was given to understand inquiries had already been made of two persons with whom I do business; I do not like so much inquiry about matters so trivial, and if I had not given Mr. Shoppee the bill, I would have made a different arrangement, paid cash, and taken a discount. G. H. HERON."
CHARLES OTWAY ( police-inspector.) I produce a certificate of the conviction of Joseph White Simmons, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—I was present at the trial—the prisoner is the roan—( read.)
MR. HERON re-examined. The prisoner was at that time in my service, and prosecuted by me.
GUILTY . Aged 30.— Transported for Fifteen Years.
Before Mr. Justice Williams.
[trialAccount t18440408-1341]
1338. ANN CARROLL was indicted for the wilful murder of Elizabeth Hobbs; and on the Coroner's inquisition with manslaughter.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM HOBBS . I am a boiler-maker, and life in Holland-street, Borough. At the time this happened I lived at 2, Bird Cage-alley, where Carroll and his wife lived—there were several lodgers in the house—I and my wife occupied the front-room second floor—I had been there four or five weeks when this happened—Carroll was my landlord—on the 2nd of March I left my wife in the Black friars-road as I went to work—we had been at a public-house, and had half-a-quarters of rum together—she did not drink quite a quarter of it—I left her at five minutes before six in the morning—she was in good health—I never knew her in better health—when I came home at night I found her dead—her name was Elizabeth Hobbs.
SARAH BRAY . I lived at this time at No. 1, Bird Cage-alley, next door to this house. On the morning of the 2nd of March I heard cries of murder proceeding from the up-stairs window of Carroll's house—I heard people quarrelling very much—I went to the street-door, and demanded for the door to be opened—I did not see the prisoner at first, nor her husband—when I first saw the prisoner I said, "Mrs. Carroll, what have you got about? or what do you make this noise about? why don't you send for a policeman? if you cannot agree with this woman, have her taken up, don't ill use her like this"—Mrs. Carroll held two pair of boots in her hand, and said to the deceased, "When you pay me for my broken things I will give you your boots back, and not before "—the blood was then running down the left side of the deceased's neck from the back of the neck, and I saw a mark of a blow on her temple—it seemed a very heavy blow—the deceased had a fork in her hand—the prisoner's husband was standing by her side—the deceased beat the fork on the stairs, and called Mrs. Carroll very bad words indeed several times, and she said, "I wish it was one o'clock, for my husband to come home, I would have my revenge for beating me like this'—she addressed herself to Mr. and Mrs. Carroll—I did not see anything in Mr. Carroll's band—he might have had something, and I not see it—I went into my own room—I then heard the deceased woman repealing these bad words again, saying, "Don't strike me with a poker, I am not a man, I am a woman"—she addressed herself to Mr. Carroll—she said, "Mr. Carroll, don't strike me with a poker."
COURT. Q. Was the deceased in liquor? A. It is my opinion she had a little liquor; but I don't think she had more than to know what she was about—she stood correct on her feet—I saw nothing in the prisoner's hands, except the boots.
HARRIET MOSEA . I lived at this time in Carroll's house—the deceased came into my room—she was quite sober then—it was about seven o'clock in the morning when I saw her first—I saw her again about half-past seven—she seemed the worse for drink then—I heard her go into her own room, which was over mine—soon after, I heard two people go up stairs—I was in bed—they went into the deceased's room—I could hear the voices of Mr. and Mrs. Carroll in her room—I did not hear what was said; but I swear it was Mr. and Mrs. Carroll—I afterwards heard a smashing of crockery—a few minutes after that, Mr. and Mrs. Carroll came into my room, followed by the deceased—Mr. Carroll brought a bed into my room, and said he would not have such work in his house, he would get rid of the lodgers if they would not be more sociable, he would not have drunken people in his place—the deceased said, she would have revenge—they afterwards went down stairs—she followed Mr. and Mrs. Carroll down—I saw the deceased again in about
five minutes—she came back again—I did not hear any cries of murder—when she came hack she seemed very much fatigued, and her neck was bleeding—she was sick—the prisoner sent for a doctor—I have not seen Mr. Carroll since—the doctor came, and said she was quite dead—I do not suppose that was more than half-an-hour after she came into my room—I observed a wound on her temple—I saw a fork in the deceased's hand when she came into my room, and when she was sitting on the bed—she did not do anything with it; but knocked it against the door, and the step, saying, she would have revenge—the prisoner came back to the house after she had been for the doctor—her husband went away immediately; I have not seen him since.
Cross-examined by MR. WILDE. Q. Mrs. Hobbs was tipsy, was she not? A. She appeared so—I did not know her before.
MARY BURNS . I live with my mother in Birdcage-alley, in Carroll's house—on this morning I was in bed when the deceased came home—I saw her about half past nine o'clock—she had been drinking—I saw the prisoner—they had words together—Mrs. Carroll was coming down stairs, and the deceased coming up—Mrs. Carroll said to her, "Betsey, instead of your making Mrs. Burns tipsy, you had better give her 6 d. to get some breakfast"—my mother was very tipsy that morning and had been out with the deceased—Mrs. Hobbs said it was her own money, she had a right to do what she liked with it—Mrs. Carroll followed her up stairs to her own room—I heard a great deal of quarrelling between them, and crockery smashed—Mr. Carroll ran up stairs—I called out to know what it was all about—I heard him go into the room, and after that I heard a great deal of scuffling—I heard Mrs. Hobbs cry out, if her husband was at home, they would not ill-use her—I went up stairs once, and saw Mrs. Carroll standing on the landing, just by the room-door, with a cap in her hand, which was torn, and her hair hanging down her back, as if she had been in a scuffle—Mr. Carroll took the bed off deceased's bedstead, and brought it down stairs—he said, she should go out of the place—I did not see him put a hand on her—she called Mr. Carroll some very bad name, and threw something at him—at that time the deceased was bleeding behind her neck—I did not hear her say anything to Mr. Carroll about it—after Mr. Carroll went down stairs, the deceased followed him down to his own room door, and they were wrangling together there—I afterwards saw the deceased very sick, and she said, both Mr. and Mrs. Carroll had been beating her—that was the last word she spoke.
Cross-examined. Q. You never saw the prisoner strike her? A. No—I heard Mr. Carroll cry out, "Unloose me; leave go of me."
WILLIAM ALLEN . I lodged at this house—I first heard the noise a little after nine o'clock—I heard the deceased cry out "Murder"—my room is above hers—I came down stairs, and saw Mrs. Hobbs—she called Mr. Carroll very bad names several times—I interfered to take Mr. Carroll away, and advised him to go away—he said several times, "She has tried to job me with the fork several times," and said, "If I had the poker I would knock her head off." I went down stairs, and Mr. Carroll followed me—in about half an hour the deceased came down stairs—I heard a quarrel about the boots and crockery between the deceased and Mr. and Mrs. Carroll—Mr. Carroll told her if she would replace the things she had broken he would give her the boots—they were then on a box in the room—a few minutes after, he complained that she had run the fork into his lip—I did not see any sign of that—the deceased had a fork in her hand, and was trying to job it at him—I saw her do that upon the landing, but did not see it strike him—there was a piece of broken long below stairs in his room where we were at this time—Carroll
picked up the tongs and ran into the passage with it—the deceased was in the passage—I did not see the deceased again until she was dead—I saw her come on the landing, she appeared to have a severe blow on the temple—that was before Carroll went out with the broken tong—shortly before she died I heard Mrs. Carroll say to Mr. Carroll, "If it had not been for you there would have been no row at all, I could have settled it all myself without you"—Mr. Carroll then went away.
Cross-examined. Q. Are you aware whether Mrs. Hobbs had been out the whole night? A. I am not—she was tipsy when I saw her.
COURT. Q. Did you see any weapon in the prisoner's hands that morning? A. No—I saw the wound on the temple when the deceased was on the landing before the man took up the broken tongs—I do not know how she got the wound—I heard a great noise up stairs, as if she was falling about—it might have been from her tumbling down or something—the landing is up two pair of stairs—it was on the ground floor that Carroll went into the passage.
SARAH SIMMERS . I lived at No. 1, Birdcage-alley, next door to Carroll's. I heard cries of murder this morning, about half-past ten or near eleven o'clock—I looked through a hole in the wainscoting of the landing-place, and saw Mr. and Mrs. Carroll and the deceased in he front room, second floor, quarrelling—I heard the deceased call Mrs. Carroll very ugly names, and on her doing that, Mrs. Carroll struck her with her hand in the face—she did not fall down from the blow—Mrs. Hobbs called the name again, and then Mr. Carroll struck her with his fist several times—they all left the room, and went out of my sight—I heard the deceased talking to Mr. Carroll—she had a German-silver fork in her hand, and was beating it up against the wall, and asking him if he wanted to make property of it—I heard her say to him, that she was no man, she was a woman, and not to hit her with a poker—I did not hear her say "You b——, don't hit me with the poker"—the next thing I heard was, that the deceased was dead—when Carroll went down, and I saw him, he had nothing in his hand.
Cross-examined. Q. How large is the crevice, have you to stoop down to look? A. No—I never spoke to Mrs. Hobbs in my life.
COURT. Q. When the prisoner struck her, was it with the fist or open hand? A. I cannot say—the deceased did nothing on being struck—she had nothing in her hand that I saw, nor had the prisoner.
WILLIAM RENDLE . I am the parish surgeon. About ten o'clock on Saturday morning, the 2nd of March, I was called in to see the deceased—both Mr. and Mrs. Carroll came to fetch me—they waited for me, and accompanied me back—I examined the deceased, and observed she was bruised about the face—I did not observe that she had drank very much—I should think not—she was dead when I got there—I could not account for her death then—there were external bruises, but not sufficient—I opened her head on the Monday—externally there were marks of bruises over the left eye, and also a contused wound behind the right ear—on removing the scalp, there were large clots of blood effused, corresponding with the external marks—there was also evidence of blows being struck immediately behind the right ear—I should say that was not done with great violence—I saw the piece of tongs at the inquest—it may have been produced by that—a fist would not produce the wound behind the ear, it must have been something sharp—there was no wound on the temple, but three or four external bruises—in my judgment death was caused by concussion of the brain, produced by repeated blows—I should not think the blow behind the ear sufficient of itself, but, in combination with other circumstances, it would aid—it was the only wound—the other marks could be accounted for by blows from a fist or hand—if she
was excited it would increase the danger—she was in perfect health in every respect.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you on any previous occasion examined the head? A. Constantly—to a certain extent it is uncertain, from the appearance of the head, whether a person has died from apoplexy or external injury, but finding the woman in perfect health in other particulars, I do not consider it could be produced but from injury—she was not of a full habit of body—I considered her a well-formed woman, and in good condition—I examined the liver and all other parts—there was no disease of any kind—I did not smell any spirits at her mouth—I should imagine if a person had taken much spirits within an hour of death, I should find it by smelling the mouth—if a person died from drinking spirits, I consider I could perceive it all over the viscera—there were many marks of apoplexy about the head—I think I could take on myself to say she did not die from apoplexy—there was considerable congestion of the brain, but there was not apoplexy from the escape of blood—congestion of the brain would not produce death, considered alone, at that age and state of health—if she were in a great passion and under the influence of liquor, I should think it just possible—the bone was not injured, but death would be caused by concussion of the brain by a violent shaking of the brain, without anything else—I should say repeated blows would produce concussion of the brain—I was led to think she might have struck against a sharp edge of a partition—if a pail had been on the stairs, I think the injury would have been more extensive—the appearance of the brain led me to believe the whole I saw was from excitement.
MR. DOANE. Q. You found considerable extravasation of blood corresponding with the external marks? A. Yes, and from that I infer there was repeated blows, which induces me to say that concussion was the immediate cause of death, and having heard she was sick, I infer it was a case of concussion; that is a usual symptom—in my judgment the blows would produce the effects I witnessed, and those effects would produce concussion of the brain—I consider she died from that—the congestion of the brain must have assisted; but that I consider was produced by excitement of the mind.
COURT. Q. The clotted blood had nothing to do with the death? A. No. that only indicated that there had been external violence—I should judge from the appearances, she must have received blows of great importance—I dare not say she died entirely from external violence—it was the chief cause—sickness does occur in apoplexy; but it is more especially a corroborative symptom of concussion—I consider that a person of her age and health would scarcely die of apoplexy—I consider external violence the chief cause of death—congestion of the brain taking place at the same time, would accelerate the death, chiefly caused by concussion—if no blow had been struck, and I had found none of the symptoms consequent upon a blow, I should not have seen cause enough to produce death.
ROBERT STURGEON ( police-constable M 4.) I went to the house where the deceased lived, at half-past twelve o'clock, and found the dead body there—I accompanied the prisoner to the station with another constable—I received a piece of iron from the beadle of the parish, at the workhouse.
WILLIAM ALLEN re-examined. This is the piece of iron which Carroll picked up—I saw some blood running down the deceased's neck on the landing—that was before I saw Carroll with the poker—that was before she came down stairs.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
